, as seen in the proof of Theorem
7
(see Step 1).
This completes the proof since
e_{u}(0)=0
when
\beta=*
, while
e_{u}(0)\in\mathbb{H}
when
\beta\in(0,\alpha/2)
.
∎

Finally, we deal with the scaling property of our processes.

Proof of Proposition
11
.

We divide the proof in two parts.

Step 1.
Let us first assume that
R_{0}=x\in\partial\mathcal{D}
. Consider the Poisson measure

\Pi_{\beta}
and the processes
(a_{u},b_{u},\tau_{u})_{u\geq 0}
from Definition
4
. We set
\gamma=1/2

when
\beta=*
and
\gamma=\beta/\alpha
when
\beta\in(0,\alpha/2)
. For
\lambda>0
, we introduce
the map
\Phi_{\lambda}:\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{E}
defined by
\Phi_{\lambda}(e)(t)=\lambda^{1/\alpha}e(t/\lambda)
.
We set
\mathcal{D}^{\lambda}=\lambda^{1/\alpha}\mathcal{D}
and observe that for
y\in\partial\mathcal{D}
, the normal vector
at
\lambda^{1/\alpha}y\in\partial\mathcal{D}^{\lambda}
is
\mathbf{n}_{y}
, so that, with obvious notations,

\mathcal{I}_{y}=\mathcal{I}^{\lambda}_{\lambda^{1/\alpha}y}
.
For any
y\in\partial\mathcal{D}^{\lambda}
, any
A\in\mathcal{I}_{y}^{\lambda}
and any
e\in\mathcal{E}
,
we will denote by
\bar{\ell}_{y}^{\lambda}(A,e)
and
g_{y}^{\lambda}(A,e)
the corresponding quantities associated
with the domain
\mathcal{D}^{\lambda}
. One can check from (
12
) and the definition of
\bar{\ell}
that
for any
y\in\partial\mathcal{D}
, any
A\in\mathcal{I}_{y}
and any
e\in\mathcal{E}
,

\lambda\bar{\ell}_{y}(A,e)=\bar{\ell}_{\lambda^{1/\alpha}y}^{\lambda}(A,\Phi_{\lambda}(e))\quad\text{and}\quad\lambda^{1/\alpha}g_{y}(A,e)=g_{\lambda^{1/\alpha}y}^{\lambda}(A,\Phi_{\lambda}(e)).

(62)

We now introduce the Poisson measure
\Pi_{\beta}^{\lambda}
defined by

\Pi_{\beta}^{\lambda}=\sum_{s\in\mathrm{J}_{\lambda}}\delta_{(s,e_{s}^{\lambda})}\quad\text{where}\quad\mathrm{J}_{\lambda}=\{s\geq 0,\>s/\lambda^{\gamma}\in\mathrm{J}\}\quad\text{and}\quad e_{s}^{\lambda}=\Phi_{\lambda}(e_{s/\lambda^{\gamma}}).

Using the scaling property of
\mathfrak{n}_{\beta}
, see Lemma
26
, one can verify that
\Pi_{\beta}^{\lambda}

is a Poisson measure distributed
as
\Pi_{\beta}
.
Recall now that
(b_{u})_{u\geq 0}
is a solution to (
14
), so that, setting

(b_{u}^{\lambda})_{u\geq 0}=(\lambda^{1/\alpha}b_{u/\lambda^{\gamma}})_{u\geq 0}
and

(a_{u}^{\lambda})_{u\geq 0}=(a_{u/\lambda^{\gamma}})_{u\geq 0}
, we have for any
u\geq 0
,

\displaystyle b_{u}^{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\lambda^{1/\alpha}x+\lambda^{1/\alpha}\int_{0}^{u/\lambda^{\gamma}}\int_{\mathcal{E}}\Big(g_{b_{v-}}(a_{v},e)-b_{v-}\Big)\Pi_{\beta}(\mathrm{d}v,\mathrm{d}e)

\displaystyle=\lambda^{1/\alpha}x+\int_{0}^{u}\int_{\mathcal{E}}\Big(g_{b_{v-}^{\lambda}}^{\lambda}(a_{v}^{\lambda},e)-b_{v-}^{\lambda}\Big)\Pi_{\beta}^{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}v,\mathrm{d}e)

where in the second equality we used (
62
) and the definition of
\Pi_{\beta}^{\lambda}
.
Thus
(b_{u}^{\lambda})_{u\geq 0}
is a solution to (
14
) valued in
\partial\mathcal{D}^{\lambda}
and
started at
\lambda^{1/\alpha}x
. Similarly,

(\tau_{u}^{\lambda})_{u\geq 0}=(\lambda\tau_{u/\lambda^{\gamma}})_{u\geq 0}
satisfies

\tau_{u}^{\lambda}=\int_{0}^{u}\int_{\mathcal{E}}\bar{\ell}_{b_{v-}^{\lambda}}^{\lambda}(a_{v}^{\lambda},e)\Pi_{\beta}^{\lambda}(\mathrm{d}v,\mathrm{d}e).

Then, if
(L_{t})_{t\geq 0}
denotes the right-continuous inverse of
(\tau_{u})_{u\geq 0}
, one can see
that
(L_{t}^{\lambda})_{t\geq 0}:=(\lambda^{\gamma}L_{t/\lambda})_{t\geq 0}
is the right-continuous inverse
of
(\tau_{u}^{\lambda})_{u\geq 0}
. Finally, by (
57
) (which follows from
Remark
6
), we have

\displaystyle\lambda^{1/\alpha}R_{t/\lambda}=

\displaystyle\lambda^{1/\alpha}h_{b_{L_{t/\lambda}-}}(a_{L_{t/\lambda}},e_{L_{t/\lambda}}(t/\lambda-\tau_{L_{t/\lambda}-})){\bm{1}}_{\{\tau_{L_{t/\lambda}}>t/\lambda\}}+\lambda^{1/\alpha}b_{L_{t/\lambda}}{\bm{1}}_{\{\tau_{L_{t/\lambda}}=t/\lambda\}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h_{b_{L_{t}^{\lambda}-}^{\lambda}}(a_{L_{t}^{\lambda}}^{\lambda},e_{L_{t}^{\lambda}}^{\lambda}(t-\tau_{L_{t}^{\lambda}-}^{\lambda})){\bm{1}}_{\{\tau_{L_{t}^{\lambda}}^{\lambda}>t\}}+b_{L_{t}^{\lambda}}^{\lambda}{\bm{1}}_{\{\tau_{L_{t}^{\lambda}}^{\lambda}=t\}}.

As a consequence, the process
(\lambda^{1/\alpha}R_{t/\lambda})_{t\geq 0}
an
(\alpha,\beta)
-stable
process reflected in
\lambda^{1/\alpha}{}\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0mu\mathcal{D}}=\{\lambda^{1/\alpha}y:y\in{}\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0mu\mathcal{D}}\}
issued
from
\lambda^{1/\alpha}x\in\partial\mathcal{D}^{\lambda}
.

Step 2.
Consider now an
(\alpha,\beta)
-stable process
(R_{t})_{t\geq 0}
reflected in
{}\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0mu\mathcal{D}}

issued from
x\in\mathcal{D}
, built as in Definition
8
: for some ISP
α,x

(Z_{t})_{t\geq 0}
,
for
\tilde{\ell}(Z)=\inf\{t>0:Z_{t}\notin\mathcal{D}\}
, set
R_{t}=Z_{t}
for
t\in[0,\tilde{\ell}(Z))
, set

Y=\Lambda(Z_{\tilde{\ell}(Z)-},Z_{\tilde{\ell}(Z)})
, pick some
\mathbb{Q}_{Y}
-distributed process
(S_{t})_{t\geq 0}
,
and set
R_{t}=S_{t-\tilde{\ell}(Z)}
for
t\geq\tilde{\ell}(Z)
.

We introduce the function
\Lambda_{\lambda}
as in (
10
) associated with
\mathcal{D}^{\lambda}
and,
for
z\in\partial\mathcal{D}^{\lambda}
, we call
\mathbb{Q}^{\lambda}_{z}
the law of the
(\alpha,\beta)
-stable process
reflected in
{}\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0mu\mathcal{D}}^{\lambda}
.

We introduce

(Z^{\lambda}_{t}=\lambda^{1/\alpha}Z_{t/\lambda})_{t\geq 0}
, which is an ISP
{}_{\alpha,\lambda^{1/\alpha}x}
,
and observe that we have
\tilde{\ell}_{\lambda}(Z^{\lambda})=\inf\{t>0:Z_{t}^{\lambda}\notin\mathcal{D}^{\lambda}\}=\lambda\tilde{\ell}(Z)
,
that
\lambda^{1/\alpha}Y=\Lambda_{\lambda}(Z^{\lambda}_{\tilde{\ell}_{\lambda}(Z^{\lambda})-},Z^{\lambda}_{\tilde{\ell}_{\lambda}(Z^{\lambda})})
, that

(S^{\lambda}_{t}=\lambda^{1/\alpha}S_{t/\lambda})_{t\geq 0}
is
\mathbb{Q}^{\lambda}_{\lambda^{1/\alpha}Y}
-distributed by Step 1
and that
\lambda^{1/\alpha}S_{(t-\sigma)/\lambda}=S^{\lambda}_{t-\sigma_{\lambda}}
.
All this shows that
(R^{\lambda}_{t}=\lambda^{1/\alpha}R_{t/\lambda})_{t\geq 0}

is indeed an
(\alpha,\beta)
-stable process reflected in
{}\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0mu\mathcal{D}}^{\lambda}
and
issued from
\lambda^{1/\alpha}x\in\mathcal{D}
.
∎

7
Infinitesimal generator and P.D.E.s

The goal of this section is to prove Theorem
15
and Proposition
16
.
We recall that
\mathbb{Q}_{x}
was introduced in Definitions
4
and
8

and that
(X^{*}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is the canonical process
on
\Omega^{*}=\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},{}\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0mu\mathcal{D}})
. Recall also that the sets
D_{\alpha}
and
H_{\beta}
were introduced in
Definitions
12
and
14
.
The main difficulty of the section is to establish the following result.

Proposition 47
.

Fix
\beta\in\{*\}\cup(0,\alpha/2)
and suppose Assumption
1
.
Let
\varphi\in D_{\alpha}\cap H_{\beta}
.
For all
x\in{}\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0mu\mathcal{D}}
, all
t\geq 0
, we have

\mathbb{Q}_{x}[\varphi(X^{*}_{t})]=\varphi(x)+\mathbb{Q}_{x}\Big[\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{L}\varphi(X^{*}_{s})\mathrm{d}s\Big].

(63)

By Theorem
9
,
\mathbb{Q}_{x}[\int_{0}^{\infty}{\bm{1}}_{\{X_{s}^{*}\in\partial\mathcal{D}\}}\mathrm{d}s]=0
,
so that everything makes sense in the above identity,
even if
\mathcal{L}\varphi
is not defined on
\partial\mathcal{D}
. Let us admit Proposition
47

for a moment and handle the
proofs of the results announced in Subsection
2.3
.

Proof of Theorem
15
.

For any
\varphi\in D_{\alpha}\cap H_{\beta}
, Proposition
47

tells us that for all
x\in\mathcal{D}
, all
t\geq 0
,

\psi(t,x):=\frac{\mathbb{Q}_{x}[\varphi(X_{t}^{*})-\varphi(x)]}{t}=\mathbb{Q}_{x}\Big[\frac{1}{t}\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{L}\varphi(X_{s}^{*})\mathrm{d}s\Big].

Since
\mathcal{L}\varphi\in C(\mathcal{D})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathcal{D})
by definition of
D_{\alpha}
and since

X^{*}
is right continuous, we immediately conclude that
\psi(t,x)
converges bounded pointwise
on
\mathcal{D}
to
\mathcal{L}\varphi(x)

as
t\to 0
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
16
.

Recall that
f(t,x,\mathrm{d}y)=\mathbb{Q}_{x}(X_{t}^{*}\in\mathrm{d}y)
for all

x\in{}\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0mu\mathcal{D}}
, all
t\geq 0
. By Theorem
9
, we know that for all
x\in{}\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0mu\mathcal{D}}
,

\int_{0}^{\infty}f(t,x,\partial\mathcal{D})\mathrm{d}t=0
and that for all
t\geq 0
, the map
x\mapsto f(t,x,\mathrm{d}y)

is weakly continuous.
For
\varphi\in D_{\alpha}\cap H_{\beta}
, Proposition
47

tells us that for all
x\in{}\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0mu\mathcal{D}}
, all
t\geq 0
,

\mathbb{Q}_{x}[\varphi(X_{t}^{*})]=\varphi(x)+\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{Q}_{x}[\mathcal{L}\varphi(X_{s}^{*})]\mathrm{d}s
,
which precisely gives us (
18
).
∎

To prove Proposition
47
, we first study what happens until the process reaches the boundary.

Lemma 48
.

Fix
\beta\in\{*\}\cup(0,\alpha/2)
and grant Assumption
1
.
Let
\varphi\in D_{\alpha}
. For all
x\in{}\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0mu\mathcal{D}}
, all
t\geq 0
,
setting
d_{0}^{*}=\inf\{t>0:X_{t}^{*}\in\partial\mathcal{D}\}
,

\mathbb{Q}_{x}\big[\varphi(X^{*}_{t\land d_{0}^{*}})\big]=\varphi(x)+\mathbb{Q}_{x}\bigg[\int_{0}^{t\land d_{0}^{*}}\mathcal{L}\varphi(X^{*}_{s})\mathrm{d}s\bigg].

Proof.

The case
x\in\partial\mathcal{D}
is obvious, since then
\mathbb{Q}_{x}(d_{0}^{*}=0)=1
, see Step 2.1 of the proof of
Proposition
46
.
Fix
x\in\mathcal{D}
and recall Definition
8
:

\mathbb{Q}_{x}\big[\varphi(X^{*}_{t\land d_{0}^{*}})\big]=\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[\varphi(R_{t\land\tilde{\ell}(Z)})\big]\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{Q}_{x}\Big[\int_{0}^{t\land d_{0}^{*}}\!\!\mathcal{L}\varphi(X^{*}_{s})\mathrm{d}s\Big]=\mathbb{E}_{x}\Big[\int_{0}^{t\land\tilde{\ell}(Z)}\!\!\mathcal{L}\varphi(R_{s})\mathrm{d}s\Big],

where
\mathbb{P}_{x}
is the law of an ISP
α,x

(Z_{t})_{t\geq 0}
, where

\tilde{\ell}(Z)=\inf\{t>0:Z_{t}\notin\mathcal{D}\}
and where
R_{t}=Z_{t}
for
t\in[0,\tilde{\ell}(Z))

and
R_{\tilde{\ell}(Z)}=\Lambda(Z_{\tilde{\ell}(Z)-},Z_{\tilde{\ell}(Z)})
. Recall also that
Z
is defined by (
6
),
through a Poisson measure
N
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
with intensity measure

\mathrm{d}s|z|^{-\alpha-d}\mathrm{d}z
. We claim that for all
t\in[0,\tilde{\ell}(Z)]
,

R_{t}=x+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\{|z|\leq 1\}}\big[\bar{\Lambda}(R_{s-},z)-R_{s-}\big]\tilde{N}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}z)+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\{|z|>1\}}\big[\bar{\Lambda}(R_{s-},z)-R_{s-}\big]N(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}z),

where
\bar{\Lambda}(r,z)=\Lambda(r,r+z)
. Indeed, let
U_{t}
be the RHS of this expression.
We have
\bar{\Lambda}(R_{s-},z)=\bar{\Lambda}(Z_{s-},z)=Z_{s-}+z=R_{{s-}}+z
for
N
-a.e.

(s,z)\in[0,\tilde{\ell}(Z))\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
(recall that
\bar{\Lambda}(x,z)=x+z
if
x+z\in\mathcal{D}
).
Thus for
t\in[0,\tilde{\ell}(Z))
,

U_{t}=x+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\{|z|\leq 1\}}z\tilde{N}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}z)+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\{|z|>1\}}zN(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}z)=Z_{t}=R_{t}.

Next,

U_{\tilde{\ell}(Z)}=U_{\tilde{\ell}(Z)-}+\bar{\Lambda}(R_{\tilde{\ell}(Z)-},\Delta Z_{\tilde{\ell}(Z)})-R_{\tilde{\ell}(Z)-}=\bar{\Lambda}(Z_{\tilde{\ell}(Z)-},\Delta Z_{\tilde{\ell}(Z)})=\Lambda(Z_{\tilde{\ell}(Z)-},Z_{\tilde{\ell}(Z)})=R_{\tilde{\ell}(Z)}.

We now introduce
\tilde{\ell}_{\varepsilon}(Z)=\inf\{t\geq 0:d(Z_{t},\mathcal{D}^{c})\leq\varepsilon\}\leq\tilde{\ell}(Z)
. We have

\displaystyle R_{t\land\tilde{\ell}_{\varepsilon}(Z)}=

\displaystyle x+\int_{0}^{t\land\tilde{\ell}_{\varepsilon}(Z)}\int_{\{|z|\leq 1\}}\big[\bar{\Lambda}(R_{s-},z)-R_{s-}\big]\tilde{N}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}z)

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t\land\tilde{\ell}_{\varepsilon}(Z)}\int_{\{|z|>1\}}\big[\bar{\Lambda}(R_{s-},z)-R_{s-}\big]N(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}z).

We can now apply the Itô formula with
\varphi\in C^{2}(\mathcal{D})\cap C({}\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0mu\mathcal{D}})
and we get

\displaystyle\varphi(R_{t\land\tilde{\ell}_{\varepsilon}(Z)})=

\displaystyle\varphi(x)+\int_{0}^{t\land\tilde{\ell}_{\varepsilon}(Z)}\int_{\{|z|\leq 1\}}\big[\varphi(\bar{\Lambda}(R_{s-},z))-\varphi(R_{s-})\big]\tilde{N}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}z)

\displaystyle\hskip-25.0pt+\int_{0}^{t\land\tilde{\ell}_{\varepsilon}(Z)}\int_{\{|z|\leq 1\}}\big[\varphi(\bar{\Lambda}(R_{s-},z))-\varphi(R_{s-})-\nabla\varphi(R_{s-})\cdot(\bar{\Lambda}(R_{s-},z)-R_{s-})\big]\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{|z|^{d+\alpha}}\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\hskip-25.0pt+\int_{0}^{t\land\tilde{\ell}_{\varepsilon}(Z)}\int_{\{|z|>1\}}\big[\varphi(\bar{\Lambda}(R_{s-},z))-\varphi(R_{s-})\big]N(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varphi(x)+M_{t}^{\varepsilon}+\int_{0}^{t\land\tilde{\ell}_{\varepsilon}(Z)}\!\!\!\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\!\!\big[\varphi(\bar{\Lambda}(R_{s-},z))-\varphi(R_{s-})-\nabla\varphi(R_{s-})\cdot z{\bm{1}}_{\{|z|\leq 1\}}\big]\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{|z|^{d+\alpha}}\mathrm{d}s

where

M_{t}^{\varepsilon}=\int_{0}^{t\land\tilde{\ell}_{\varepsilon}(Z)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big[\varphi(\bar{\Lambda}(R_{s-},z))-\varphi(R_{s-})\big]\tilde{N}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}z).

Since
\varphi\in C({}\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0mu\mathcal{D}})\cap C^{2}(\mathcal{D})
and since
d(r,\mathcal{D}^{c})\geq\varepsilon
and
|z|<\varepsilon/2
imply that

\bar{\Lambda}(r,z)=r+z
,

\sup_{r:d(r,\mathcal{D}^{c})\geq\varepsilon}|\varphi(\bar{\Lambda}(r,z))-\varphi(r)|\leq 2||\varphi||_{\infty}{\bm{1}}_{\{|z|\geq\varepsilon/2\}}+\Big(\sup_{r:d(r,\mathcal{D}^{c})\geq\varepsilon/2}|\nabla\varphi(r)|\Big)|z|{\bm{1}}_{\{|z|<\varepsilon/2\}},

which belongs to
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},|z|^{-\alpha-d}\mathrm{d}z)
. Thus
(M_{t}^{\varepsilon})_{t\geq 0}
is a true martingale, and we
conclude, recalling Definition
12
, that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x}\big[\varphi(R_{t\land\tilde{\ell}_{\varepsilon}(Z)})\big]=

\displaystyle\varphi(x)+\mathbb{E}_{x}\Big[\int_{0}^{t\land\tilde{\ell}_{\varepsilon}(Z)}\mathcal{L}\varphi(R_{s})\mathrm{d}s\Big].

It then suffices to let
\varepsilon\to 0
, using that
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbb{P}_{x}(\tilde{\ell}_{\varepsilon}(Z)=\tilde{\ell}(Z))=1
by (
43
)
(since
x\in\mathcal{D}
), that
\varphi\in C({}\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0mu\mathcal{D}})
and that
\mathcal{L}\varphi
is bounded.
∎

We next show that our reflected process can be approximated by a similar process with a
finite
excursion
measure. This is a little fastidious, but it seems unavoidable
to prove Proposition
47
without heavy restrictions on the
test functions. Such restrictions would make the conclusion of
Remark
17
incorrect, which would be an important issue.

Definition 49
.

Grant Assumption
1
.
Let
m>0
and let
j
be a finite measure on
\mathbb{H}
left invariant by any isometry of
\mathbb{H}
sending

\mathbf{e}_{1}
to
\mathbf{e}_{1}
. Consider the finite measure
\mathfrak{n}_{j}
on
\mathcal{E}
defined by

\mathfrak{n}_{j}(B)=\int_{x\in\mathbb{H}}\mathbb{P}_{x}\big((Z_{t\land\ell(Z)})_{t\geq 0}\in B\big)j(\mathrm{d}x)\quad\text{for all Borel subset $B$ of $\mathcal{E}$.}

(64)

For
x\in\partial\mathcal{D}
, we say that
(R_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is an
(\alpha,m,j)
-stable process reflected in
{}\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0mu\mathcal{D}}
issued
from
x
if there exists a filtration
(\mathcal{G}_{u})_{u\geq 0}
, a
(\mathcal{G}_{u})_{u\geq 0}
-Poisson measure

\Pi_{j}=\sum_{u\in\mathrm{J}}\delta_{(u,e_{u})}
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{E}
with intensity measure
\mathrm{d}u\mathfrak{n}_{j}(\mathrm{d}e)
,
a càdlàg
(\mathcal{G}_{u})_{u\geq 0}
-adapted
\partial\mathcal{D}
-valued process
(b_{u})_{u\geq 0}
and a
(\mathcal{G}_{u})_{u\geq 0}
-predictable
process
(a_{u})_{u\geq 0}
such that a.s., for all
u\geq 0
,
a_{u}\in\mathcal{I}_{b_{u-}}
and

b_{u}=x+\int_{0}^{u}\int_{\mathcal{E}}\Big(g_{b_{v-}}(a_{v},e)-b_{v-}\Big)\Pi_{j}(\mathrm{d}v,\mathrm{d}e)

(65)

and such that, introducing the càdlàg increasing
\mathbb{R}_{+}
-valued
(\mathcal{G}_{u})_{u\geq 0}
-adapted process

\tau_{u}=mu+\int_{0}^{u}\int_{\mathcal{E}}\bar{\ell}_{b_{v-}}(a_{v},e)\Pi_{j}(\mathrm{d}v,\mathrm{d}e)

(66)

and its generalized inverse
L_{t}=\inf\{u\geq 0:\tau_{u}>t\}
,

R_{t}
is given by (
16
) for all
t\geq 0
.
We then call
\mathbb{Q}^{m,j}_{x}
the law of
(R_{t})_{t\geq 0}
.

Recall that for any
z\in\partial\mathcal{D}
, the measurable family
(A^{z}_{y})_{y\in\partial\mathcal{D}}
(or
(A_{y})_{y\in\partial\mathcal{D}}
if
d=2
)
such that
A_{y}^{z}\in\mathcal{I}_{y}
was introduced in Lemma
77
.
For any
z\in\partial\mathcal{D}
fixed,
the strong existence and uniqueness of an
(\alpha,m,j)
-stable process reflected in
{}\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0mu\mathcal{D}}

such that
a_{u}=A^{z}_{b_{u-}}

(or
a_{u}=A_{b_{u-}}
if
d=2
) is obvious, since the Poisson measure
\Pi_{j}
is finite.
The uniqueness in law (with any choice for
(a_{u})_{u\geq 0}
) of the
(\alpha,m,j)
-stable process
reflected in
{}\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0mu\mathcal{D}}
can be checked exactly as in Step 2 of the proof of Theorem
7
,
making use of a result similar to Lemma
43
for
\Pi_{j}
,
which is licit since
j
is invariant by any isometry of
\mathbb{H}
sending

\mathbf{e}_{1}
to
\mathbf{e}_{1}
.

We are now ready to specify the approximating processes. Recall that
r>0
and
\ell_{r}
were
defined in Remark
3
.
For
n\geq 1
, we introduce (recall the definition (
8
) of
\mathfrak{n}_{\beta}
)

\displaystyle j_{\beta}^{n}(\mathrm{d}x)=\mathfrak{n}_{\beta}(e(0)\in\mathrm{d}x,|e(0)|>1/n)={\bm{1}}_{\{|x|>1/n\}}\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{|x|^{1+\beta}}\quad\text{if $\beta\in(0,\alpha/2)$},

\displaystyle j_{*}^{n}(\mathrm{d}x)=\mathfrak{n}_{*}(e(1/n)\in\mathrm{d}x,\>\ell_{r}(e)>1/n).

These two measures on
\mathbb{H}
are finite and invariant by any isometry sending
\mathbf{e}_{1}
to
\mathbf{e}_{1}
.

Lemma 50
.

Let
\beta\in\{*\}\cup(0,\alpha/2)
and suppose Assumption
1
. For
n\geq 1
, set
m_{n}=1/n
.
For any
x\in\partial\mathcal{D}
, in the sense of finite-dimensional distributions,

\lim_{n}\mathbb{Q}_{x}^{m_{n},j_{\beta}^{n}}=\mathbb{Q}_{x}.

Proof.

As in the proof of Theorem
7
, we only treat the case
d\geq 3
, the case
d=2
being easier,
as we can use Lemma
77
-(a) instead of Lemma
77
-(b).
We fix
\beta\in\{*\}\cup(0,\alpha/2)
and

x\in\partial\mathcal{D}
and consider
z\in\partial\mathcal{D}
such that Proposition
45
-(b) applies.

Consider, on some probability space with a filtration
(\mathcal{G}_{u})_{u\geq 0}
,
a
(\mathcal{G}_{u})_{u\geq 0}
-Poisson measure
\Pi_{\beta}=\sum_{u\in\mathrm{J}}\delta_{(u,e_{u})}

on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{E}
with intensity
\mathrm{d}u\mathfrak{n}_{\beta}(\mathrm{d}e)
, as well as the boundary process

(b_{u})_{u\geq 0}
built in Proposition
45
-(b). We set
(a_{u})_{u\geq 0}=(A^{z}_{b_{u-}})_{u\geq 0}
,
define

(\tau_{u})_{u\geq 0}
by (
15
), introduce
(L_{t}=\inf\{u\geq 0:\tau_{u}>t\})_{t\geq 0}
and define

(R_{t})_{t\geq 0}
by (
16
). Then, as in Step 1 of the proof of Theorem
7
,

(R_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is
\mathbb{Q}_{x}
-distributed.

If
\beta\in(0,\alpha/2)
,
we set
\Pi_{\beta}^{n}=\sum_{u\in\mathrm{J}}\delta_{(u,e_{u})}{\bm{1}}_{\{|e_{u}(0)|>1/n\}}
and we see that
\Pi_{\beta}^{n}
is a
Poisson measure with intensity
\mathrm{d}u\mathfrak{n}_{j_{\beta}^{n}}(\mathrm{d}e)
.

If
\beta=*
, then for any
u\in\mathrm{J}
such that

\ell_{r}(e_{u})>1/n
, we define
e_{u}^{n}=(e_{u}(1/n+s))_{s\geq 0}=:\theta_{1/n}(e_{u})
and we set

\Pi_{\beta}^{n}=\sum_{u\in\mathrm{J}}\delta_{(u,e_{u}^{n})}{\bm{1}}_{\{\ell_{r}(e_{u})>1/n\}}
. Let us observe at once that

\text{for all $b\in\partial\mathcal{D}$, all $A\in\mathcal{I}_{b}$,}\quad\bar{\ell}_{b}(A,e^{n}_{u})=\bar{\ell}_{b}(A,e_{u})-\frac{1}{n}\quad\text{and}\quad g_{b}(A,e^{n}_{u})=g_{b}(A,e_{u}),

(67)

because
\bar{\ell}_{b}(a,e)\geq\ell_{r}(e)
, see Remark
3
.
For any measurable

F:\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{E}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathcal{E}}F(u,e)\Pi_{\beta}^{n}(\mathrm{d}u,\mathrm{d}e)\bigg]=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathcal{E}}F(u,\theta_{1/n}(e)){\bm{1}}_{\{\ell_{r}(e)>1/n\}}\mathfrak{n}_{*}(\mathrm{d}e)\mathrm{d}u

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{E}_{e(1/n)}[F(u,(Z_{t\land\tilde{\ell}(Z)})_{t\geq 0})]{\bm{1}}_{\{\ell_{r}(e)>1/n\}}\mathfrak{n}_{*}(\mathrm{d}e)\mathrm{d}u,

where we used the Markov property at time
1/n
of the measure
\mathfrak{n}_{*}
, see Lemma
29
.
This is possible since
\ell(e)\geq\ell_{r}(e)
. Recalling (
64
) and the definition of
j^{n}_{*}
,
this gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathcal{E}}F(u,e)\Pi_{\beta}^{n}(\mathrm{d}u,\mathrm{d}e)\bigg]=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathcal{E}}F(u,e)\mathfrak{n}_{j_{*}^{n}}(\mathrm{d}e)\mathrm{d}u,

which shows that
\Pi_{\beta}^{n}
has intensity
\mathrm{d}u\mathfrak{n}_{j_{*}^{n}}(\mathrm{d}e)
.

We then introduce, for each
n\geq 1
, the
(\alpha,m_{n},j^{n}_{\beta})
-process
(R^{n}_{t})_{t\geq 0}

built as in Definition
49
with the Poisson measure
\Pi_{\beta}^{n}
(and with the choice

a^{n}_{u}=A^{z}_{b^{n}_{u-}}
,
with the value of
z
introduced above to build
(R_{t})_{t\geq 0}
),
and denote by
(b^{n}_{u})_{u\geq 0}
,
(a^{n}_{u})_{u\geq 0}
,
(\tau^{n}_{u})_{u\geq 0}
,

(L^{n}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
the processes involved in its construction.

We now show, following line by line Steps 3.2-3.7 of the proof of Theorem
7

(with
t_{n}=t
and
x_{n}=x
), that
for each
t\geq 0
,
R^{n}_{t}
converges in probability to
R_{t}
. This will complete
the proof.

Step 3.2.
For any
T>0
,
\sup_{[0,T)}(|b^{n}_{u}-b_{u}|+|a^{n}_{u}-a_{u}|)\to 0
in probability.

We introduce
\rho^{k}=\inf\{u\geq 0:|b_{u}-z|\leq 1/k\}
and
\rho^{k}_{n}=\inf\{u\geq 0:|b_{u}^{n}-z|\leq 1/k\}

and show that
\lim_{n}\mathbb{E}[\sup_{[0,T\land\rho^{k}\land\rho^{k}_{n})}|b^{n}_{u}-b_{u}|]=0

for each
k\geq 1
, the rest of the proof is similar.

If
\beta\in(0,\alpha/2)
, we have

\displaystyle b_{u}^{n}=x+\int_{0}^{u}\int_{\mathcal{E}}[g_{b_{v-}^{n}}(A^{z}_{b_{v-}^{n}},e)-b_{v-}^{n}]{\bm{1}}_{\{|e(0)|>1/n\}}\Pi_{\beta}(\mathrm{d}v,\mathrm{d}e),

\displaystyle b_{u}=x+\int_{0}^{u}\int_{\mathcal{E}}[g_{b_{v-}}(A^{z}_{b_{v-}},e)-b_{v-}]\Pi_{\beta}(\mathrm{d}v,\mathrm{d}e).

Using (
47
) and that
|g_{b}(A,e)-b|\leq M(e)\land D
(where
D=
diam
(\mathcal{D})
)
for all
b\in\partial\mathcal{D}
, all
A\in\mathcal{I}_{b}
, all
e\in\mathcal{E}
,
we get, for all
u\in[0,T]
,

\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{[0,u\land\rho^{k}\land\rho^{k}_{n})}|b^{n}_{u}-b_{u}|\Big]\leq C_{k}\int_{0}^{u}\mathbb{E}\Big[|b^{n}_{v\land\rho^{k}\land\rho^{k}_{n}}-b_{v\land\rho^{k}\land\rho^{k}_{n}}|\Big]\mathrm{d}v+T\int_{\mathcal{E}}(M(e)\land D){\bm{1}}_{\{|e(0)|\leq 1/n\}}\mathfrak{n}_{\beta}(\mathrm{d}e).

Thanks to the Gronwall lemma, we conclude that

\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{[0,T\land\rho^{k}\land\rho^{k}_{n})}|b^{n}_{u}-b_{u}|\Big]\leq Te^{C_{k}T}\int_{\mathcal{E}}(M(e)\land D){\bm{1}}_{\{|e(0)|\leq 1/n\}}\mathfrak{n}_{\beta}(\mathrm{d}e),

which tends to
0
as
n\to\infty
by (
9
) and since
|e(0)|>0
for
\mathfrak{n}_{\beta}
-a.e.
e\in\mathcal{E}
.

If
\beta=*
, we have, thanks to (
67
),

b_{u}^{n}=x+\int_{0}^{u}\int_{\mathcal{E}}[g_{b_{v-}^{n}}(A^{z}_{b_{v-}^{n}},e)-b_{v-}^{n}]{\bm{1}}_{\{\ell_{r}(e)>1/n\}}\Pi_{\beta}(\mathrm{d}v,\mathrm{d}e).

We get as previously that

\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{[0,T\land\rho^{k}\land\rho^{k}_{n})}|b^{n}_{u}-b_{u}|\Big]\leq Te^{c_{K}T}\int_{\mathcal{E}}(M(e)\land D){\bm{1}}_{\{\ell_{r}(e)\leq 1/n\}}\mathfrak{n}_{\beta}(\mathrm{d}e),

which tends to
0
as
n\to\infty
by (
9
) and since
\ell_{r}>0

\mathfrak{n}_{*}
-a.e. by Lemma
32
.

Step 3.3.
For any
T>0
,
\sup_{[0,T]}|\tau^{n}_{u}-\tau_{u}|\to 0
in probability.

If
\beta\in(0,\alpha/2)
, we have

\displaystyle\tau_{u}^{n}=\frac{u}{n}+\int_{0}^{u}\int_{\mathcal{E}}\bar{\ell}_{b_{v-}^{n}}(A^{z}_{b_{v-}^{n}},e){\bm{1}}_{\{|e(0)|>1/n\}}\Pi_{\beta}(\mathrm{d}v,\mathrm{d}e)\quad\text{and}\quad\tau_{u}=\int_{0}^{u}\int_{\mathcal{E}}\bar{\ell}_{b_{v-}}(A^{z}_{b_{v-}},e)\Pi_{\beta}(\mathrm{d}v,\mathrm{d}e).

Using the subadditivity of
r\mapsto r\land 1
on
[0,\infty)
and that
\bar{\ell}_{b}(A,e)\leq\ell(e)
, we write

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{[0,T]}|\tau^{n}_{u}-\tau_{u}|\land 1\Big]\leq

\displaystyle\frac{T}{n}+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{E}\Big[|\bar{\ell}_{b_{v-}^{n}}(A^{z}_{b_{v-}^{n}},e)-\bar{\ell}_{b_{v-}}(A^{z}_{b_{v-}},e)|\land 1\Big]\mathfrak{n}_{\beta}(\mathrm{d}e)\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle+T\int_{\mathcal{E}}(\ell(e)\land 1){\bm{1}}_{\{|e(0)|\leq 1/n\}}\mathfrak{n}_{\beta}(\mathrm{d}e).

We conclude by dominated convergence, exactly as in Step 3.3 of the proof of Theorem
7
.

If now
\beta=*
, we have, thanks to (
67
),

\tau_{u}^{n}=\frac{u}{n}+\int_{0}^{u}\int_{\mathcal{E}}[\ell_{b_{v-}^{n}}(A^{z}_{b_{v-}^{n}},e)-1/n]{\bm{1}}_{\{\ell_{r}(e)\leq 1/n\}}\Pi_{\beta}(\mathrm{d}v,\mathrm{d}e),

whence as previously

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{[0,T]}|\tau^{n}_{u}-\tau_{u}|\land 1\Big]\leq

\displaystyle\frac{T}{n}+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big|\Big(\bar{\ell}_{b_{v-}^{n}}(A^{z}_{b_{v-}^{n}},e)-\frac{1}{n}\Big)_{+}-\bar{\ell}_{b_{v-}}(A^{z}_{b_{v-}},e)\Big|\land 1\Big]\mathfrak{n}_{*}(\mathrm{d}e)\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle+T\int_{\mathcal{E}}(\ell(e)\land 1){\bm{1}}_{\{\ell_{r}(e)\leq 1/n\}}\mathfrak{n}_{*}(\mathrm{d}e).

We conclude by dominated convergence, using (
9
) and that
\ell_{r}>0

\mathfrak{n}_{*}
-a.e. for the last term.

Steps 3.4 and 3.5 are exactly the same as in the proof of Theorem
7
, while Steps 3.6 and 3.7
are slightly easier (since
t_{n}=t
and
x_{n}=x
).
∎

Let us now write down some kind of Itô formula for the approximate process.

Lemma 51
.

Grant Assumption
1
.
Let
m>0
and let
j
be a finite measure on
\mathbb{H}
, invariant by any isometry of
\mathbb{H}
sending

\mathbf{e}_{1}
to
\mathbf{e}_{1}
. Fix
x\in\partial\mathcal{D}
. Let
(R_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be some
(\alpha,m,j)
-stable process
reflected in
{}\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0mu\mathcal{D}}
issued from
x
, built as in Definition
49
with some Poisson measure
\Pi_{j}
.
Consider the associated processes
(b_{u})_{u\geq 0}
,
(a_{u})_{u\geq 0}
,
(\tau_{u})_{u\geq 0}
,
(L_{t})_{t\geq 0}
.

(a) For all
t\geq 0
, setting
\theta=\int_{\mathcal{E}}(1-\exp(-\ell_{r}(e))\mathfrak{n}_{j}(\mathrm{d}e)
, we have

\mathbb{E}[L_{t}]\leq\theta^{-1}e^{t}.

(b) For all
\varphi\in D_{\alpha}
(recall Definition
12
), all
t\geq 0
, we have

\mathbb{E}[\varphi(R_{t})]=\varphi(x)+\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{t}{\bm{1}}_{\{R_{s}\notin\partial\mathcal{D}\}}\mathcal{L}\varphi(R_{s})\mathrm{d}s\Big]+\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{L_{t}}\mathcal{K}_{j}\varphi(b_{u},a_{u})\mathrm{d}u\Big],

where for
b\in\partial\mathcal{D}
and
A\in\mathcal{I}_{b}
,

\mathcal{K}_{j}\varphi(b,A)=\int_{\mathbb{H}}[\varphi(\Lambda(b,h_{b}(A,z)))-\varphi(b)]j(\mathrm{d}z).

(68)

(c) For all
\varphi\in D_{\alpha}
, all
t\geq 0
, we have

\Big|\mathbb{E}[\varphi(R_{t})]-\varphi(x)-\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{t}{\bm{1}}_{\{R_{s}\notin\partial\mathcal{D}\}}\mathcal{L}\varphi(R_{s})\mathrm{d}s\Big]\Big|\leq\theta^{-1}e^{t}\sup_{b\in\partial\mathcal{D},A\in\mathcal{I}_{b}}|\mathcal{K}_{j}\varphi(b,A)|.

All the expressions in (b) and (c) make sense, thanks to (a), since
j
is finite, and since

\varphi\in D_{\alpha}
implies that
\varphi
and
\mathcal{L}\varphi
are bounded on
\mathcal{D}
.

Proof.

Point (c) immediately follows from (a) and (b).

We start with (a). For
u>0
,
\mathbb{P}(L_{t}>u)\leq\mathbb{P}(\tau_{u}\leq t)=\mathbb{P}(e^{-\tau_{u}}\geq e^{-t})\leq e^{t}\mathbb{E}[e^{-\tau_{u}}]
. But recalling (
66
) and that
\bar{\ell}_{b}(a,e)\geq\ell_{r}(e)
, see Remark
3
,
we have

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}u}\mathbb{E}[e^{-\tau_{u}}]\leq-\int_{\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{E}[e^{-\tau_{u}}(1-\exp(-\bar{\ell}_{b_{u}}(a_{u},e)))]\mathfrak{n}_{j}(\mathrm{d}e)\leq-\int_{\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{E}[e^{-\tau_{u}}](1-\exp(-\ell_{r}(e)))\mathfrak{n}_{j}(\mathrm{d}e).

Hence
\mathbb{E}[e^{-\tau_{u}}]\leq\exp(-\theta u)
, so that
\mathbb{P}(L_{t}>u)\leq e^{t}e^{-\theta u}
and thus

\mathbb{E}[L_{t}]\leq e^{t}\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-\theta u}\mathrm{d}u=\theta^{-1}e^{t}
.

For (b), we write, recalling (
65
) and that
\Pi_{j}=\sum_{u\in\mathrm{J}}\delta_{(u,e_{u})}
has a finite
intensity measure,

\displaystyle\varphi(R_{t})=

\displaystyle\varphi(x)+\sum_{u\in\mathrm{J}}{\bm{1}}_{\{u<L_{t}\}}[\varphi(b_{u})-\varphi(b_{u-})]+\varphi(R_{t})-\varphi(b_{L_{t}-})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varphi(x)+\sum_{u\in\mathrm{J}}{\bm{1}}_{\{u<L_{t}\}}[\varphi(g_{b_{u-}}(a_{u},e_{u}))-\varphi(b_{u-})]+\varphi(R_{t})-\varphi(b_{L_{t}-}).

(69)

But
\bar{\ell}_{b_{u-}}(a_{u},e_{u})\leq t-\tau_{u-}
for all
u\in\mathrm{J}
such that
u<L_{t}
(because
u<L_{t}

implies that
\tau_{u}\leq t
,
i.e.

\tau_{u-}+\bar{\ell}_{b_{u-}}(a_{u},e_{u})\leq t
).
Moreover, by (
16
) (see also Remark
6
),

R_{t}={\bm{1}}_{\{L_{t}\notin\mathrm{J}\}}b_{L_{t}-}+{\bm{1}}_{\{L_{t}\in\mathrm{J},\tau_{L_{t}>t}\}}h_{b_{L_{t}-}}(a_{L_{t}},e_{L_{t}}(t-\tau_{L_{t}-}))+{\bm{1}}_{\{L_{t}\in\mathrm{J},\tau_{L_{t}}=t\}}g_{b_{L_{t}-}}(a_{L_{t}},e_{L_{t}}),

so that, since
\tau_{L_{t}}=\tau_{L_{t}-}+\bar{\ell}_{b_{L_{t}-}}(a_{L_{t}},e_{L_{t}})
when
L_{t}\in\mathrm{J}
,

\displaystyle\varphi(R_{t})-\varphi(b_{L_{t}-})=

\displaystyle{\bm{1}}_{\{L_{t}\in\mathrm{J}\}}\Big({\bm{1}}_{\{\bar{\ell}_{b_{L_{t}-}}(a_{L_{t}},e_{L_{t}})=t-\tau_{L_{t}-}\}}\varphi(g_{b_{L_{t}-}}(a_{L_{t}},e_{L_{t}}))

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt+{\bm{1}}_{\{\bar{\ell}_{b_{L_{t}-}}(a_{L_{t}},e_{L_{t}})<t-\tau_{L_{t}-}\}}\varphi(h_{b_{L_{t}-}}(a_{L_{t}},e_{L_{t}}(t-\tau_{L_{t}-})))-\varphi(b_{L_{t}-})\Big).

All in all, (
69
) rewrites as

\displaystyle\varphi(R_{t})=\varphi(x)+\sum_{u\in\mathrm{J}}{\bm{1}}_{\{u\leq L_{t}\}}\Big(

\displaystyle{\bm{1}}_{\{\bar{\ell}_{b_{u-}}(a_{u},e_{u})\leq t-\tau_{u-}\}}\varphi(g_{b_{u-}}(a_{u},e_{u}))

\displaystyle\quad+{\bm{1}}_{\{\bar{\ell}_{b_{u-}}(a_{u},e_{u})>t-\tau_{u-}\}}\varphi(h_{b_{u-}}(a_{u},e_{u}(t-\tau_{u-})))-\varphi(b_{u-})\Big).

The compensation formula gives us, since
L_{t}
is a
(\mathcal{G}_{u})_{u\geq 0}
-stopping time,

\mathbb{E}[\varphi(R_{t})]=\varphi(x)+\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{L_{t}}\Gamma\varphi(t-\tau_{u},b_{u},a_{u})\mathrm{d}u\Big],

where for
w\geq 0
,
b\in\partial\mathcal{D}
and
A\in\mathcal{I}_{b}
,

\Gamma\varphi(w,b,A)=\int_{\mathcal{E}}[{\bm{1}}_{\{\bar{\ell}_{b}(A,e)\leq w\}}\varphi(g_{b}(A,e))+{\bm{1}}_{\{\bar{\ell}_{b}(A,e)>w\}}\varphi(h_{b}(A,e(w)))-\varphi(b)]\mathfrak{n}_{j}(\mathrm{d}e).

Recall the definition (
64
) of
\mathfrak{n}_{j}
, from which we get
\mathfrak{n}_{j}(e(0)\in\mathrm{d}z)=j(\mathrm{d}z)
.
Recall also from (
68
) the definition of
\mathcal{K}_{j}
.
We now write
\Gamma\varphi(w,b,A)=\Theta\varphi(w,b,A)+\mathcal{K}_{j}\varphi(b,A)
, where

\Theta\varphi(w,b,A)\!=\!\int_{\mathcal{E}}\![{\bm{1}}_{\{\bar{\ell}_{b}(A,e)\leq w\}}\varphi(g_{b}(A,e))+{\bm{1}}_{\{\bar{\ell}_{b}(A,e)>w\}}\varphi(h_{b}(A,e(w)))-\varphi(\Lambda(b,h_{b}(A,e(0))))]\mathfrak{n}_{j}(\mathrm{d}e).

At this point, we have shown that

\mathbb{E}[\varphi(R_{t})]=\varphi(x)+\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{L_{t}}\Theta\varphi(t-\tau_{u},b_{u},a_{u})\mathrm{d}u\Big]+\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{L_{t}}\mathcal{K}_{j}\varphi(b_{u},a_{u})\mathrm{d}u\Big],

and it remains to check that
\mathbb{E}[I_{t}]=\mathbb{E}[J_{t}]
, where

I_{t}:=\int_{0}^{L_{t}}\Theta\varphi(t-\tau_{u},b_{u},a_{u})\mathrm{d}u\quad\text{and}\quad J_{t}:=\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{L}\varphi(R_{s}){\bm{1}}_{\{R_{s}\notin\partial\mathcal{D}\}}\mathrm{d}s.

Since
(R_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is defined by (
16
) (see also Remark
6
),
it holds that
R_{s}\in\partial\mathcal{D}
if and only if
s\in\cup_{u\in\mathrm{J}}(\tau_{u-},\tau_{u})
. Since moreover

\tau_{u-}\leq t
for all
u\in\mathrm{J}\cap[0,L_{t}]
, we have

\displaystyle J_{t}=\sum_{u\in\mathrm{J}}{\bm{1}}_{\{u\leq L_{t}\}}\int_{\tau_{u-}}^{\tau_{u}\land t}\mathcal{L}\varphi(R_{s})\mathrm{d}s.

Again by (
16
), we have
R_{s}=h_{b_{u-}}(a_{u},e_{u}(s-\tau_{u-}))
for all
u\in\mathrm{J}
, all

s\in(\tau_{u-},\tau_{u})
, whence

\displaystyle J_{t}=

\displaystyle\sum_{u\in\mathrm{J}}{\bm{1}}_{\{u\leq L_{t}\}}\int_{\tau_{u-}}^{\tau_{u}\land t}\mathcal{L}\varphi(h_{b_{u-}}(a_{u},e_{u}(s-\tau_{u-})))\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{u\in\mathrm{J}}{\bm{1}}_{\{u\leq L_{t}\}}\int_{0}^{(t-\tau_{u-})\land(\tau_{u}-\tau_{u-})}\mathcal{L}\varphi(h_{b_{u-}}(a_{u},e_{u}(s)))\mathrm{d}s.

Since finally
\tau_{u}-\tau_{u-}=\bar{\ell}_{b_{u-}}(a_{u},e_{u})
by (
66
), the compensation formula
tells us that

\mathbb{E}[J_{t}]=\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{L_{t}}\int_{\mathcal{E}}\Big(\int_{0}^{(t-\tau_{u})\land\bar{\ell}_{b_{u}}(a_{u},e)}\mathcal{L}\varphi(h_{b_{u}}(a_{u},e(s)))\mathrm{d}s\Big)\mathfrak{n}_{j}(\mathrm{d}e)\mathrm{d}u\Big].

(70)

Next, we show that for all
w\geq 0
, all
b\in\partial\mathcal{D}
, all
A\in\mathcal{I}_{b}
,

\Theta\varphi(w,b,A)=\int_{\mathcal{E}}\Big(\int_{0}^{w\land\bar{\ell}_{b}(A,e)}\mathcal{L}\varphi(h_{b}(A,e(s)))\mathrm{d}s\Big)\mathfrak{n}_{j}(\mathrm{d}e).

(71)

Inserted in the expression of
I_{t}
and comparing to (
70
), this will show that

\mathbb{E}[I_{t}]=\mathbb{E}[J_{t}]
and the proof will be complete.

Recall the expression (
64
) of
\mathfrak{n}_{j}
: we have, with
Z
an ISP
α,z
under
\mathbb{P}_{z}
,

\displaystyle\Theta\varphi(w,b,A)=\int_{\mathbb{H}}\Delta_{z}\varphi(w,b,A)j(\mathrm{d}z),

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{E}}\Big(\int_{0}^{w\land\bar{\ell}_{b}(A,e)}\mathcal{L}\varphi(h_{b}(A,e(s)))\mathrm{d}s\Big)\mathfrak{n}_{j}(\mathrm{d}e)=\int_{\mathbb{H}}\Delta^{\prime}_{z}\varphi(w,b,A)j(\mathrm{d}z),

where

\displaystyle\Delta_{z}\varphi(w,b,A)\!\!=\mathbb{E}_{z}\!\Big[\!{\bm{1}}_{\{\bar{\ell}_{b}(A,Z)\leq w\}}\varphi(g_{b}(A,Z_{\cdot\land\bar{\ell}_{b}(A,Z)}))\!+\!{\bm{1}}_{\{\bar{\ell}_{b}(A,Z)>w\}}\varphi(h_{b}(A,Z_{w}))\!-\!\varphi(\Lambda(b,h_{b}(A,z)))\!\Big]

\displaystyle\Delta_{z}^{\prime}\varphi(w,b,A)\!\!=\mathbb{E}_{z}\Big[\int_{0}^{w\land\bar{\ell}_{b}(A,Z)}\mathcal{L}\varphi(h_{b}(A,Z_{s}))\mathrm{d}s\Big],

and it suffices to show that
\Delta_{z}\varphi(w,b,A)=\Delta_{z}^{\prime}\varphi(w,b,A)
for all
z\in\mathbb{H}
,

w\geq 0
,
b\in\partial\mathcal{D}
,
A\in\mathcal{I}_{b}
.

If
h_{b}(A,z)\notin\mathcal{D}
, this is obvious, since we
\mathbb{P}_{z}
-a.s. have
\bar{\ell}_{b}(A,Z)=0
and

g_{b}(A,Z_{\cdot\land\bar{\ell}_{b}(A,Z)})=\Lambda(b,h_{b}(A,z))
.

If
h_{b}(A,z)\in\mathcal{D}
, we have, recalling the definition of
g_{b}(A,\cdot)
,
using that
h_{b}(A,Z)
is an ISP
{}_{\alpha,h_{b}(A,z)}
under
\mathbb{P}_{z}
and setting

\tilde{\ell}(Z)=\inf\{t>0:Z_{t}\notin\mathcal{D}\}
,

\Delta_{z}\varphi(w,b,A)=\mathbb{E}_{h_{b}(A,z)}\Big[{\bm{1}}_{\{\tilde{\ell}(Z)\leq w\}}\varphi(\Lambda(Z_{\tilde{\ell}(Z)-},Z_{\tilde{\ell}(Z)}))+{\bm{1}}_{\{\tilde{\ell}(Z)>w\}}\varphi(Z_{w})-\varphi(h_{b}(A,z))\Big].

Recalling Definition
8
, we thus find, with
d_{0}^{*}=\inf\{s>0:X^{*}_{s}\notin\mathcal{D}\}
,

\displaystyle\Delta_{z}\varphi(w,b,A)=

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}_{h_{b}(A,z)}\Big[\varphi(X^{*}_{w\land d_{0}^{*}})-\varphi(h_{b}(A,z))\Big]=\mathbb{Q}_{h_{b}(A,z)}\Big[\int_{0}^{w\land d_{0}^{*}}\mathcal{L}\varphi(X^{*}_{s})\mathrm{d}s\Big]

by Lemma
48
.
Using one more time Definition
8
and then the isotropy of the stable process,

\displaystyle\Delta_{z}\varphi(w,b,A)\!=\!\mathbb{E}_{h_{b}(A,z)}\Big[\int_{0}^{w\land\tilde{\ell}(Z)}\mathcal{L}\varphi(Z_{s})\mathrm{d}s\Big]\!=\!\mathbb{E}_{z}\Big[\int_{0}^{w\land\bar{\ell}_{b}(A,Z)}\mathcal{L}\varphi(h_{b}(A,Z_{s}))\mathrm{d}s\Big]\!=\!\Delta_{z}^{\prime}\varphi(w,b,A)

as desired.
∎

Finally, we can handle the

Proof of Proposition
47
.

We consider
\varphi\in D_{\alpha}\cap H_{\beta}
and
split the proof into 3 steps.

Step 1.
We first prove (
63
) when
x\in\partial\mathcal{D}
and
\beta\in(0,\alpha/2)
.
For each
n\geq 1
, we consider
m_{n}=1/n
and
j^{n}_{\beta}
as in Lemma
50
. By Lemma
51
, we know
that, with
\theta_{n}=\int_{\mathcal{E}}(1-e^{-\ell_{r}(e)})\mathfrak{n}_{j^{n}_{\beta}}(\mathrm{d}e)
,

\Big|\mathbb{Q}_{x}^{m_{n},j^{n}_{\beta}}[\varphi(X^{*}_{t})]-\varphi(x)-\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{Q}_{x}^{m_{n},j^{n}_{\beta}}[{\bm{1}}_{\{X_{s}^{*}\notin\partial\mathcal{D}\}}\mathcal{L}\varphi(X_{s}^{*})]\mathrm{d}s\Big|\leq\theta_{n}^{-1}e^{t}\sup_{b\in\partial\mathcal{D},A\in\mathcal{I}_{b}}|\mathcal{K}_{j^{n}_{\beta}}\varphi(b,A)|.

(72)

Fix
n_{0}>1/r
, so that
B_{d}(r\mathbf{e}_{1},r)\cap\{|z|>1/n_{0}\}\neq\emptyset
. Then for any
n\geq n_{0}
,
we have
\theta_{n}\geq\theta_{n_{0}}>0
. Indeed,
recalling the definition (
64
) of
\mathfrak{n}_{j}
and that of
j^{\beta}_{n}
, we find

\theta_{n_{0}}=\int_{\mathbb{H}}{\bm{1}}_{\{|z|>1/n_{0}\}}\mathbb{E}_{z}[1-\exp(-\ell_{r}(Z))]\frac{\mathrm{d}z}{|z|^{d+\beta}},

which is strictly positive since
\mathbb{P}_{z}(\ell_{r}(Z)>0)=1
for all
z\in B_{d}(r\mathbf{e}_{1},r)
.
Next, comparing Definition
14
-(a) and (
68
), we see that

\mathcal{K}_{j^{n}_{\beta}}\varphi(b,A)=\mathcal{H}_{\beta,1/n}\varphi(b)
.
Since
\varphi\in H_{\beta}
, we conclude that
\lim_{n}\sup_{b\in\partial\mathcal{D},A\in\mathcal{I}_{b}}|\mathcal{K}_{j^{n}_{\beta}}\varphi(b,A)|=0
.

By Lemma
50
and since
\varphi\in C({}\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0mu\mathcal{D}})
, we see that
\lim_{n}\mathbb{Q}_{x}^{m_{n},j^{n}_{\beta}}[\varphi(X^{*}_{t})]=\mathbb{Q}_{x}[\varphi(X^{*}_{t})]
. Since
\mathcal{L}\varphi\in C(\mathcal{D})\cap L^{\infty}(\mathcal{D})
, we also have

\lim_{n}\mathbb{Q}_{x}^{m_{n},j^{n}_{\beta}}[\mathcal{L}\varphi(X^{*}_{s}){\bm{1}}_{\{X^{*}_{s}\notin\partial\mathcal{D}\}}]=\mathbb{Q}_{x}[\mathcal{L}\varphi(X^{*}_{s})]
for all
s\geq 0
such that
\mathbb{Q}_{x}(X^{*}_{s}\in\partial\mathcal{D})=0
. Since
\mathbb{Q}_{x}(X^{*}_{s}\in\partial\mathcal{D})=0

for a.e.
s\geq 0
, see Theorem
9
, and since
\mathcal{L}\varphi
is bounded,
we conclude that
\lim_{n}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{Q}_{x}^{m_{n},j^{n}_{\beta}}[{\bm{1}}_{\{X_{s}^{*}\notin\partial\mathcal{D}\}}\mathcal{L}\varphi(X_{s}^{*})]\mathrm{d}s=\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{Q}_{x}[\mathcal{L}\varphi(X_{s}^{*})]\mathrm{d}s
.
We thus may let
n\to\infty
in (
72
) and find (
63
).

Step 2.
We next prove (
63
) when
x\in\partial\mathcal{D}
and
\beta=*
.
As in Step 1, we start from (
72
) and, using the very same arguments, we only have to verify that
the RHS of (
72
) tends to
0
as
n\to\infty
,
i.e.
that

\liminf_{n}\theta_{n}>0
and that
\lim_{n}\Delta_{n}=0
,
where
\Delta_{n}=\sup_{b\in\partial\mathcal{D},A\in\mathcal{I}_{b}}|\mathcal{K}_{j^{n}_{*}}\varphi(b,A)|=0
.

Let us first recall that
j^{n}_{*}(\mathrm{d}x)=\mathfrak{n}_{*}(e(1/n)\in\mathrm{d}x,\ell_{r}(e)>1/n)
, from which we get that for any

n\geq 1
,
\theta_{n}=\int_{\mathcal{E}}(1-e^{-(\ell_{r}(e)-1/n)}){\bm{1}}_{\{\ell_{r}(e)>1/n\}}\mathfrak{n}_{*}(\mathrm{d}e)
.
Using Fatou’s lemma, we get

\liminf_{n}\theta_{n}\geq\int_{\mathcal{E}}(1-e^{-\ell_{r}(e)})\mathfrak{n}_{*}(\mathrm{d}e)>0
, see Lemma
32
.

Since
j^{n}_{*}
is carried by
B_{d}(r\mathbf{e}_{1},r)
by definition
of
\ell_{r}
, since
h_{b}(A,z)=b+Az\in B_{d}(b+r\mathbf{n}_{b},r)
for all
z\in B_{d}(r\mathbf{e}_{1},r)

and since
B_{d}(b+r\mathbf{n}_{b},r)\subset\mathcal{D}
by definition of
r
(see Remark
3
),
we have, recalling (
68
),

\mathcal{K}_{j^{n}_{*}}\varphi(b,A)=\int_{B_{d}(r\mathbf{e}_{1},r)}[\varphi(b+Az)-\varphi(b)]j^{n}_{*}(\mathrm{d}z).

Recall that

\mathbb{S}_{*}=\{\rho\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:|\rho|=1,\rho\cdot\mathbf{e}_{1}=0\}
and that
\varsigma
is the uniform measure on
\mathbb{S}_{*}
.
Since
j^{n}_{*}
is invariant by any
isometry of
\mathbb{H}
sending
\mathbf{e}_{1}
to
\mathbf{e}_{1}
, it holds that

j^{n}_{*}(B)=\int_{B_{2}(r\mathbf{e}_{1},r)}\int_{\mathbb{S}_{*}}{\bm{1}}_{\{h_{1}\mathbf{e}_{1}+h_{2}\rho\in B\}}\zeta(\mathrm{d}\rho)g^{n}_{*}(\mathrm{d}h)

for any
B\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{H})
, where
g^{n}_{*}
is the image measure of
j^{n}_{*}
by

B_{d}(r\mathbf{e}_{1},r)\ni z\mapsto h=(z\cdot\mathbf{e}_{1},\sqrt{|z|^{2}-(z\cdot\mathbf{e}_{1})^{2}})\in B_{2}(r\mathbf{e}_{1},r).

As a conclusion,

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{j^{n}_{*}}\varphi(b,A)=

\displaystyle\int_{B_{2}(r\mathbf{e}_{1},r)}\int_{\mathbb{S}_{*}}[\varphi(b+A(h_{1}\mathbf{e}_{1}+h_{2}\rho))-\varphi(b)]\zeta(\mathrm{d}\rho)g^{n}_{*}(\mathrm{d}h)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{B_{2}(r\mathbf{e}_{1},r)}|h|^{\alpha/2}\mathcal{H}_{*}\varphi(b,h)g^{n}_{*}(\mathrm{d}h),

with
\mathcal{H}_{*}\varphi
introduced in Definition
14
-(b). We set

\Psi(u)=\sup_{b\in\partial\mathcal{D},h\in B_{2}(r\mathbf{e}_{1},r)\cap B_{2}(0,u)}|\mathcal{H}_{*}\varphi(b,h)|
for
u\in(0,2r)

(observe that
h\in B_{2}(r\mathbf{e}_{1},r)
implies that
|h|<2r
). Since
\varphi\in H_{*}
, recall
Definition
14
-(b),
\Psi
is bounded and satisfies
\lim_{u\to 0}\Psi(u)=0
. We now write

\Delta_{n}\leq\int_{B_{2}(r\mathbf{e}_{1},r)}|h|^{\alpha/2}\Psi(|h|)g^{n}_{*}(\mathrm{d}h)=\int_{B_{d}(r\mathbf{e}_{1},r)}|z|^{\alpha/2}\Psi(|z|)j^{n}_{*}(\mathrm{d}z).

Recalling the definition of
j^{n}_{*}
and that
\ell\geq\ell_{r}
, we find

\displaystyle\Delta_{n}\leq

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{E}}|e(1/n)|^{\alpha/2}\Psi(|e(1/n)|){\bm{1}}_{\{e(1/n)\in B_{d}(r\mathbf{e}_{1},r)\}}{\bm{1}}_{\{\ell_{r}(e)>1/n\}}\mathfrak{n}_{*}(\mathrm{d}e).

Using the notation of Lemma
28
, this gives

\Delta_{n}\leq\int_{\mathbb{H}}|a|^{\alpha/2}\Psi(|a|){\bm{1}}_{\{a\in B_{d}(r\mathbf{e}_{1},r)\}}k_{1/n}(\mathrm{d}a).

But Lemma
28
tells us that
|a|^{\alpha/2}k_{t}(\mathrm{d}a)\to c_{1}\delta_{0}(\mathrm{d}a)
weakly as
t\to 0
.
Since
\lim_{u\to 0}\Psi(u)=0
and since
\Psi
is bounded, we conclude
that
\lim_{n}\Delta_{n}=0
as desired.

Step 3.
We finally prove (
63
) when
x\in\mathcal{D}
. With
d_{0}^{*}=\inf\{t>0:X^{*}_{t}\in\partial\mathcal{D}\}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}_{x}[\varphi(X_{t}^{*})]=

\displaystyle\mathbb{Q}_{x}[\varphi(X_{t\land d_{0}^{*}}^{*})]+\mathbb{Q}_{x}[{\bm{1}}_{\{t>d_{0}^{*}\}}(\varphi(X_{t}^{*})-\varphi(X_{t\land d_{0}^{*}}^{*}))]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varphi(x)+\mathbb{Q}_{x}\Big[\int_{0}^{t\land d_{0}^{*}}\mathcal{L}\varphi(X^{*}_{s})\mathrm{d}s\Big]+\mathbb{Q}_{x}\Big[{\bm{1}}_{\{t>d_{0}^{*}\}}\psi(X^{*}_{d_{0}^{*}},t-d_{0}^{*})\Big],

where
\psi(z,r)=\mathbb{Q}_{z}[\varphi(X^{*}_{r})-\varphi(z)]
. We used Lemma
48
for the first term and the
strong Markov property for the second one. By Steps 1 and 2, we know that

\psi(z,r)=\mathbb{Q}_{z}[\int_{0}^{r}\mathcal{L}\varphi(X^{*}_{s})\mathrm{d}s]

for all
z\in\partial\mathcal{D}
. Since
X^{*}_{d_{0}^{*}}\in\partial\mathcal{D}
, we conclude, using the strong Markov property again, that

\mathbb{Q}_{x}[\varphi(X_{t}^{*})]=\varphi(x)+\mathbb{Q}_{x}\Big[\int_{0}^{t\land d_{0}^{*}}\mathcal{L}\varphi(X^{*}_{s})\mathrm{d}s\Big]+\mathbb{Q}_{x}\Big[{\bm{1}}_{\{t>d_{0}^{*}\}}\int_{d_{0}^{*}}^{t}\mathcal{L}\varphi(X^{*}_{s})\mathrm{d}s\Big].

The conclusion follows.
∎

8
The scattering process and its Markov version

Here we prove Remark
20
, which shows that the scattering process
introduced in Definition
19
does not explode. We then introduce a Markov variation of the (position
of) the scattering process and explain why it is sufficient to the study scaling limit of this Markov process.

Proof of Remark
20
.

Recall the definition of the sequence
(T_{n}^{\varepsilon})_{n\geq 1}
from Definition
19
.
First, the sequence
T_{n}^{\varepsilon}
is a.s. strictly increasing because
\lambda(x,v,s)>0
for all
(x,v)\in\bm{E}
,
all
s>0
. We next show that there exist
a>0
and
\rho>0
(depending on
\varepsilon
) such that
for
U\sim\mathrm{F}(v)\mathrm{d}v
,
W\sim\mathrm{G}_{+}(v)\mathrm{d}v
and
E^{\varepsilon}\sim\text{Exp}(\varepsilon^{-1})
independent,

\displaystyle\forall x\in\mathcal{D},\quad p_{x}(a)=\mathbb{P}(\lambda(x,U,E^{\varepsilon})\geq a)\geq\rho,

(73)

\displaystyle\forall x\in\partial\mathcal{D},\forall A\in\mathcal{I}_{x},\quad q_{x}(a)=\mathbb{P}(\lambda(x,AW,E^{\varepsilon})\geq a)\geq\rho,

(74)

The domain
\mathcal{D}
being open and
C^{2}
, there exists
\delta>0
such that for all
x\in\mathcal{D}
, there
exists a ball
C_{x}\subset\mathcal{D}
with radius
\delta
such that
x\in\partial C_{x}
. Calling
y_{x}
the center of

C_{x}
, we have
C_{x}=B_{d}(y_{x},\delta)
and
\delta=|x-y_{x}|
. One may check that for
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
s>0
,

x+sv\in C_{x}
if and only if
\frac{v}{|v|}\cdot\frac{y_{x}-x}{|y_{x}-x|}>\frac{|v|s}{2\delta}
, and in such a case,

\lambda(x,v,s)=s
. Thus for all
x\in\mathcal{D}
,

p_{x}(a)\geq\mathbb{P}\Big(E^{\varepsilon}\geq a,\frac{U}{|U|}\cdot\frac{y_{x}-x}{|y_{x}-x|}>\frac{|U|E^{\varepsilon}}{2\delta}\Big)=\mathbb{P}\Big(E^{\varepsilon}\geq a,\frac{U}{|U|}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{1}>\frac{|U|E^{\varepsilon}}{2\delta}\Big)

by rotational invariance of
\mathrm{F}
. Since
\frac{U}{|U|}
is independent of
|U|
,

p_{x}(a)\geq\mathbb{P}(E^{\varepsilon}\in(a,2a))\mathbb{P}\Big(\frac{U}{|U|}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{1}>\frac{1}{2}\Big)\mathbb{P}\Big(|U|<\frac{\delta}{2a}\Big).

This last quantity does not depend on
x
and is positive if

a>0
is small enough so that
{\mathbb{P}(|U|<\frac{\delta}{2a})>0}
, since
\frac{U}{|U|}
is uniformly distributed
on
\mathbb{S}_{d-1}
.

The domain
\mathcal{D}
being open and
C^{2}
, there is
\delta>0
such that for all
x\in\partial\mathcal{D}
,

B_{d}(x+\delta\mathbf{n}_{x},\delta)\subset\mathcal{D}
. One concludes as previously that
\inf_{x\in\partial\mathcal{D}}q_{x}(a)>0

if
a>0
is small enough.

Recalling Definition
19
, one easily deduces from (
73
)-(
74
)
that if setting
\mathcal{G}_{n}^{\varepsilon}=\sigma(T_{1}^{\varepsilon},\dots,T_{n}^{\varepsilon})
, we have
\mathbb{P}(T_{n+1}^{\varepsilon}-T_{n}^{\varepsilon}\geq a|\mathcal{G}_{n}^{\varepsilon})\geq\rho

for all
n\geq 0
, so that
\mathbb{E}[1-e^{-(T_{n+1}^{\varepsilon}-T_{n}^{\varepsilon})}|\mathcal{G}_{n}^{\varepsilon}]\geq\rho(1-e^{-a})
. Hence, we get

\mathbb{E}[e^{-T_{n+1}^{\varepsilon}}]=\mathbb{E}\Big[e^{-T_{n}^{\varepsilon}}\mathbb{E}\Big[e^{-(T_{n+1}^{\varepsilon}-T_{n}^{\varepsilon})}\Big|\mathcal{G}_{n}^{\varepsilon}\Big]\Big]\leq(1-\rho(1-e^{-a}))\mathbb{E}[e^{-T_{n}^{\varepsilon}}].

Thus
\mathbb{E}[e^{-T_{n}^{\varepsilon}}]\leq\theta^{n}
, where
\theta=1-\rho(1-e^{-a})<1

and
\mathbb{P}(T_{n}^{\varepsilon}\leq T)=\mathbb{P}(e^{-T_{n}^{\varepsilon}}\geq e^{-T})\leq e^{T}\theta^{n}
.
Hence
\lim_{n}T_{n}^{\varepsilon}=\infty
a.s. and
\mathbb{P}(\bm{M}^{\varepsilon}_{T}\geq n)=\mathbb{P}(T_{n}^{\varepsilon}\leq T)\leq e^{T}\theta^{n}
,
so that
\mathbb{E}[\bm{M}^{\varepsilon}_{T}]<\infty
.
∎

We introduce a few notation.

Notation 52
.

Grant Assumption
18
.
Fix
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
and consider three independent random variables
U\sim\mathrm{F}(v)\mathrm{d}v
,
W\sim\mathrm{G}_{+}(v)\mathrm{d}v
and

E^{\varepsilon}\sim\rm{Exp}(\varepsilon^{-1})
. We call
\mathrm{F}_{\varepsilon}
the density of
\varepsilon^{(1-\alpha)/\alpha}E^{\varepsilon}U
, carried by
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\mathrm{G}_{\varepsilon}
the density of
\varepsilon^{(1-\alpha)/\alpha}E^{\varepsilon}W
, carried by
\mathbb{H}
, and
finally
\mathrm{H}_{\varepsilon}
the density of
(\varepsilon^{(1-\alpha)/\alpha}E^{\varepsilon}U,\varepsilon^{(1-\alpha)/\alpha}E^{\varepsilon}W)
,
carried by
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{H}
.

The scattering process
(\bm{X}^{\varepsilon}_{t},\bm{V}^{\varepsilon}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is Markov, but the position process
(\bm{X}^{\varepsilon}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
alone
is not. We now introduce some Markov process
(R^{\varepsilon}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
that will be close to
(\bm{X}^{\varepsilon}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
.
More precisely, as we will see in the proof of Theorem
23
,

(\bm{X}^{\varepsilon}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is the linear interpolation of
(R^{\varepsilon}_{\lambda^{\varepsilon}_{t}})_{t\geq 0}
, where
\lambda^{\varepsilon}
is a time-change
close to identity.

Definition 53
.

Grant Assumptions
1
and
18
.
Fix
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
and
consider a Poisson measure
\mathrm{M}_{\varepsilon}
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{H}
, with intensity

\varepsilon^{-1}\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{H}_{\varepsilon}(u,w)\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}w
.
We also consider a measurable family
(A_{y})_{y\in\partial\mathcal{D}}
such that
A_{y}\in\mathcal{I}_{y}

for each
y\in\partial\mathcal{D}
and recall that
\Lambda
was defined in (
10
).
For
x\in{}\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0mu\mathcal{D}}
, we say that
(R^{\varepsilon}_{t})
is an
\varepsilon
-Markov scattering process issued from
x
if a.s.,
for all
t\geq 0
,

R_{t}^{\varepsilon}=x+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{H}}[\Lambda(R_{s-}^{\varepsilon},R_{s-}^{\varepsilon}+u{\bm{1}}_{\{R_{s-}^{\varepsilon}\notin\partial\mathcal{D}\}}+A_{R_{s-}^{\varepsilon}}w{\bm{1}}_{\{R_{s-}^{\varepsilon}\in\partial\mathcal{D}\}})-R_{s-}^{\varepsilon}]\mathrm{M}_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}u,\mathrm{d}w).

(75)

This process is well-defined.

Remark 54
.

Grant Assumptions
1
and
18
and
fix
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
. The S.D.E. (
75
) has, for each
x\in{}\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0mu\mathcal{D}}
, a pathwise unique
solution
(R^{\varepsilon}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
, which is
{}\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0mu\mathcal{D}}
-valued. As solution to a time-homogeneous
well-posed S.D.E., the resulting process is strongly Markov. The law of
(R^{\varepsilon}_{t})_{t\geq 0}

does not depend on the choice of the family
(A_{y})_{y\in\partial\mathcal{D}}
.

Proof.

Note that for any
t>0
,

\mathrm{M}_{\varepsilon}([0,t]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{H})=\varepsilon^{-1}t<\infty
.
Hence (
75
) may be solved by induction on the jump times of
\mathrm{M}_{\varepsilon}
,
implying its pathwise well-posedness. The last assertion follows from the fact that for
y\in\partial\mathcal{D}
and
for
A,B\in\mathcal{I}_{y}
,
\mathrm{G}_{\varepsilon}\#A=\mathrm{G}_{\varepsilon}\#B
.
∎

The following convergence result will be the object of Sections
9
and
10
.

Proposition 55
.

Grant Assumption
1
and Assumption
18
with some
\alpha\in(0,2)
and with

\kappa_{\mathrm{F}}=1/\Gamma(\alpha+1)
. Grant either Assumption
22
-(a)
(in which case, set
\beta=*
) or Assumption
22
-(b)
(in which case
\beta\in(0,\alpha/2)
).
Consider the family

(\mathbb{Q}_{x})_{x\in{}\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0mu\mathcal{D}}}
as in Theorem
9
, with these values of
\alpha
and
\beta
.
Consider, for each
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
, the solution
(R_{t}^{\varepsilon})
to (
75
) starting from
some
x_{\varepsilon}\in{}\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0mu\mathcal{D}}
. If
x_{\varepsilon}\to x\in{}\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0mu\mathcal{D}}
, then

(R_{t}^{\varepsilon})_{t\geq 0}\quad\text{converges in law to}\quad\mathbb{Q}_{x}\quad\text{as }\varepsilon\to 0

in
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},{}\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0mu\mathcal{D}})
, endowed with the
{\bm{\mathrm{J}}_{1}}
-topology.

We will also show the following result in Section
10
.

Lemma 56
.

Grant the same assumptions as in Proposition
55
.
For
(x,v)\in\bm{E}
and
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
, consider the sequence
(T_{n}^{\varepsilon})_{n\geq 1}
introduced in
Definition
19
, set
T_{0}^{\varepsilon}=0
and
N^{\varepsilon}_{t}=\sum_{n\geq 1}{\bm{1}}_{\{T_{n}^{\varepsilon}\leq t\}}
. For any
T>0
,

\displaystyle\sup_{n=0,\dots,N^{\varepsilon}_{T}}(T^{\varepsilon}_{n+1}-T_{n}^{\varepsilon})\to 0\quad\text{in probability as $\varepsilon\to 0$,}

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|\varepsilon N^{\varepsilon}_{t}-t|\to 0\quad\text{in probability as $\varepsilon\to 0$.}

Admitting Lemma
56
and Proposition
55
, we give the

Proof of Theorem
23
.

We divide the proof in several steps.

Step 1.
With the notation of Definition
19
and with
T^{\varepsilon}_{0}=0
, we introduce the process

\bar{\bm{X}}^{\varepsilon}_{t}=\sum_{n\geq 0}\bm{X}^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n}^{\varepsilon}}{\bm{1}}_{\{t\in[T_{n}^{\varepsilon},T^{\varepsilon}_{n+1})\}}
, of which
\bm{X}^{\varepsilon}
is the linear
interpolation. We check here that
it is enough to show that
(\bar{\bm{X}}^{\varepsilon}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
converges in law to
\mathbb{Q}_{x}
for the
{\bm{\mathrm{J}}_{1}}
-topology.

We know from Lemma
56
that
\sup_{n=0,\dots,N^{\varepsilon}_{T}}|T^{\varepsilon}_{n+1}-T_{n}^{\varepsilon}|\to 0

in probability as
\varepsilon\to 0
for any
T>0
. By Lemma
84
, we conclude that
if
(\bar{\bm{X}}^{\varepsilon}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
converges in law to
\mathbb{Q}_{x}
in
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},{}\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0mu\mathcal{D}})
for the

{\bm{\mathrm{J}}_{1}}
-topology, then
(\bm{X}^{\varepsilon}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
converges in law to
\mathbb{Q}_{x}

for the
{\bm{\mathrm{M}}_{1}}
-topology.

Step 2.
We next introduce
(Y^{\varepsilon}_{t}=\bar{\bm{X}}^{\varepsilon}_{T_{1}^{\varepsilon}+t})_{t\geq 0}
and verify that it suffices to show that

(Y^{\varepsilon}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
converges in law to
\mathbb{Q}_{x}
for the
{\bm{\mathrm{J}}_{1}}
-topology.

We introduce the continuous increasing time-change

\lambda_{\varepsilon}(t)=\frac{T^{\varepsilon}_{2}}{T^{\varepsilon}_{2}-T^{\varepsilon}_{1}}t{\bm{1}}_{\{t\leq T^{\varepsilon}_{2}-T^{\varepsilon}_{1}\}}+(T_{1}^{\varepsilon}+t){\bm{1}}_{\{t>T^{\varepsilon}_{2}-T^{\varepsilon}_{1}\}}.

By definition of the
{\bm{\mathrm{J}}_{1}}
-topology, see Appendix
C
, it suffices to show that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}||\lambda_{\varepsilon}-I||_{\infty}=0
and
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}||Y^{\varepsilon}-\bar{\bm{X}}^{\varepsilon}\circ\lambda_{\varepsilon}||_{\infty}=0

in probability. But

||\lambda_{\varepsilon}-I||_{\infty}=T^{\varepsilon}_{1}\leq E^{\varepsilon}_{1}\sim\text{Exp}(\varepsilon^{-1})
, which tends to 0 in probability,
while

||Y^{\varepsilon}-\bar{\bm{X}}^{\varepsilon}\circ\lambda_{\varepsilon}||_{\infty}=\sup_{t\in[0,T^{\varepsilon}_{2}-T^{\varepsilon}_{1}]}|\bar{\bm{X}}^{\varepsilon}_{T_{1}^{\varepsilon}+t}-\bar{\bm{X}}^{\varepsilon}_{\lambda_{\varepsilon}(t)}|\leq\sup_{t\in[0,T^{\varepsilon}_{2}-T^{\varepsilon}_{1}]}|\bar{\bm{X}}^{\varepsilon}_{T_{1}^{\varepsilon}+t}-x|+\sup_{t\in[0,T^{\varepsilon}_{2}-T^{\varepsilon}_{1}]}|\bar{\bm{X}}^{\varepsilon}_{\lambda_{\varepsilon}(t)}-x|,

so that

||Y^{\varepsilon}-\bar{\bm{X}}^{\varepsilon}\circ\lambda_{\varepsilon}||_{\infty}\leq 2\sup_{[0,T_{2}^{\varepsilon}]}|\bar{\bm{X}}^{\varepsilon}_{t}-x|\leq 2(|\bm{X}^{\varepsilon}_{T^{\varepsilon}_{1}}-x|\lor|\bm{X}^{\varepsilon}_{T^{\varepsilon}_{2}}-x|).

Looking at Definition
19
, we deduce that

\displaystyle||Y^{\varepsilon}-\bar{\bm{X}}^{\varepsilon}\circ\lambda_{\varepsilon}||_{\infty}\leq

\displaystyle 2\varepsilon^{(1-\alpha)/\alpha}|v|T_{1}^{\varepsilon}+2\varepsilon^{(1-\alpha)/\alpha}|\bm{V}^{\varepsilon}_{T_{1}^{\varepsilon}}|(T_{2}^{\varepsilon}-T^{\varepsilon}_{1})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2|v|\varepsilon^{(1-\alpha)/\alpha}E^{\varepsilon}_{1}+2(|U_{1}|+|W_{1}|)\varepsilon^{(1-\alpha)/\alpha}E^{\varepsilon}_{2},

which goes to
0
in probability, since
\varepsilon^{(1-\alpha)/\alpha}E^{\varepsilon}_{1}
and
\varepsilon^{(1-\alpha)/\alpha}E^{\varepsilon}_{2}

are equal in law to
\varepsilon^{1/\alpha}E_{1}^{1}
.

Step 3.
We consider an i.i.d. family
(F_{n}^{\varepsilon})_{n\geq 1}
of
\mathrm{Exp}(\varepsilon^{-1})
-distributed random variables
independent of everything else, set
S^{\varepsilon}_{n}=F_{1}^{\varepsilon}+\dots+F_{n}^{\varepsilon}
and introduce the Poisson measure

\mathrm{M}_{\varepsilon}=\sum_{n\geq 1}\delta_{(S^{\varepsilon}_{n},U_{n}^{\varepsilon},W_{n}^{\varepsilon})},\quad\text{where}\quad U_{n}^{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon^{(1-\alpha)/\alpha}E_{n+1}^{\varepsilon}U_{n}\quad\text{and}\quad W_{n}^{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon^{(1-\alpha)/\alpha}E_{n+1}^{\varepsilon}W_{n}.

Its intensity is
\varepsilon^{-1}\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{H}_{\varepsilon}(u,w)\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}w
, and it is independent of
\bm{X}^{\varepsilon}_{T_{1}^{\varepsilon}}

(which is a function of
x,v,E^{\varepsilon}_{1}
). We then consider the solution
Z^{\varepsilon}_{t}

to (
75
) with this Poisson measure and starting at

\bm{X}^{\varepsilon}_{T_{1}^{\varepsilon}}
. We claim that
Y^{\varepsilon}_{t}=Z^{\varepsilon}_{\rho^{\varepsilon}_{t}}
, where
\rho^{\varepsilon}_{t}=S^{\varepsilon}_{M^{\varepsilon}_{t}}
, with

M^{\varepsilon}_{t}=\sum_{n\geq 1}{\bm{1}}_{\{T_{n+1}^{\varepsilon}-T_{1}^{\varepsilon}\leq t\}}
.

Looking at Definition
19
, one can check that for all
n\geq 1
,

\bm{X}^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n+1}^{\varepsilon}}=\Lambda(\bm{X}^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n}^{\varepsilon}},\bm{X}^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n}^{\varepsilon}}+U_{n}^{\varepsilon}){\bm{1}}_{\{\bm{X}^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n}^{\varepsilon}}\in\mathcal{D}\}}+\Lambda(\bm{X}^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n}^{\varepsilon}},\bm{X}^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n}^{\varepsilon}}+A_{\bm{X}^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n}^{\varepsilon}}}W_{n}^{\varepsilon}){\bm{1}}_{\{\bm{X}^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n}^{\varepsilon}}\in\partial\mathcal{D}\}}.

Indeed, if first
\bm{X}^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n}^{\varepsilon}}\in\mathcal{D}
, then
\bm{V}^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n}^{\varepsilon}}=U_{n}
and thus

\bm{X}^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n+1}^{\varepsilon}}=\bm{X}^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n}^{\varepsilon}}+\varepsilon^{(1-\alpha)/\alpha}U_{n}\lambda(\bm{X}^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n}^{\varepsilon}},\varepsilon^{(1-\alpha)/\alpha}U_{n},E^{\varepsilon}_{n+1})=\Lambda(\bm{X}^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n}^{\varepsilon}},\bm{X}^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n}^{\varepsilon}}+\varepsilon^{(1-\alpha)/\alpha}E_{n+1}^{\varepsilon}U_{n})

by (
23
). If next
\bm{X}^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n}^{\varepsilon}}\in\partial\mathcal{D}
, then
\bm{V}^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n}^{\varepsilon}}=A_{\bm{X}^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n}^{\varepsilon}}}W_{n}
and thus

\bm{X}^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n+1}^{\varepsilon}}=\bm{X}^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n}^{\varepsilon}}+\varepsilon^{(1-\alpha)/\alpha}A_{\bm{X}^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n}^{\varepsilon}}}W_{n}\lambda(\bm{X}^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n}^{\varepsilon}},\varepsilon^{(1-\alpha)/\alpha}A_{\bm{X}^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n}^{\varepsilon}}}W_{n},E^{\varepsilon}_{n+1}),

which equals
\Lambda(\bm{X}^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n}^{\varepsilon}},\bm{X}^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n}^{\varepsilon}}+\varepsilon^{(1-\alpha)/\alpha}E_{n+1}^{\varepsilon}A_{\bm{X}^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n}^{\varepsilon}}}W_{n})
by (
23
).

Consequently, for all
n\geq 1
, since
Y^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n}^{\varepsilon}-T_{1}^{\varepsilon}}=\bm{X}^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n}^{\varepsilon}}
,

Y^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n+1}^{\varepsilon}-T_{1}^{\varepsilon}}=\Lambda(Y^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n}^{\varepsilon}-T_{1}^{\varepsilon}},Y^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n}^{\varepsilon}-T_{1}^{\varepsilon}}+U_{n}^{\varepsilon}){\bm{1}}_{\{Y^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n}^{\varepsilon}-T_{1}^{\varepsilon}}\in\mathcal{D}\}}+\Lambda(Y^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n}^{\varepsilon}-T_{1}^{\varepsilon}},Y^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n}^{\varepsilon}-T_{1}^{\varepsilon}}+A_{Y^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n}^{\varepsilon}-T_{1}^{\varepsilon}}}W_{n}^{\varepsilon}){\bm{1}}_{\{Y^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n}^{\varepsilon}-T_{1}^{\varepsilon}}\in\partial\mathcal{D}\}}.

Next, we immediately deduce from (
75
) and the definition of
\mathrm{M}_{\varepsilon}

that for all
n\geq 1
,

Z^{\varepsilon}_{S^{\varepsilon}_{n}}=\Lambda(Z^{\varepsilon}_{S^{\varepsilon}_{n-1}},Z^{\varepsilon}_{S^{\varepsilon}_{n-1}}+U_{n}^{\varepsilon}){\bm{1}}_{\{Z^{\varepsilon}_{S^{\varepsilon}_{n-1}}\in\mathcal{D}\}}+\Lambda(Z^{\varepsilon}_{S^{\varepsilon}_{n-1}},Z^{\varepsilon}_{S^{\varepsilon}_{n-1}}+A_{Z^{\varepsilon}_{S^{\varepsilon}_{n-1}}}W_{n}^{\varepsilon}){\bm{1}}_{\{Z^{\varepsilon}_{S^{\varepsilon}_{n-1}}\in\partial\mathcal{D}\}}.

Since
Y^{\varepsilon}_{0}=\bm{X}^{\varepsilon}_{T_{1}^{\varepsilon}}=Z^{\varepsilon}_{0}
, we conclude that for all
n\geq 0
, we have
Y^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n+1}^{\varepsilon}-T_{1}^{\varepsilon}}=Z^{\varepsilon}_{S^{\varepsilon}_{n}}
.

Thus for all
n\geq 0
, all
t\in[T_{n+1}^{\varepsilon}-T_{1}^{\varepsilon},T_{n+2}^{\varepsilon}-T_{1}^{\varepsilon})
, it holds that

Y^{\varepsilon}_{t}=Y^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n+1}^{\varepsilon}-T_{1}^{\varepsilon}}=Z^{\varepsilon}_{S^{\varepsilon}_{n}}=Z^{\varepsilon}_{\rho^{\varepsilon}_{t}},

because by definition of
M^{\varepsilon}_{t}
, we have
M^{\varepsilon}_{t}=n
for all
t\in[T_{n+1}^{\varepsilon}-T_{1}^{\varepsilon},T_{n+2}^{\varepsilon}-T_{1}^{\varepsilon})
.

Step 4.
Here we prove that for all
T>0
,
\sup_{[0,T]}|\varepsilon M^{\varepsilon}_{t}-t|\to 0
in probability.

Recall that
M^{\varepsilon}_{t}=\sum_{n\geq 1}{\bm{1}}_{\{T_{n+1}^{\varepsilon}-T_{1}^{\varepsilon}\leq t\}}
and that
N^{\varepsilon}_{t}=\sum_{n\geq 1}{\bm{1}}_{\{T_{n}^{\varepsilon}\leq t\}}
.
For all
t\geq 0
, we have
M^{\varepsilon}_{t}=N^{\varepsilon}_{T_{1}^{\varepsilon}+t}-1
, so that

\sup_{[0,T]}|\varepsilon M^{\varepsilon}_{t}-t|\leq\varepsilon+\sup_{[0,T]}|\varepsilon N^{\varepsilon}_{t+T^{\varepsilon}_{1}}-t|\leq\varepsilon+\sup_{[0,T+T_{1}^{\varepsilon}]}|\varepsilon N^{\varepsilon}_{t}-t|+T_{1}^{\varepsilon}.

Since
T_{1}^{\varepsilon}\to 0
in probability (see Step 2) and

\sup_{[0,T+1]}|\varepsilon N^{\varepsilon}_{t}-t|\to 0
in probability by Lemma
56
, the conclusion follows.

Step 5.
By Step 2, it suffices that
(Y^{\varepsilon}_{t})_{t\geq 0}\to\mathbb{Q}_{x}
in law for the
{\bm{\mathrm{J}}_{1}}
-topology.
Since
Y^{\varepsilon}_{t}=Z^{\varepsilon}_{\rho^{\varepsilon}_{t}}
by Step 3, it is enough, by Lemma
85
,
to show that (i)
(Z^{\varepsilon}_{t})_{t\geq 0}\to\mathbb{Q}_{x}
in law for the
{\bm{\mathrm{J}}_{1}}
-topology and that (ii)
for all
T>0
,
\sup_{[0,T]}|\rho^{\varepsilon}_{t}-t|\to 0
in probability.

First, (i) follows from Proposition
55
, because
(Z^{\varepsilon})_{t\geq 0}
is a solution
to (
75
) starting from
\bm{X}^{\varepsilon}_{T_{1}^{\varepsilon}}
and since
\bm{X}^{\varepsilon}_{T_{1}^{\varepsilon}}=\bar{\bm{X}}^{\varepsilon}_{T_{1}^{\varepsilon}}\to x
in probability (as seen in Step 2).

Next, we write
\sup_{[0,T]}|\rho^{\varepsilon}_{t}-t|=A^{1}_{T,\varepsilon}+A^{2}_{T,\varepsilon}
, where

A^{1}_{T,\varepsilon}=\sup_{[0,T]}|S^{\varepsilon}_{M^{\varepsilon}_{t}}-\varepsilon M^{\varepsilon}_{t}|\quad\text{and}\quad A^{2}_{T,\varepsilon}=\sup_{[0,T]}|\varepsilon M^{\varepsilon}_{t}-t|.

We know from Step 4 that
A^{2}_{T,\varepsilon}\to 0
in probability. For
\eta>0

\mathbb{P}(A^{1}_{T,\varepsilon}>\eta)\leq\mathbb{P}\Big(M^{\varepsilon}_{T}>\frac{2T}{\varepsilon}\Big)+\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{k=0,\dots,\lfloor 2T/\varepsilon\rfloor}|S^{\varepsilon}_{k}-\varepsilon k|>\eta\Big)\leq\mathbb{P}\Big(M^{\varepsilon}_{T}>\frac{2T}{\varepsilon}\Big)+\frac{4\mathrm{Var}(S^{\varepsilon}_{\lfloor 2T/\varepsilon\rfloor})}{\eta^{2}}

by Doob’s
L^{2}
inequality, since
(S^{\varepsilon}_{k}-\varepsilon k)_{k\geq 0}
is a martingale. Since
Var
(S^{\varepsilon}_{\lfloor 2T/\varepsilon\rfloor})=\varepsilon^{2}\lfloor 2T/\varepsilon\rfloor\to 0

and since
\mathbb{P}(M^{\varepsilon}_{T}>2T/\varepsilon)\leq\mathbb{P}(|\varepsilon M^{\varepsilon}_{T}-T|>T)\to 0
by Step 4, we have shown (ii).
∎

9
Convergence of the Markov scattering process

The goal of this section is to prove Proposition
55
, assuming a few results,
see Proposition
59
, that will be proved in Section
10
.

9.1
Excursions outside the boundary

We consider the solution
(R_{t}^{\varepsilon})_{t\geq 0}
to (
75
), starting from some
x\in\partial\mathcal{D}
.
We show that this process
shares the same structure as
(R_{t})_{t\geq 0}
,
i.e.
it can be built by concatenating, translating,
rotating and stopping
excursions outside the half-space, in a way resembling Definition
4
.

Notation 57
.

Grant Assumptions
1
and
18
, fix
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
and
c_{\varepsilon}>0
to be chosen later.
Recall that
\mathrm{F}_{\varepsilon}
and
\mathrm{G}_{\varepsilon}
were introduced
in Notation
52
. Pick
O_{\varepsilon}\sim\mathrm{G}_{\varepsilon}(v)\mathrm{d}v
, independent of a Poisson measure

\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
with intensity
\varepsilon^{-1}\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{F}_{\varepsilon}(u)\mathrm{d}u
. Set

Y^{\varepsilon}_{t}=O_{\varepsilon}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}u)\quad\text{and}\quad\ell(Y^{\varepsilon})=\inf\{t>0:Y^{\varepsilon}_{t}\notin\mathbb{H}\}.

We denote by

\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}=\frac{c_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon}\rm{Law}((Y^{\varepsilon}_{t\land\ell(Y^{\varepsilon})})_{t\geq 0})
,
which is a measure on
\mathcal{E}
with mass
\frac{c_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon}
.

Recall that
h
,
g
,
\bar{\ell}
and the excursion measures
\mathfrak{n}_{*}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{\beta}

were introduced in Subsection
2.1
.

Lemma 58
.

Grant Assumptions
1
and
18
, fix
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
and
c_{\varepsilon}>0
. Consider
x\in\partial\mathcal{D}
, as well as
a measurable family
(A_{y})_{y\in\partial\mathcal{D}}
such that
A_{y}\in\mathcal{I}_{y}
for each
y\in\partial\mathcal{D}
,
and a Poisson measure
\Pi^{\varepsilon}=\sum_{u\in\mathrm{J}_{\varepsilon}}\delta_{(u,e^{\varepsilon}_{u})}

on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{E}
with intensity
\mathrm{d}u\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{d}e)
. The following equations
have pathwise-unique solutions

\displaystyle b_{u}^{\varepsilon}=x+\int_{0}^{u}\int_{\mathcal{E}}[g_{b_{v-}^{\varepsilon}}(A_{b_{s-}^{\varepsilon}},e)-b_{v-}^{\varepsilon}]\Pi_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{d}v,\mathrm{d}e),

(76)

\displaystyle\tau_{u}^{\varepsilon}=c_{\varepsilon}u+\int_{0}^{u}\int_{\mathcal{E}}\bar{\ell}_{b_{v-}^{\varepsilon}}(A_{b_{s-}^{\varepsilon}},e)\Pi_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{d}v,\mathrm{d}e),

(77)

and
(b^{\varepsilon}_{u})_{u\geq 0}
is valued in
\partial\mathcal{D}
, while
(\tau_{u}^{\varepsilon})_{u\geq 0}
is increasing, valued in
\mathbb{R}_{+}
and

\lim_{u\to\infty}\tau^{\varepsilon}_{u}=\infty
.
Let
(L_{t}^{\varepsilon}=\inf\{u\geq 0:\tau^{\varepsilon}_{u}>t\})_{t\geq 0}
and set

R_{t}^{\varepsilon}=\begin{cases}h_{b^{\varepsilon}_{L^{\varepsilon}_{t}-}}(A_{b^{\varepsilon}_{L^{\varepsilon}_{t}-}},e^{\varepsilon}_{L_{t}^{\varepsilon}}(t-\tau^{\varepsilon}_{L_{t}^{\varepsilon}-}))&\text{if }\tau^{\varepsilon}_{L_{t}^{\varepsilon}}>t,\\
g_{b_{L_{t}^{\varepsilon}-}^{\varepsilon}}(A_{b^{\varepsilon}_{L^{\varepsilon}_{t}-}},e^{\varepsilon}_{L_{t}^{\varepsilon}})&\text{if }L^{\varepsilon}_{t}\in\mathrm{J}_{\varepsilon}\text{ and }\tau^{\varepsilon}_{L_{t}^{\varepsilon}}=t,\\
b_{L_{t}^{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}&\text{if }L^{\varepsilon}_{t}\notin\mathrm{J}_{\varepsilon}.\end{cases}

(78)

Then
(R^{\varepsilon}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
has the same law as the process built in Remark
54

(issued from
x\in\partial\mathcal{D}
).

Existence and pathwise uniqueness for (
76
)-(
77
) are straightforward, since

\Pi_{\varepsilon}
is finite of
[0,T]\times\mathcal{E}
for all
T>0
.
We clearly have
\lim_{u\to\infty}\tau^{\varepsilon}_{u}=\infty

(because
\tau^{\varepsilon}_{u}\geq c_{\varepsilon}u
), so that
(L^{\varepsilon}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is also well-defined. The process

(R^{\varepsilon}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
introduced in (
78
) is thus uniquely defined.

Proof.

We fix
\varepsilon>0
and consider the process
(R^{\varepsilon}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
starting at
x\in\partial\mathcal{D}

built in Remark
54
with some Poisson
measure
\mathrm{M}_{\varepsilon}=\sum_{s\in\mathrm{I}_{\varepsilon}}\delta_{(s,v_{s},w_{s})}

on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{H}
with intensity
\varepsilon^{-1}\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{H}_{\varepsilon}(v,w)\mathrm{d}v\mathrm{d}w
:

R^{\varepsilon}_{t}=x+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{H}}[\Lambda(R_{s-}^{\varepsilon},R_{s-}^{\varepsilon}+v{\bm{1}}_{\{R_{s-}^{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{D}\}}+A_{R_{s-}^{\varepsilon}}w{\bm{1}}_{\{R_{s-}^{\varepsilon}\in\partial\mathcal{D}\}})-R_{s-}^{\varepsilon}]\mathrm{M}_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}v,\mathrm{d}w).

(79)

Our goal is to
build a Poisson measure
\Pi_{\varepsilon}
as in the statement such that
(R^{\varepsilon}_{t})_{t\geq 0}

satisfies (
78
). Let
(\mathcal{F}^{\varepsilon}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be the canonical filtration
of
\mathrm{M}_{\varepsilon}
.

Step 1.
We first build the (extended) excursions
(\bar{e}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
of
(R^{\varepsilon}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
outside
\partial\mathcal{D}
:
we introduce the sequences of
stopping times
(\sigma_{n})_{n\geq 0}
and
(\rho_{n})_{n\geq 1}
defined by
\sigma_{0}=0

and, for any
n\geq 1
,

\rho_{n}=\inf\{t\geq\sigma_{n-1},\>\mathrm{M}_{\varepsilon}([\sigma_{n-1},t]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{H})>0\}\quad\text{and}\quad\sigma_{n}=\inf\{t\geq\rho_{n},\>R^{\varepsilon}_{t}\in\partial\mathcal{D}\}.

We may have
\sigma_{n}=\rho_{n}
if
R^{\varepsilon}
jumps from
\partial\mathcal{D}
to
\partial\mathcal{D}
at time
\rho_{n}

(
i.e.
if
R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}+A_{R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}}w_{\rho_{n}}\notin\mathcal{D}
).
We define
(\bar{e}_{n}(t))_{t\in[0,\sigma_{n}-\rho_{n}]}
for each
n\geq 1
by setting, for all

t\in[0,\sigma_{n}-\rho_{n}]
,

\bar{e}_{n}(t)=R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}+A_{R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}}w_{\rho_{n}}+\int_{(\rho_{n},\rho_{n}+t]}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{H}}v\mathrm{M}_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}v,\mathrm{d}w).

We now check that for all
n\geq 1
,

(i) if
\sigma_{n}=\rho_{n}
, then
R^{\varepsilon}_{\rho_{n}}=\Lambda(R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}},\bar{e}_{n}(0))
and

\bar{e}_{n}(0)\notin\mathcal{D}
,

(ii) if
\sigma_{n}>\rho_{n}
, then
R^{\varepsilon}_{t+\rho_{n}}=\bar{e}_{n}(t)
for
t\in[0,\sigma_{n}-\rho_{n})
,
while
\bar{e}_{n}(\sigma_{n}-\rho_{n})\notin\mathcal{D}

and
R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n}}=\Lambda(\bar{e}_{n}((\sigma_{n}-\rho_{n})-),\bar{e}_{n}(\sigma_{n}-\rho_{n}))
.

For (i), we have
R^{\varepsilon}_{\rho_{n}}=\Lambda(R^{\varepsilon}_{\rho_{n}-},R^{\varepsilon}_{\rho_{n}-}+A_{R^{\varepsilon}_{\rho_{n}-}}w_{\rho_{n}})
by (
79
) and since

R^{\varepsilon}_{\rho_{n}-}=R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}\in\partial\mathcal{D}
. Thus

R^{\varepsilon}_{\rho_{n}}=\Lambda(R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}},R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}+A_{R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}}w_{\rho_{n}})=\Lambda(R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}},\bar{e}_{n}(0))
.
We have
\bar{e}_{n}(0)\notin\mathcal{D}
, because else we would have
R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n}}=R^{\varepsilon}_{\rho_{n}}=\bar{e}_{n}(0)\in\mathcal{D}
.

For (ii), we have
R^{\varepsilon}_{\rho_{n}}=R^{\varepsilon}_{\rho_{n}-}+A_{R^{\varepsilon}_{\rho_{n}-}}w_{\rho_{n}}
by (
79
) and since

R^{\varepsilon}_{\rho_{n}-}=R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}\in\partial\mathcal{D}
and
R^{\varepsilon}_{\rho_{n}}\in\mathcal{D}
(because
\sigma_{n}>\rho_{n}
). Thus

R^{\varepsilon}_{\rho_{n}}=R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}+A_{R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}}w_{\rho_{n}}=\bar{e}_{n}(0)
. Next, by definition of
\sigma_{n}
, we have

R^{\varepsilon}_{s-},R^{\varepsilon}_{s}\in\mathcal{D}
for all

s\in\mathrm{I}_{\varepsilon}\cap(\rho_{n},\sigma_{n})
, so that

\Lambda(R_{s-}^{\varepsilon},R_{s-}^{\varepsilon}+v_{s}{\bm{1}}_{\{R_{s-}^{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{D}\}}+A_{R_{s-}^{\varepsilon}}w_{s}{\bm{1}}_{\{R_{s-}^{\varepsilon}\in\partial\mathcal{D}\}})-R_{s-}^{\varepsilon}=\Lambda(R_{s-}^{\varepsilon},R_{s-}^{\varepsilon}+v_{s})-R^{\varepsilon}_{s-}=v_{s},

and thus for
t\in(0,\sigma_{n}-\rho_{n})
, recalling (
79
),

R^{\varepsilon}_{\rho_{n}+t}=R^{\varepsilon}_{\rho_{n}}+\int_{(\rho_{n},\rho_{n}+t]}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{H}}v\mathrm{M}_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}v,\mathrm{d}w)=\bar{e}_{n}(t).

Finally,
R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n}}=\Lambda(R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n}-},R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n}-}+v_{\sigma_{n}})
because
R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n}-}\in\mathcal{D}
.
But
R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n}-}=\bar{e}_{n}((\sigma_{n}-\rho_{n})-)
from the above discussion, while

R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n}-}+v_{\sigma_{n}}=\bar{e}_{n}((\sigma_{n}-\rho_{n})-)+v_{\sigma_{n}}=\bar{e}_{n}(\sigma_{n}-\rho_{n})

by definition of
\bar{e}_{n}
. Thus
R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n}}=\Lambda(\bar{e}_{n}((\sigma_{n}-\rho_{n})-),\bar{e}_{n}(\sigma_{n}-\rho_{n}))
.
Notice that
\bar{e}_{n}(\sigma_{n}-\rho_{n})\notin\mathcal{D}
because else, we would have

R_{\sigma_{n}}=\bar{e}_{n}(\sigma_{n}-\rho_{n})\in\mathcal{D}
.

Step 2.
We now rotate/translate/complete the excursions
\bar{e}_{n}
to get some i.i.d. excursions
outside the half-space. We recall that the marginals of
\mathrm{H}_{\varepsilon}
are
\mathrm{F}_{\varepsilon}
and
\mathrm{G}_{\varepsilon}
, see Notation
52
.

We consider an i.i.d. sequence of Poisson measures

(\mathrm{K}_{n})_{n\geq 0}
on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
with intensity
\varepsilon^{-1}\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{F}_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{d}z)
,
independent of everything else. We define
\mathcal{G}_{n}=\sigma(\mathrm{K}_{n})
and, for all
n\geq 1
, all
t\geq 0
,

Y^{n}_{t}=w_{\rho_{n}}+\int_{(\rho_{n},(\rho_{n}+t)\land\sigma_{n}]}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{H}}A_{R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}}^{-1}v\mathrm{M}_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}v,\mathrm{d}w)+{\bm{1}}_{\{t>\sigma_{n}-\rho_{n}\}}\int_{0}^{t-\sigma_{n}}v\mathrm{K}_{n}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}v).

Since
w_{\rho_{n}}\sim\mathrm{G}_{\varepsilon}
by definition of
\rho_{n}
and
since
\mathrm{F}_{\varepsilon}
is rotationally invariant, the process
(Y^{n}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
has
the same law
\mu_{\varepsilon}
as
(Y^{\varepsilon}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
introduced in Notation
57
.
Moreover,
(Y^{n}_{t})_{t\geq 0}

is
\mathcal{F}_{\sigma_{n}}\lor\mathcal{G}_{n}
-measurable and independent of
\mathcal{F}_{\rho_{n}-}
.

We introduce
\ell(Y^{n})=\inf\{t>0:Y^{n}_{t}\notin\mathbb{H}\}
. It holds that
\ell(Y^{n})\geq\sigma_{n}-\rho_{n}
,
because for all
t\in[0,\sigma_{n}-\rho_{n})
,
Y^{n}_{t}=A_{R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}}^{-1}(\bar{e}_{n}(t)-R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}})

with
\bar{e}_{n}(t)=R^{\varepsilon}_{\rho_{n}+t}\in\mathcal{D}
by Step 1-(ii) and because for all
y\in\partial\mathcal{D}
, all
z\in\mathcal{D}
,

A_{y}^{-1}(z-y)\in\mathbb{H}
by convexity of
\mathcal{D}
.

We introduce
e_{n}(t)=Y^{n}_{t\land\ell(Y^{n})}
. Again,
(e_{n}(t))_{t\geq 0}

is
\mathcal{F}_{\sigma_{n}}\lor\mathcal{G}_{n}
-measurable and independent of
\mathcal{F}_{\rho_{n}-}
(and thus of
\mathcal{F}_{\sigma_{n-1}}
)
and has the same law
\nu_{\varepsilon}

as
(Y^{\varepsilon}_{t\land\ell(Y^{\varepsilon})})_{t\geq 0}
introduced in Notation
57
.

For each
n\geq 0
, by definition of
\sigma_{n-1}
and of
\rho_{n}
and since
\mathrm{M}_{\varepsilon}
is Poisson,

\rho_{n}-\sigma_{n-1}
is Exp
(\varepsilon^{-1})
-distributed,
\mathcal{F}_{\rho_{n}-}
-measurable (and thus
\mathcal{F}_{\sigma_{n}}
-measurable) and
independent of
\mathcal{F}_{\sigma_{n-1}}
.

For each
n\geq 2
,
((e_{n}(t))_{t\geq 0},\rho_{n}-\sigma_{n-1})
is independent of
\mathcal{F}_{\sigma_{n-1}}
and
thus of the vector
((e_{1}(t))_{t\geq 0},\rho_{1}-\sigma_{0},\ldots,(e_{n-1}(t))_{t\geq 0},\rho_{n-1}-\sigma_{n-2})
.
Furthermore,

(e_{n}(t))_{t\geq 0}
is independent of
\mathcal{F}_{\rho_{n}-}
and thus of
\rho_{n}-\sigma_{n-1}
.

All in all, the whole family
((e_{n}(t))_{t\geq 0},\rho_{n}-\sigma_{n-1})_{n\geq 1}

is independent and for each
n\geq 1
,
(e_{n}(t))_{t\geq 0}\sim\nu_{\varepsilon}

and
\rho_{n}-\sigma_{n-1}\sim\text{Exp}(\varepsilon^{-1})
.

Step 3.
We set
T_{0}=0
and, for
n\geq 1
,

T_{n}=c_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\sum_{k=1}^{n}(\rho_{k}-\sigma_{k-1})
. By Step 2 and since e.g.

T_{1}=c_{\varepsilon}^{-1}(\rho_{1}-\sigma_{0})\sim\text{Exp}(\frac{c_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon})
, the measure

\Pi_{\varepsilon}=\sum_{n\geq 1}\delta_{(T_{n},e_{n})}

is Poisson on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathcal{E}
with intensity
\mathrm{d}u\mathfrak{n}_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{d}e)

(because
\mathfrak{n}_{\varepsilon}=\frac{c_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon}\nu_{\varepsilon}
, see Notation
57
), with
set of jump times
\mathrm{J}_{\varepsilon}=\{T_{n}:n\geq 1\}
.
Note that we may write
\Pi_{\varepsilon}=\sum_{s\in\mathrm{J}_{\varepsilon}}\delta_{(s,e^{\varepsilon}_{s})}
as in the statement by setting

e^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n}}=e_{n}
for each
n\geq 1
.

For all
n\geq 1
,
\bar{e}_{n}(t)=R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}+A_{R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}}e_{n}(t)
for all
t\in[0,\sigma_{n}-\rho_{n}]
:
since
\ell(Y^{n})\geq\sigma_{n}-\rho_{n}
,

\displaystyle R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}+A_{R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}}e_{n}(t)=

\displaystyle R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}+A_{R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}}Y^{n}_{t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}+A_{R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}}w_{\rho_{n}}+\int_{(\rho_{n},\rho_{n}+t]}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{H}}v\mathrm{M}_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}v,\mathrm{d}w)=\bar{e}_{n}(t).

Step 4.
We now check that for all
n\geq 1
, we have

(i)
g_{R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}}(A_{R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}},e_{n})=R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n}}
,

(ii)
\bar{\ell}_{R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}}(A_{R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}},e_{n})=\sigma_{n}-\rho_{n}
,

(iii)
R^{\varepsilon}_{t}=h_{R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}}(A_{R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}},e_{n}(t-\rho_{n}))
for all
t\in[\rho_{n},\sigma_{n})

if
\sigma_{n}>\rho_{n}
.

We start with (iii), writing, for
t\in[\rho_{n},\sigma_{n})
,

\displaystyle h_{R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}}(A_{R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}},e_{n}(t-\rho_{n}))=R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}+A_{R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}}e_{n}(t-\rho_{n})=\bar{e}_{n}(t-\rho_{n})=R^{\varepsilon}_{t}.

We used the definition of
h
, the link between
e_{n}
and
\bar{e}_{n}
(see Step 3) and Step 1-(ii).

We next check (ii) when
\sigma_{n}>\rho_{n}
. In this case,
R^{\varepsilon}_{t}\in\mathcal{D}
for all
t\in[\rho_{n},\sigma_{n})

by definition of
\rho_{n}
and
\sigma_{n}
, so that
(iii) implies that
\bar{\ell}_{R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}}(A_{R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}},e_{n})\geq\sigma_{n}-\rho_{n}
, and it remains to verify
that
h_{R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}}(A_{R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}},e_{n}(\sigma_{n}-\rho_{n}))\notin\mathcal{D}
. By definition of
h
and
by the link between
e_{n}
and
\bar{e}_{n}
, we have

h_{R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}}(A_{R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}},e_{n}(\sigma_{n}-\rho_{n}))=\bar{e}_{n}(\sigma_{n}-\rho_{n})\notin\mathcal{D}
by see Step 1-(ii).

We now prove (i) when
\sigma_{n}>\rho_{n}
: it follows from the definition of
g
and point (ii) that

\displaystyle g_{R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}}(A_{R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}},e_{n})=

\displaystyle\Lambda(h_{R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}}(A_{R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}},e_{n}((\sigma_{n}-\rho_{n})-),h_{R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}}(A_{R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}},e_{n}(\sigma_{n}-\rho_{n})))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Lambda(\bar{e}_{n}((\sigma_{n}-\rho_{n})-),\bar{e}_{n}(\sigma_{n}-\rho_{n})),

which equals
R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n}}
by Step 1-(ii).

We finally check (i) and (ii) when
\sigma_{n}=\rho_{n}
. We have
\bar{\ell}_{R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}}(A_{R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}},e_{n})=0
, because

h_{R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}}(A_{R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}},e_{n}(0))=\bar{e}_{n}(0)\notin\mathcal{D}
, see Step 1-(i).
This also implies that
g_{R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}}(A_{R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}}},e_{n})=\Lambda(R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n-1}},\bar{e}_{n}(0))
,
which equals
R^{\varepsilon}_{\rho_{n}}
by Step 1-(i).

Step 5.
We consider
(b^{\varepsilon}_{u})_{u\geq 0}
,
(\tau^{\varepsilon}_{u})_{u\geq 0}
,
(L^{\varepsilon}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
as in the statement, defined
with
\Pi_{\varepsilon}
introduced in Step 3, and we show that
(R^{\varepsilon}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
satisfies (
78
).

Step 5.1.
We first check by induction that for all
n\geq 0
, we have (convention:
\sum_{1}^{0}=0
)

\text{for all}\;u\in[T_{n},T_{n+1}),\quad b^{\varepsilon}_{u}=R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n}}\quad\text{and}\quad\tau^{\varepsilon}_{u}=c_{\varepsilon}u+\sum_{k=1}^{n}(\sigma_{k}-\rho_{k}).

(80)

Recalling (
76
)-(
77
),
we have
b^{\varepsilon}_{u}=x=R^{\varepsilon}_{0}=R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{0}}
and
\tau^{\varepsilon}_{u}=c_{\varepsilon}u
for all
u\in[0,T_{1})
,
which shows (
80
) when
n=0
.
If now (
80
) holds true for some
n\geq 0
, then by (
76
), for all

u\in[T_{n+1},T_{n+2})
,

b^{\varepsilon}_{u}=b^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n+1}}=g_{b^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n}}}(A_{b^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n}}},e_{n+1})=g_{R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n}}}(A_{R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n}}},e_{n+1})=R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n+1}}

by the induction hypothesis and Step 4. Moreover, by (
77
), for all
u\in[T_{n+1},T_{n+2})
,

\tau^{\varepsilon}_{u}=\tau^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n+1}}+c_{\varepsilon}(u-T_{n+1})=\tau^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n+1}-}+\bar{\ell}_{b^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n}}}(A_{b^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n}}},e_{n+1})+c_{\varepsilon}(u-T_{n+1}).

By the induction hypothesis and since
\bar{\ell}_{b^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n}}}(A_{b^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n}}},e_{n+1})=\bar{\ell}_{R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n}}}(A_{R^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{n}}},e_{n+1})=\sigma_{n+1}-\rho_{n+1}
by Step 4, this gives

\tau^{\varepsilon}_{u}=c_{\varepsilon}T_{n+1}+\sum_{k=1}^{n}(\sigma_{k}-\rho_{k})+(\sigma_{n+1}-\rho_{n+1})+c_{\varepsilon}(u-T_{n+1})=c_{\varepsilon}u+\sum_{k=1}^{n+1}(\sigma_{k}-\rho_{k}).

Step 5.2.
We now check, using (
80
) and recalling that

T_{n}=c_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\sum_{k=1}^{n}(\rho_{k}-\sigma_{k-1})
and that

(L_{t}^{\varepsilon})_{t\geq 0}
is the right-continuous inverse of
