(Y^{\varepsilon,x}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
was introduced in
Subsection
10.1
. There is a constant
C
such that for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
, all
x\in\mathbb{H}
, all
t>0
,
all
y>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\ell(Y^{\varepsilon,x})>t)\leq C(\varepsilon^{1/2}+x_{1}^{\alpha/2})t^{-1/2},

(113)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(M(Y^{\varepsilon,x})>y)\leq C(\varepsilon^{1/2}+|x|^{\alpha/2})y^{-\alpha/2}.

(114)

For all
x\in\mathbb{H}
,

\mathbb{P}(\ell(Y^{1,x})>t)\sim\mathcal{V}(x_{1})t^{-1/2}\quad\text{as $t\to\infty$.}

(115)

Proof.

By (
104
), it suffices to show (
113
)-(
114
) when
\varepsilon=1
.
But we can write
Y^{1,x}_{t}=x+\mathbf{S}_{N_{t}}
, where
(\mathbf{S}_{n})_{n\geq 0}
is as in Lemma
66

and is independent of a Poisson process
(N_{t})_{t\geq 0}
of parameter
1
. We also observe that

\{\ell(Y^{1,x})>t\}=\{\check{\ell}(x+\mathbf{S})>N_{t}\}
and that
M(Y^{1,x})=\check{M}(x+\mathbf{S})
.
Thus (
114
) (with
\varepsilon=1
) immediately follows from (
111
), while (
110
)
tells us that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\ell(Y^{1,x})>t)\leq C(1+x_{1}^{\alpha/2})\mathbb{E}\big[(1+N_{t})^{-1/2}\big]\leq C(1+x_{1}^{\alpha/2})t^{-1/2},

because
\mathbb{E}[(1+N_{t})^{-1/2}]\leq\mathbb{E}[(1+N_{t})^{-1}]^{1/2}=[e^{-t}\sum_{k\geq 0}\frac{t^{k}}{(k+1)!}]^{1/2}=[\frac{1-e^{-t}}{t}]^{1/2}\leq\frac{1}{t^{1/2}}
.
This proves (
113
) (when
\varepsilon=1
). Finally, for any
a\in(0,1)
,

\mathbb{P}(\check{\ell}(x+\mathbf{S})>t(1+a),\>|N_{t}/t-1|\leq a)\leq\mathbb{P}(\ell(Y^{1,x})>t,\>|N_{t}/t-1|\leq a)\leq\mathbb{P}(\check{\ell}(x+\mathbf{S})>t(1-a)).

By Bienaymé-Tchebychev’s inequality, it holds that
\mathbb{P}(|N_{t}/t-1|>a)\leq a^{-2}t^{-1}
, from which

t^{1/2}\mathbb{P}(|N_{t}/t-1|>a)\to 0
as
t\to\infty
.
We thus get from (
109
) that

\mathcal{V}(x_{1})(1+a)^{-1/2}\leq\liminf_{t\to\infty}t^{1/2}\mathbb{P}(\ell(Y^{1,x})>t)\leq\limsup_{t\to\infty}t^{1/2}\mathbb{P}(\ell(Y^{1,x})>t)\leq\mathcal{V}(x_{1})(1-a)^{-1/2}.

Letting
a\to 0
completes the proof of (
115
).
∎

10.3
Convergence of the excursion measure under Assumption
22
-(b)

We start with an easy lemma. Recall that
\ell_{r}(w)=\inf\{t>0:w(t)\notin B_{d}(r\mathbf{e}_{1},r)\}
.

Lemma 69
.

Grant Assumption
18
with
\kappa_{\mathrm{F}}=1/\Gamma(\alpha+1)

and recall that for
x\in\mathbb{H}
, the process
(Y^{\varepsilon,x}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
was introduced in Subsection
10.1
.
Let
(Z_{t}^{x})_{t\geq 0}
be an ISP
α,x
.

(a) We have the following convergence in law in
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R}^{d})

endowed with
\bm{\mathrm{J}}_{1}
-topology:

\big(Y_{t\wedge\ell(Y^{\varepsilon,x})}^{\varepsilon,x}\big)_{t\geq 0}\longrightarrow\big(Z_{t\wedge\ell(Z^{x})}^{x}\big)_{t\geq 0}\quad\text{as }\varepsilon\to 0

(b) For any
r>0
,
\ell_{r}(Y^{\varepsilon,x})
converges in law to
\ell_{r}(Z^{x})
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

Proof.

As in Step 3 of the proof of Lemma
65
, it holds that
that
(Y^{\varepsilon,x}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
converges in law to
(Z_{t}^{x})_{t\geq 0}
for the local uniform topology.
By Skorokhod’s representation theorem, we may assume that the convergence holds a.s.
From (
107
), it holds that a.s.,
\inf_{t\in[0,\ell(Z^{x}))}d(Z_{t}^{x},\mathbb{H}^{c})>0

and
Z^{x}_{\ell(Z^{x})}\notin{}\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0mu\mathbb{H}}
. Thus for
\varepsilon
small enough, we have

\inf_{t\in[0,\ell(Z^{x}))}d(Y_{t}^{\varepsilon,x},\mathbb{H}^{c})>0
and
Y^{\varepsilon,x}_{\ell(Z^{x})}\notin{}\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0mu\mathbb{H}}
, implying that

\ell(Y^{\varepsilon,x})=\ell(Z^{x})
. Point (a) follows. The proof of (b) is similar.
∎

We can now give the

Proof of Proposition
59
under Assumption
22
-(b).

We recall that

\chi_{\mathrm{G}}=1/(2\kappa_{\mathrm{G}}\Gamma(\beta+1))
with

\kappa_{\mathrm{G}}
introduced in Assumption
22
-(b).

Step 1.
Here we prove that

\mathrm{G}_{1}(x)\sim 2\Gamma(\beta+1)\kappa_{\mathrm{G}}|x|^{-d-\beta}\quad\text{as }|x|\to\infty

(116)

and that there is
C>0
such that for all
x\in\mathbb{H}
,

\mathrm{G}_{1}(x)\leq C(|x|^{1-d}\wedge|x|^{-\beta-d}).

(117)

We recall from Notation
52
that
\mathrm{G}_{1}
is the law of
EW
where
E\sim\mathrm{Exp}(1)
and

W\sim\mathrm{G}_{+}(v)\mathrm{d}v=2\mathrm{G}(v){\bm{1}}_{\{v\in\mathbb{H}\}}\mathrm{d}v
. An easy computation shows that for any
x\in\mathbb{H}
,

\mathrm{G}_{1}(x)=2\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{-d}e^{-t}\mathrm{G}(x/t)\mathrm{d}t.

Recalling Assumption
22
-(b),
\mathrm{G}(x/t)\sim\kappa_{\mathrm{G}}t^{\beta+d}|x|^{-\beta-d}

as
|x|\to\infty
, for any
t>0
, and there is a constant
C
such

\mathrm{G}(x/t)\leq Ct^{\beta+d}|x|^{-\beta-d}
for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, any
t>0
.
Then (
116
) follows by dominated convergence, and we also have

\mathrm{G}_{1}(x)\leq C|x|^{-d-\beta}
. Assumption
22
-(b) also requires that

G(v)\leq C(1+|v|)^{-\beta-d}\leq C(1\land|v|^{-\beta-d})
, so that

\mathrm{G}(x/t)\leq C(1\land|x|^{-\beta-d}t^{\beta+d})
and thus

\mathrm{G}_{1}(x)\leq C|x|^{-\beta-d}\int_{0}^{|x|}t^{\beta}e^{-t}\mathrm{d}t+C\int_{|x|}^{\infty}t^{-d}e^{-t}\mathrm{d}t\leq C|x|^{-\beta-d}\int_{0}^{|x|}t^{\beta}\mathrm{d}t+C\int_{|x|}^{\infty}t^{-d}\mathrm{d}t=C|x|^{1-d}.

Step 2.
We fix
\delta>0
and
\phi:\mathcal{E}\to\mathbb{R}
bounded and continuous
for the
{\bm{\mathrm{J}}_{1}}
-topology and we prove (
87
). By (
105
) and since

\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon,\delta}(\mathrm{d}e)={\bm{1}}_{\{|e(0)|>\delta\}}\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{d}e)
, we have

\int_{\mathcal{E}}\phi(e)\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon,\delta}(\mathrm{d}e)=\chi_{\mathrm{G}}\varepsilon^{-\beta/\alpha-d/\alpha}\int_{\{|y|>\delta\}}\mathbb{E}\Big[\phi\big(\big(Y_{t\wedge\ell(Y^{\varepsilon,y})}^{\varepsilon,y}\big)_{t\geq 0}\big)\Big]\mathrm{G}_{1}(\varepsilon^{-1/\alpha}y)\mathrm{d}y.

(118)

By Lemma
69
, for each
y\in\mathbb{H}
,

\mathbb{E}\Big[\phi\big(\big(Y_{t\wedge\ell(Z^{\varepsilon,y})}^{\varepsilon,y}\big)_{t\geq 0}\big)\Big]\longrightarrow\mathbb{E}_{y}\Big[\phi\big(\big(Z_{t\wedge\ell(Z)}\big)_{t\geq 0}\big)\Big]\quad\text{as }\varepsilon\to 0.

Moreover, it follows from Step 1 that for any
y\in\mathbb{H}
,
\chi_{\mathrm{G}}\varepsilon^{-(\beta+d)/\alpha}\mathrm{G}_{1}(\varepsilon^{-1/\alpha}y)\to|y|^{-d-\beta}
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. We also get from (
117
) that

\varepsilon^{-(\beta+d)/\alpha}\mathrm{G}_{1}(\varepsilon^{-1/\alpha}y)\leq C|y|^{-d-\beta}
, which is integrable on
\{|y|>\delta\}
.
We thus can use the dominated convergence theorem to get

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\mathcal{E}}\phi(e)\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon,\delta}(\mathrm{d}e)=\int_{\{|y|>\delta\}}\mathbb{E}_{y}\Big[\phi\big(\big(Z_{t\wedge\ell(Z)}\big)_{t\geq 0}\big)\Big]|y|^{-d-\beta}\mathrm{d}y=\int_{\mathcal{E}}\phi(e)\mathfrak{n}_{\beta}^{\delta}(\mathrm{d}e),

recall that
\mathfrak{n}_{\beta}
was defined in (
8
) and that
\mathfrak{n}_{\beta}^{\delta}(\mathrm{d}e)={\bm{1}}_{\{|e(0)|>\delta\}}\mathfrak{n}_{\beta}(\mathrm{d}e)
, see (
83
).
Note that we have not yet used the fact that
\beta<\alpha/2
.

Step 3.
We next show (
84
): we have
\mathfrak{n}_{\beta}^{\delta}(\mathcal{E})=\int_{\{|y|>\delta\}}|y|^{-d-\beta}\mathrm{d}y<\infty
,
while using (
118
), we find

\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon,\delta}(\mathcal{E})=\chi_{\mathrm{G}}\varepsilon^{-(\beta+d)/\alpha}\int_{\{|y|>\delta\}}\mathrm{G}_{1}(\varepsilon^{-1/\alpha}y)\mathrm{d}y\leq C\int_{\{|y|>\delta\}}|y|^{-d-\beta}\mathrm{d}y\leq C
.

Step 4.
We now prove (
85
). Fix
\theta\in(0,\beta/\alpha)
.
We have to show that
I_{\varepsilon}
is bounded, where

\displaystyle I_{\varepsilon}:=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{E}}[M(e)\land 1+\ell(e)\land[\ell(e)]^{\theta}]\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{d}e)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\chi_{\mathrm{G}}\varepsilon^{-(\beta+d)/\alpha}\int_{\mathbb{H}}\mathbb{E}\Big[M(Y^{\varepsilon,y}\big))\land 1+\ell(Y^{\varepsilon,y})\land[\ell(Y^{\varepsilon,y})]^{\theta}\Big]\mathrm{G}_{1}(\varepsilon^{-1/\alpha}y)\mathrm{d}y.

by (
105
). We deduce from (
114
) that

\mathbb{E}\Big[M(Y^{\varepsilon,y})\land 1\Big]=\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{P}(M(Y^{\varepsilon,y})>z)\mathrm{d}z\leq C(\varepsilon^{1/2}+|y|^{\alpha/2})\land 1.

Consider a r.v.
L>0
satisfying
\mathbb{P}(L>t)\leq at^{-1/2}
for some
a>0
. If
a>1
,
since
\theta\in(0,1/2)
,

\mathbb{E}[L\land L^{\theta}]=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}(L\land L^{\theta}>t)\mathrm{d}t\leq a^{2\theta}+\int_{a^{2\theta}}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}(L>t^{1/\theta})\mathrm{d}t\leq a^{2\theta}+a\int_{a^{2\theta}}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{t^{1/(2\theta)}}=Ca^{2\theta}.

If
a\in(0,1]
, we have

\mathbb{E}[L\land L^{\theta}]\leq\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{P}(L>t)\mathrm{d}t+\int_{1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}(L>t^{1/\theta})\mathrm{d}t\leq\int_{0}^{1}\frac{a\mathrm{d}t}{t^{1/2}}+\int_{0}^{1}\frac{a\mathrm{d}t}{t^{1/(2\theta)}}=Ca.

Thus
\mathbb{E}[L\land L^{\theta}]\leq C(a\land a^{2\theta})
in any case. From this and (
113
), we conclude that

\mathbb{E}\Big[\ell(Z^{\varepsilon,y})\land[\ell(Z^{\varepsilon,y})]^{\theta}\Big]\leq C[(\varepsilon^{1/2}+|y|^{\alpha/2})\land(\varepsilon^{1/2}+|y|^{\alpha/2})^{2\theta}].

All in all (and since
a\land 1\leq a\land a^{2\theta}
for all
a>0
), we have proved that

\displaystyle I_{\varepsilon}\leq

\displaystyle C\varepsilon^{-(\beta+d)/\alpha}\int_{\mathbb{H}}[(\varepsilon^{1/2}+|y|^{\alpha/2})\land(\varepsilon^{1/2}+|y|^{\alpha/2})^{2\theta}]\mathrm{G}_{1}(\varepsilon^{-1/\alpha}y)\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\varepsilon^{-(\beta+d)/\alpha}\int_{\mathbb{H}}[(\varepsilon^{1/2}+|y|^{\alpha/2})\land(\varepsilon^{1/2}+|y|^{\alpha/2})^{2\theta}]\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{|\varepsilon^{-1/\alpha}y|^{d-1}\lor|\varepsilon^{-1/\alpha}y|^{\beta+d}}

(119)

by (
117
). We now write
I_{\varepsilon}\leq I_{\varepsilon,1}+I_{\varepsilon,2}+I_{\varepsilon,3}
, where
I_{\varepsilon,1}
(resp.
I_{\varepsilon,2}
,
I_{\varepsilon,3}
)
stands for the integral on
\{|y|\leq\varepsilon^{1/\alpha}\}
(resp.
\{\varepsilon^{1/\alpha}<|y|\leq 1\}
,
\{|y|>1\}
). First,

I_{\varepsilon,1}\leq\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{(\beta+d)/\alpha}}\int_{\{|y|\leq\varepsilon^{1/\alpha}\}}(\varepsilon^{1/2}+|y|^{\alpha/2})\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{\varepsilon^{-1/\alpha}|y|^{d-1}}\leq\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{(\beta+d)/\alpha}}\int_{\{|y|\leq\varepsilon^{1/\alpha}\}}\varepsilon^{1/2}\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{|\varepsilon^{-1/\alpha}y|^{d-1}}.

Since
\int_{\{|y|\leq\varepsilon^{1/\alpha}\}}\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{|y|^{d-1}}=C\varepsilon^{1/\alpha}
,
we find
I_{\varepsilon,1}\leq C\varepsilon^{1/2-\beta/\alpha}\leq C
.
Next,

I_{\varepsilon,2}\leq\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{(\beta+d)/\alpha}}\int_{\{\varepsilon^{1/\alpha}<|y|\leq 1\}}(\varepsilon^{1/2}+|y|^{\alpha/2})\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{|\varepsilon^{-1/\alpha}y|^{\beta+d}}\leq\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{(\beta+d)/\alpha}}\int_{\{\varepsilon^{1/\alpha}<|y|\leq 1\}}|y|^{\alpha/2}\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{|\varepsilon^{-1/\alpha}y|^{\beta+d}}.

Since
\int_{\{|y|\leq 1\}}|y|^{\alpha/2-\beta-d}\mathrm{d}y=C
(because
\alpha/2-\beta>0
),
we conclude that
I_{\varepsilon,2}\leq C
. Finally,

I_{\varepsilon,3}\leq\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{(\beta+d)/\alpha}}\int_{\{|y|\geq 1\}}(\varepsilon^{1/2}+|y|^{\alpha/2})^{2\theta}\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{|\varepsilon^{-1/\alpha}y|^{\beta+d}}\leq\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{(\beta+d)/\alpha}}\int_{\{|y|\geq 1\}}|y|^{\alpha\theta}\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{|\varepsilon^{-1/\alpha}y|^{\beta+d}}.

Since
\int_{\{|y|\geq 1\}}|y|^{\alpha\theta-\beta-d}\mathrm{d}y=C
(because
\alpha\theta-\beta<0
,
since
\theta<\beta/\alpha
), we have
I_{\varepsilon,3}\leq C
.

Step 5.
We next prove (
86
). Exactly as in Step 4 (with
\theta=0
), see (
119
), we find

\displaystyle J_{\varepsilon,\delta}:=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{E}}[M(e)\land 1+\ell(e)\land 1](\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}-\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon,\delta})(\mathrm{d}e)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{(\beta+d)/\alpha}}\int_{\{|y|\leq\delta\}}(\varepsilon^{1/2}+|y|^{\alpha/2})\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{|\varepsilon^{-1/\alpha}y|^{d-1}\wedge|\varepsilon^{-1/\alpha}y|^{\beta+d}}.

For
\varepsilon
small enough, we write
J_{\varepsilon,\delta}\leq J_{\varepsilon,1}+J_{\varepsilon,\delta,2}
, where
J_{\varepsilon,1}
(resp.
J_{\varepsilon,\delta,2}
)
stands for the integral on
\{|y|\leq\varepsilon^{1/\alpha}\}
(resp.
\{\varepsilon^{1/\alpha}<|y|\leq\delta\}
).
We have
J_{\varepsilon,1}=I_{\varepsilon,1}\leq C\varepsilon^{1/2-\beta/\alpha}
, so that
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}J_{\varepsilon,1}=0
. We also have

J_{\varepsilon,\delta,2}\leq\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{(\beta+d)/\alpha}}\int_{\{\varepsilon^{1/\alpha}<|y|\leq\delta\}}\!\!(\varepsilon^{1/2}+|y|^{\alpha/2})\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{|\varepsilon^{-1/\alpha}y|^{\beta+d}}\leq\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{(\beta+d)/\alpha}}\int_{\{\varepsilon^{1/\alpha}<|y|\leq\delta\}}\!\!|y|^{\alpha/2}\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{|\varepsilon^{-1/\alpha}y|^{\beta+d}}.

but
\int_{\{|y|\leq\delta\}}|y|^{\alpha/2-\beta-d}\mathrm{d}y=C\delta^{\alpha/2-\beta}
,
whence
J_{\varepsilon,\delta,2}\leq C\delta^{\alpha/2-\beta}
and
\lim_{\delta\to 0}\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}J_{\varepsilon,\delta,2}=0
.

Step 6.
We prove (
90
). For
\delta>0
and
a>0
, we write, since
z>a
implies

z\land 1\geq a\land 1
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\{e\in\mathcal{E}:|e(0)|\leq\delta,\ell(e)>a\})\leq

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}-\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon,\delta})(\{e\in\mathcal{E}:\ell(e)\land 1\geq a\land 1\})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{a\land 1}\int_{\mathcal{E}}(\ell(e)\land 1)(\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}-\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon,\delta})(\mathrm{d}e).

The result then follows from (
86
).

Step 7.
It only remains to check (
89
). By (
105
), we have

\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\ell_{r}>\eta)=\chi_{\mathrm{G}}\varepsilon^{-(\beta+d)/\alpha}\int_{\mathbb{H}}\mathbb{P}(\ell_{r}(Y^{\varepsilon,y})>\eta)\mathrm{G}_{1}(\varepsilon^{-1/\alpha}y)\mathrm{d}y.

Thanks to Lemma
69
, for any
y\in B_{d}(r\mathbf{e}_{1},r)
, it holds that

\ell_{r}(Y^{\varepsilon,y})
converges in law to
\ell_{r}(Z^{y})
where
(Z_{t}^{y})_{t\geq 0}
is an
\mathrm{ISP}_{\alpha,y}
.
Moreover,
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\chi_{\mathrm{G}}\varepsilon^{-(\beta+d)/\alpha}\mathrm{G}_{1}(\varepsilon^{-1/\alpha}y)=|y|^{-d-\beta}
by (
116
).
Combining Fatou’s lemma and Portmanteau’s theorem, we get

\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\ell_{r}(e)>\eta)\geq\int_{B_{d}(r\mathbf{e}_{1},r)}\mathbb{P}(\ell_{r}(Z^{y})>\eta)|y|^{-d-\beta}\mathrm{d}y.

Since
\mathbb{P}(\ell_{r}(Z^{y})>0)=1
for all
y\in B_{d}(r\mathbf{e}_{1},r)
, we get by monotone convergence

\liminf_{\eta\to 0}\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\ell_{r}(e)>\eta)\geq\int_{B_{d}(r\mathbf{e}_{1},r)}|y|^{-d-\beta}\mathrm{d}y=\infty.

∎

10.4
Convergence of the excursion measure under Assumption
22
-(a)

We are now able to specify the value of the constant
\chi_{\mathrm{G}}
in Proposition
59

when
\beta=*
.

Definition 70
.

Grant Assumption
18
and Assumption
22
-(a). Let
\mathcal{V}
be the
function from Lemma
66
. We define, recalling that
\mathrm{G}_{1}=\mathcal{L}(UW)

with
U\sim\mathrm{Exp}(1)
and
W\sim 2\mathrm{G}(v){\bm{1}}_{\{v\in\mathbb{H}\}}\mathrm{d}v
,

\chi_{\mathrm{G}}=\frac{\mathfrak{n}_{*}(\ell>1)}{\int_{\mathbb{H}}\mathcal{V}(x_{1})\mathrm{G}_{1}(\mathrm{d}x)}.

Let us check that
I:=\int_{\mathbb{H}}\mathcal{V}(x_{1})\mathrm{G}_{1}(\mathrm{d}x)<\infty
: recalling Notation
52
,
we have
I=\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{V}(EW_{1})]
, where
E\sim\mathrm{Exp}(1)
and
W\sim\mathrm{G}_{+}(v)\mathrm{d}v
are
independent. Thus
I\leq C\mathbb{E}[1+|EW_{1}|^{\alpha/2}]
by (
110
). This quantity is
finite since
\mathbb{E}[|W|^{\alpha/2}]<\infty
under Assumption
22
-(a).

The main goal of this subsection is to prove that
\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}\to\mathfrak{n}_{*}
under Assumption
22
-(a).
Informally, starting from (
106
) and using
Lemma
69
-(a), we should have

\int_{\mathcal{E}}\phi(e)\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{d}e)\simeq\chi_{\mathrm{G}}\varepsilon^{-1/2}\int_{\mathbb{H}}\mathbb{E}\Big[\phi\big(\big(Z_{t\wedge\ell(Z^{\varepsilon^{1/\alpha}x})}^{\varepsilon^{1/\alpha}x}\big)_{t\geq 0}\big)\Big]\mathrm{G}_{1}(x)\mathrm{d}x.

Admitting (
2
), we would have

\mathbb{E}[\phi((Z_{t\wedge\ell(Z^{\varepsilon^{1/\alpha}x})}^{\varepsilon^{1/\alpha}x})_{t\geq 0})]\simeq a_{*}\varepsilon^{1/2}x_{1}^{\alpha/2}\mathfrak{n}_{*}(\phi)
, whence

\int_{\mathcal{E}}\phi(e)\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{d}e)\simeq\kappa\mathfrak{n}_{*}(\phi),\quad\text{where}\quad\kappa=\chi_{\mathrm{G}}a_{*}\int_{\mathbb{H}}x_{1}^{\alpha/2}\mathrm{G}_{1}(x)\mathrm{d}x,

and it might be possible to show that
\kappa=1
. However, we are far from being able to establish the first
approximate equality. We are thus led to reproduce in a multidimensional setting the arguments of
Doney
[
36
]
, initiated by Bolthausen
[
21
]
,
regarding the convergence in law of conditioned random walks.
The first step consists in showing that
\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\ell>\delta)\to\mathfrak{n}_{*}(\ell>\delta)
.

Lemma 71
.

Grant Assumptions
18
and
22
-(a). For any
\delta>0
,

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\ell>\delta)=\mathfrak{n}_{*}(\ell>\delta)=\delta^{-1/2}\mathfrak{n}_{*}(\ell>1)\quad\text{as $\varepsilon\to 0$}.

Moreover, there is
C>0
such that
\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\ell>\delta)\leq C\delta^{-1/2}

for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
, all
\delta>0
.
Finally,

\mathfrak{n}^{1}(\ell>t)\sim t^{-1/2}\mathfrak{n}_{*}(\ell>1)\quad\text{as $t\to\infty$}.

Proof.

By (
106
), we have

\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\ell>\delta)=\chi_{\mathrm{G}}\varepsilon^{-1/2}\int_{\mathbb{H}}\mathbb{P}(\ell(Y^{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{1/\alpha}x})>\delta)\mathrm{G}_{1}(x)\mathrm{d}x.

Using next (
113
), we find

\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\ell>\delta)\leq C\delta^{-1/2}\int_{\mathbb{H}}(1+x_{1}^{\alpha/2})\mathrm{G}_{1}(x)\mathrm{d}x\leq C\delta^{-1/2},

since
\mathrm{G}
(and thus
\mathrm{G}_{1}
) has a moment of order
\alpha/2
by Assumption
22
-(a). Moreover,
(
104
) and (
115
) tell us that

\mathbb{P}(\ell(Y^{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{1/\alpha}x})>\delta)=\mathbb{P}(\ell(Y^{1,x})>\delta/\varepsilon)\sim\mathcal{V}(x_{1})(\varepsilon/\delta)^{1/2}

as
\varepsilon\to 0
. By dominated convergence, we end with

\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\ell>\delta)\sim\chi_{\mathrm{G}}\delta^{-1/2}\int_{\mathbb{H}}\mathcal{V}(x_{1})\mathrm{d}x=\delta^{-1/2}\mathfrak{n}_{*}(\ell>1)

by definition of
\chi_{\mathrm{G}}
. It holds that
\delta^{-1/2}\mathfrak{n}_{*}(\ell>1)=\mathfrak{n}_{*}(\ell>\delta)
by Lemma
27
.
Finally, (
103
) (with
\varepsilon=1/t
) implies that
\mathfrak{n}^{1}(\ell>t)=t^{-1/2}\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\ell>1)\sim t^{-1/2}\mathfrak{n}_{*}(\ell>1)
as
t\to\infty
.
∎

The next step is to show that
\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\cdot|\ell>\delta)\to\mathfrak{n}_{*}(\cdot|\ell>\delta)
.
To mimic the arguments of Doney
[
36
]
, we need the following result,
that will allow us to express
\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\cdot|\ell>\delta)
as a piece of the path of some process

(U^{\varepsilon}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
. Doney’s work is concerned with a one-dimensional random walk, while we have to work
a little more, because
d\geq 2
and under
\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}
,
the walk does not start from
0
, but from a random variable
O
distributed according to
\mathrm{G}_{1}
.
The assumption that
\mathrm{G}
has a moment
of order
\alpha/2
is crucial in the following results.

Lemma 72
.

Grant Assumption
18
and Assumption
22
-(a).
There exists a family of processes
((U^{\varepsilon}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
,
\varepsilon\in(0,1])
, with the following properties.

(i)
(U^{\varepsilon}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
converges in law to the ISP
α,0
for the local uniform topology as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

(ii) Fix
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
and set
\tau^{\varepsilon}_{0}=0
and, for
n\geq 0
,

\rho_{n}^{\varepsilon}=\inf\{t>\tau_{n}^{\varepsilon}:\Delta U^{\varepsilon}_{t}\neq 0\}\quad\text{and}\quad\tau_{n+1}^{\varepsilon}=\inf\{t>\rho_{n}^{\varepsilon}:U^{\varepsilon}_{t}-U^{\varepsilon}_{\tau_{n}^{\varepsilon}}\notin\mathbb{H}\}.

The family
((V^{\varepsilon,n}_{t})_{t\geq 0}=(U^{\varepsilon}_{(\rho^{\varepsilon}_{n}+t)\land\tau^{\varepsilon}_{n+1}}-U^{\varepsilon}_{\rho_{n}^{\varepsilon}-}),n\geq 0)
is i.i.d.
with common law
\chi_{\mathrm{G}}^{-1}\varepsilon^{1/2}\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}
.

(iii) For all
T>0
, it holds that (convention :
\rho^{\varepsilon}_{-1}=0
)

\sup\{\rho_{n}^{\varepsilon}-\tau_{n}^{\varepsilon}:n\geq 0,\rho_{n-1}^{\varepsilon}\leq T\}\to 0\quad\text{in probability as $\varepsilon\to 0$.}

(120)

(iv) Set
I^{\varepsilon}_{t}=\inf_{s\in[0,t]}U^{\varepsilon}_{t}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{1}
. For all
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
, all
n\geq 0
,

I^{\varepsilon}_{\tau_{n+1}^{\varepsilon}-}=I^{\varepsilon}_{\rho^{\varepsilon}_{n}-}=I^{\varepsilon}_{\tau^{\varepsilon}_{n}}=U^{\varepsilon}_{\tau_{n}^{\varepsilon}}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{1}=U^{\varepsilon}_{\rho_{n}^{\varepsilon}-}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{1}.

(121)

Proof.

We recall that
\mathrm{H}_{1}
is a probability density on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{H}

with marginals
\mathrm{F}_{1}
and
\mathrm{G}_{1}
, see Notation
52
. We consider
a Poisson measure
\mathrm{M}=\sum_{s\in\mathrm{J}}\delta_{(s,u_{s},v_{s})}
on

[0,\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{H}
with intensity
\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{H}_{1}(u,v)\mathrm{d}u\mathrm{d}v
and introduce

(\mathcal{F}_{t}=\sigma(\{\mathrm{M}(A),A\in\mathcal{B}([0,t]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{H})\})_{t\geq 0}
.
Then for

S_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{H}}u\mathrm{M}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}u,\mathrm{d}v),

(\varepsilon^{1/\alpha}S_{t/\varepsilon})_{t\geq 0}

has the same law as
(Y^{\varepsilon,0}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
and thus converges in law
to an ISP
α,0
for the local uniform topology as in Step 3 of
the proof of Lemma
65
. We next consider

U_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{H}}\!\!\!(u{\bm{1}}_{\{X_{s-}>0\}}+v{\bm{1}}_{\{X_{s-}=0\}})\mathrm{M}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}u,\mathrm{d}v),\;\;\text{where}\;\;X_{t}=U_{t}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{1}-\inf_{s\in[0,t]}U_{s}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{1}.

This path-dependent S.D.E. obviously has a unique solution
(U_{t})_{t\geq 0}
,
since
\mathrm{M}
has a finite number of jumps on each finite time interval.

Step 1.
We introduce
\tau_{0}=0
and, for
n\geq 0
, we set

\rho_{n}=\inf\{t>\tau_{n}:\Delta U_{t}\neq 0\}\quad\text{and}\quad\tau_{n+1}=\inf\{t>\rho_{n}:U_{t}-U_{\tau_{n}}\notin\mathbb{H}\}.

Here we check that for all
n\geq 0
,
\rho_{n}=\inf\{t>\tau_{n}:X_{t}>0\}
and

\tau_{n+1}=\inf\{t>\rho_{n}:X_{t}=0\}
. We set
U_{1,t}=U_{t}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{1}
and

I_{t}=\inf_{s\in[0,t]}U_{1,s}
, whence
X_{t}=U_{1,t}-I_{t}
. We only give the first steps.

\bullet
For
t\in[0,\rho_{0})
, we have
U_{t}=0
and
I_{t}=0
, whence
X_{t}=0
,
so that
U_{\rho_{0}}=v_{\rho_{0}}\in\mathbb{H}
, i.e.
U_{1,\rho_{0}}>0
. Thus
I_{\rho_{0}}=0
and
X_{\rho_{0}}>0
.
Consequently,
\rho_{0}=\inf\{t>0:X_{t}>0\}
.

\bullet
For
t\in[\rho_{0},\tau_{1})
, we have
U_{t}=U_{t}-U_{\tau_{0}}\in\mathbb{H}
,
i.e.
U_{1,t}>0
. Thus
I_{t}=0
and
X_{t}>0
for all
t\in[\rho_{0},\tau_{1})
.
Moreover,
U_{\tau_{1}}\notin\mathbb{H}
, i.e.
U_{1,\tau_{1}}\leq 0
, so that
I_{\tau_{1}}=U_{1,\tau_{1}}
and thus
X_{\tau_{1}}=0
.
Consequently,
\tau_{1}=\inf\{t>\rho_{0}:X_{t}=0\}
.

\bullet
Since
X_{\tau_{1}}=0
and since
\rho_{1}
is the first jump instant of
U
after
\tau_{1}
,
we have
X_{t}=0
for all
t\in[\tau_{1},\rho_{1})
and
U_{\rho_{1}}=U_{\tau_{1}}+v_{\rho_{1}}
.
Since
v_{\rho_{1}}\in\mathbb{H}
, we have
U_{1,\rho_{1}}>U_{1,\tau_{1}}
, whence

I_{\rho_{1}}=I_{\tau_{1}}=U_{1,\tau_{1}}
, so that
X_{\rho_{1}}=v_{\rho_{1}}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{1}>0
. Thus
\rho_{1}=\inf\{t>\tau_{1}:X_{t}>0\}
.

\bullet
For
t\in[\rho_{1},\tau_{2})
,
U_{t}-U_{\tau_{1}}\in\mathbb{H}
, i.e.
U_{1,t}>U_{1,\tau_{1}}
,
so that
I_{t}=I_{\rho_{1}}=U_{1,\tau_{1}}
, whence
X_{t}>0
.
Moreover,
U_{\tau_{2}}-U_{\tau_{1}}\notin\mathbb{H}
, i.e.
U_{1,\tau_{2}}\leq U_{1,\tau_{1}}
,
so that
I_{\tau_{2}}=U_{1,\tau_{2}}
and thus
X_{\tau_{2}}=0
.
As a consequence,
\tau_{2}=\inf\{t>\rho_{1}:X_{t}=0\}
.

Step 2.
We set
V^{n}_{t}=U_{(\rho_{n}+t)\land\tau_{n+1}}-U_{\tau_{n}}
and prove that

((V^{n}_{t})_{t\geq 0},n\geq 0)
is i.i.d. with common law
\chi_{\mathrm{G}}^{-1}\mathfrak{n}_{1}
.
It suffices that for each
n\geq 0
,
(V^{n}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is independent of
\mathcal{F}_{\tau_{n}}
and

\chi_{\mathrm{G}}^{-1}\mathfrak{n}_{1}
-distributed
We let
\mathrm{K}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}u)=\sum_{s\in\mathrm{J}}\delta_{(s,u_{s})}

which is Poisson with intensity
\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{F}_{1}(u)\mathrm{d}u
.

Since
X_{\rho_{n}-}=X_{\tau_{n}}=0
and since
X_{s-}>0

for all
s\in(\rho_{n},\tau_{n+1}]
by Step 1,

V^{n}_{t}=U_{\rho_{n}}-U_{\tau_{n}}+\int_{\rho_{n}+}^{(\rho_{n}+t)\land\tau_{n+1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{H}}u\mathrm{M}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}u,\mathrm{d}w)=v_{\rho_{n}}+\int_{\rho_{n}+}^{(\rho_{n}+t)\land\tau_{n+1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{H}}u\mathrm{K}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}u).

Introducing
\mathrm{K}^{n}=\sum_{s\in\mathrm{J},s>\rho_{n}}\delta_{(s-\rho_{n},u_{s})}
,
which is a Poisson measure with intensity
\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{F}_{1}(u)\mathrm{d}u
independent of
\mathcal{F}_{\rho_{n}}
, we get

V^{n}_{t}=v_{\rho_{n}}+\int_{0}^{t\land(\tau_{n+1}-\rho_{n})}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u\mathrm{K}^{n}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}u).

Thus
(V^{n}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is independent of
\mathcal{F}_{\rho_{n}-}
and thus of
\mathcal{F}_{\tau_{n}}
. Setting

Y^{n}_{t}=v_{\rho_{n}}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u\mathrm{K}^{n}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}u)
, we have
\tau_{n+1}-\rho_{n}=\ell(Y^{n})
, because

Y^{n}_{t}=U_{(\rho_{n}+t)\land\tau_{n+1}}-U_{\tau_{n}}\in\mathbb{H}
for all
t\in[0,\tau_{n+1}-\rho_{n})
and

Y^{n}_{\tau_{n+1}-\rho_{n}}=U_{\tau_{n+1}}-U_{\tau_{n}}\notin\mathbb{H}
by definition of
\tau_{n+1}
.
Thus
(V^{n}_{t})_{t\geq 0}=(Y^{n}_{t\land\ell(Y^{n})})_{t\geq 0}
.
Since
\rho_{n}=\inf\{t>\tau_{n}:\Delta U_{t}\neq 0\}
,

v_{\rho_{n}}
is
\mathrm{G}_{1}(v)\mathrm{d}v
-distributed and independent of
\mathrm{K}^{n}
(because it is

\mathcal{F}_{\rho_{n}}
-measurable) and we conclude that
(V^{n}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is

\chi_{\mathrm{G}}^{-1}\mathfrak{n}_{1}
-distributed, recall Subsection
10.1
.

Step 3.
We now introduce
U^{\varepsilon}_{t}=\varepsilon^{1/\alpha}U_{t/\varepsilon}
and verify (ii). For
(\tau_{n}^{\varepsilon},\rho_{n}^{\varepsilon})_{n\geq 0}

as in the statement, we have
\tau_{n}^{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon\tau_{n}
and
\rho_{n}^{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon\rho_{n}
. Indeed,
\tau_{0}^{\varepsilon}=0=\varepsilon\tau_{0}

and if
\tau_{n}^{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon\tau_{n}
for some
n\geq 0
, then

\rho^{\varepsilon}_{n}=\!\inf\{t>\tau_{n}^{\varepsilon}:\Delta U^{\varepsilon}_{t}\neq 0\}=\!\inf\{t>\varepsilon\tau_{n}:\Delta U_{t/\varepsilon}\neq 0\}=\!\varepsilon\inf\{s>\tau_{n}:\Delta U_{s}\neq 0\}=\varepsilon\rho_{n},

see Step 1. Similarly,

\tau^{\varepsilon}_{n+1}=\!\inf\{t>\rho_{n}^{\varepsilon}:U^{\varepsilon}_{t}-U^{\varepsilon}_{\tau_{n}^{\varepsilon}}\notin\mathbb{H}\}=\!\inf\{t>\varepsilon\rho_{n}:U_{t/\varepsilon}-U_{\tau_{n}}\notin\mathbb{H}\}=\!\varepsilon\inf\{s>\rho_{n}:U_{s}-U_{\tau_{n}}\notin\mathbb{H}\},

which equals
\varepsilon\tau_{n+1}
.
Since
U^{\varepsilon}_{\rho_{n}^{\varepsilon}-}=U^{\varepsilon}_{\tau_{n}^{\varepsilon}}
, we get

(U^{\varepsilon}_{(\rho^{\varepsilon}_{n}+t)\land\tau^{\varepsilon}_{n+1}}-U^{\varepsilon}_{\rho_{n}^{\varepsilon}-})_{t\geq 0}=(\varepsilon^{1/\alpha}V^{n}_{t/\varepsilon})_{t\geq 0}
for each
n\geq 0
, so that (ii) follows from Step 2 and (
103
).

Step 4.
We next show (i). Since
(\varepsilon^{1/\alpha}S_{t/\varepsilon})_{t\geq 0}
goes in law
to the ISP
α,0
, it suffices that

\Delta^{\varepsilon}_{T}=\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|U^{\varepsilon}_{t}-\varepsilon^{1/\alpha}S_{t/\varepsilon}|\to 0
in probability for any
T>0
. But

\Delta^{\varepsilon}_{T}=\varepsilon^{1/\alpha}\sup_{[0,T/\varepsilon]}|S_{t}-U_{t}|\leq\varepsilon^{1/\alpha}\int_{0}^{T/\varepsilon}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{H}}(|u|+|v|){\bm{1}}_{\{X_{s-}=0\}}\mathrm{M}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}u,\mathrm{d}w).

We set
M_{t}=\sum_{n\geq 0}{\bm{1}}_{\{\rho_{n}\leq t\}}
and write, recalling Step 1,

\Delta^{\varepsilon}_{T}\leq\varepsilon^{1/\alpha}\sum_{k=0}^{M_{T/\varepsilon}}(|u_{\rho_{k}}|+|v_{\rho_{k}}|)=(\varepsilon^{1/2}M_{T/\varepsilon})^{2/\alpha}\Gamma_{M_{T/\varepsilon}},\quad\text{where}\quad\Gamma_{n}=\frac{1}{n^{2/\alpha}}\sum_{k=0}^{n}(|u_{\rho_{k}}|+|v_{\rho_{k}}|).

To complete the proof, it suffices to check that
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\Gamma_{M_{T/\varepsilon}}=0
in probability and that

\lim_{a\to\infty}\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbb{P}(\varepsilon^{1/2}M_{T/\varepsilon}>a)=0
.

The sequence
(u_{\rho_{k}},v_{\rho_{k}})_{k\geq 0}
is i.i.d. with common law
\mathrm{H}_{1}
(because for each
n\geq 0
,

\rho_{n}
is the first jump time of
\mathrm{M}
after
\tau_{n}
, which is an
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
stopping time).
By Assumptions
18

and
22
-(a),

\mathbb{E}[|u_{\rho_{1}}|^{\alpha/2}+|v_{\rho_{1}}|^{\alpha/2}]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|z|^{\alpha/2}\mathrm{F}_{1}(z)\mathrm{d}z+\int_{\mathbb{H}}|z|^{\alpha/2}\mathrm{G}_{1}(z)\mathrm{d}z<\infty.

We conclude e.g. from
[
11
, Proposition 33]
that

\Gamma_{n}\to 0
in probability as
n\to\infty
. Since
\rho_{n}<\infty
for all
n\geq 0
,
we have
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}M_{T/\varepsilon}=\infty
a.s., whence
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\Gamma_{M_{T/\varepsilon}}=0
in probability. Next,

\{\varepsilon^{1/2}M_{T/\varepsilon}>a\}=\{\rho_{\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1/2}a\rfloor}<T/\varepsilon\}\subset\cap_{k=0,\cdots,\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1/2}a\rfloor-1}\{\tau_{k+1}-\rho_{k}<T/\varepsilon\},

because for all
n>k\geq 0
,
\rho_{n}\geq\rho_{k+1}-\rho_{k}\geq\tau_{k+1}-\rho_{k}
.
The sequence
(\tau_{n+1}-\rho_{n})_{n\geq 0}
is i.i.d. and
\mathbb{P}(\tau_{1}-\rho_{0}>t)=\chi_{\mathrm{G}}^{-1}\mathfrak{n}^{1}(\ell>t)
by Step 2.
Thus
\mathbb{P}(\tau_{1}-\rho_{0}>t)\sim ct^{-1/2}
by Lemma
71
, where
c=\chi_{\mathrm{G}}^{-1}\mathfrak{n}_{*}(\ell>1)
. Hence

\mathbb{P}(\varepsilon^{1/2}M_{T/\varepsilon}>a)\leq(1-\mathbb{P}(\tau_{1}-\rho_{0}>T/\varepsilon))^{\lfloor\varepsilon^{-1/2}a\rfloor}\sim(1-cT^{-1/2}\varepsilon^{1/2})^{\varepsilon^{-1/2}a}\to\exp(-cT^{-1/2}a)

as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Thus
\lim_{a\to\infty}\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbb{P}(\varepsilon^{1/2}M_{T/\varepsilon}>a)=0
as desired.

Step 5.
We now prove (iii). Set
\Delta_{\varepsilon,T}=\sup\{\rho_{n}^{\varepsilon}-\tau_{n}^{\varepsilon}:n\geq 0,\rho_{n-1}^{\varepsilon}\leq T\}
.
With the notation of Step 4, we have
\Delta_{\varepsilon,T}=\varepsilon\sup\{\rho_{n}-\tau_{n}:n\geq 0,n\leq M_{T/\varepsilon}+1\}
.
Thus for any
\eta>0
, any
a>0
,

\mathbb{P}(\Delta_{\varepsilon,T}>\eta)\leq\mathbb{P}(\varepsilon^{1/2}M_{T/\varepsilon}>a)+\mathbb{P}\Big(\sup_{n=0,\dots,\lfloor a\varepsilon^{-1/2}\rfloor+1}(\rho_{n}-\tau_{n})>\eta\varepsilon^{-1}\Big).

For each
n\geq 0
,
\rho_{n}-\tau_{n}

is
\mathrm{Exp}(1)
-distributed. Thus for any
a>0
,

\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbb{P}(\Delta_{\varepsilon,T}>\eta)\leq\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\Big[\mathbb{P}(\varepsilon^{1/2}M_{T/\varepsilon}>a)+(a\varepsilon^{-1/2}+1)e^{-\eta/\varepsilon}\Big]=\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbb{P}(\varepsilon^{1/2}M_{T/\varepsilon}>a).

Since
\lim_{a\to\infty}\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbb{P}(\varepsilon^{1/2}M_{T/\varepsilon}>a)=0
as already seen, the result follows.

Step 6.
For (iv), it suffices to study the case without
\varepsilon
.
By definition of
\rho_{n}
, we clearly have
U_{\rho_{n}-}=U_{\tau_{n}}
and
I_{\rho_{n}-}=I_{\tau_{n}}
.
Since
\tau_{n}=\inf\{t>\rho_{n-1}:X_{t}=0\}
with
X_{t}=U_{1,t}-I_{t}
by Step 1, we have

U_{1,\tau_{n}}=I_{\tau_{n}}
. Finally, since
X_{t}\geq 0
during
(\tau_{n},\tau_{n+1})
, we have
I_{\tau_{n+1}-}=I_{\tau_{n}}
.
∎

We can now show that
\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\cdot|\ell>\delta)\to\mathfrak{n}_{*}(\cdot|\ell>\delta)
,
as well as some other useful convergences.

Lemma 73
.

Grant Assumptions
18
and
22
-(a).
For all
\delta>0
, the set
\mathbb{D}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R}^{d})
being endowed with the
{\bm{\mathrm{J}}_{1}}
-topology,

\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\cdot|\ell>\delta)\to\mathfrak{n}_{*}(\cdot|\ell>\delta)\quad\text{as $\varepsilon\to 0$.}

Moreover, for all
\delta>0
, all
y>0
,

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(M>y|\ell>\delta)=\mathfrak{n}_{*}(M>y|\ell>\delta)\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\ell>\delta|M>y)=\mathfrak{n}_{*}(\ell>\delta|M>y).

Proof.

We fix
\delta>0
,
y>0
and consider the objects introduced in Lemma
72
.
By Skorokhod’s representation theorem, we may assume
that
(U^{\varepsilon}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
a.s. converges to
some ISP
α,0

(Z_{t})_{t\geq 0}
for the local uniform topology.

Step 1.
For
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
fixed, we introduce

\sigma_{\varepsilon}=\inf\{n\geq 0:\ell(V^{\varepsilon,n})>\delta\}\quad\text{and}\quad\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}=\inf\{n\geq 0:M(V^{\varepsilon,n})>y\}.

By Lemma
72
-(ii),
\bm{v}_{\varepsilon}=(V^{\varepsilon,\sigma_{\varepsilon}}_{t})_{t\geq 0}\sim\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\cdot|\ell>\delta)
and

\bm{v}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}=(V^{\varepsilon,\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}_{t})_{t\geq 0}\sim\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\cdot|M>y)
. In other words, if setting

g_{\varepsilon}=\rho^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{\varepsilon}}
,
d_{\varepsilon}=\tau^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{\varepsilon}+1}
,
g_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}=\rho^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}

and
d_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}=\tau^{\varepsilon}_{\sigma_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}+1}
,

\bm{v}_{\varepsilon}=(U^{\varepsilon}_{(g_{\varepsilon}+t)\land d_{\varepsilon}}-U^{\varepsilon}_{g_{\varepsilon}-})_{t\geq 0}\sim\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\cdot|\ell>\delta)\;\;\;\text{and}\;\;\;\bm{v}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}=(U^{\varepsilon}_{(g_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}+t)\land d_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}-U^{\varepsilon}_{g_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}-})_{t\geq 0}\sim\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\cdot|M>y).

Step 2.
Recall that
\mathfrak{n}_{*}
was defined through an ISP
α,0

(Z_{t})_{t\geq 0}
and the corresponding
Poisson measure of excursions
\Pi_{*}=\sum_{u\in\mathrm{J}}\delta_{(u,e_{u})}
: this Poisson measure has for intensity

\mathrm{d}u\mathfrak{n}_{*}(\mathrm{d}e)
, see the paragraph around (
7
).
We set

\sigma=\inf\{u\in\mathrm{J}:\ell(e_{u})>\delta\}\quad\text{and}\quad\sigma^{\prime}=\inf\{u\in\mathrm{J}:M(e_{u})>y\}.

Due to the properties of Poisson measures,

\bm{v}=e_{\sigma}\sim\mathfrak{n}_{*}(\cdot|\ell>\delta)
and
\bm{v}^{\prime}=e_{\sigma^{\prime}}\sim\mathfrak{n}_{*}(\cdot|M>y)
.
By (
7
), this rewrites, setting
g=\gamma_{\sigma-}
,
d=\gamma_{\sigma}
,

g^{\prime}=\gamma_{\sigma^{\prime}-}
and
d^{\prime}=\gamma_{\sigma^{\prime}}
,

\bm{v}=(Z_{(g+t)\land d}-Z_{g})_{t\geq 0}\sim\mathfrak{n}_{*}(\cdot|\ell>\delta)\quad\text{and}\quad\bm{v}^{\prime}=(Z_{(g^{\prime}+t)\land d^{\prime}}-Z_{g^{\prime}})_{t\geq 0}\sim\mathfrak{n}_{*}(\cdot|M>y).

Step 3.
We now recall a few properties of the stable process
(Z_{t})_{t\geq 0}
. We set

Z_{1,t}=Z_{t}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{1}
and
I_{t}=\inf_{s\in[0,t]}Z_{1,s}
. Recall that
(\xi_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is the local
time of
(Z_{1,t}-I_{t})_{t\geq 0}
and that
(\gamma_{u})_{u\geq 0}
is its right-continuous generalized inverse.

(a) Almost surely, for all
u\in\mathrm{J}
,
(Z_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is continuous at
\gamma_{u-}
.

Indeed, we know from Doney
[
36
, Lemma 2 point 6]
that a.s.,
for all
u\in\mathrm{J}
,
(Z_{1,t})_{t\geq 0}
is continuous at
\gamma_{u-}
, and the result follows since a.s.,

\{s\geq 0:\Delta Z_{t}\neq 0\}=\{s\geq 0:\Delta Z_{1,t}\neq 0\}
.

(b) Almost surely, for all
u\in\mathrm{J}
,
\ell(e_{u})\neq\delta
and
M(e_{u})\neq y
.

This follows from the fact that
\mathfrak{n}_{*}(\ell=\delta)=\mathfrak{n}_{*}(M=y)=0
by Lemma
27
.

(c) Almost surely, for all
u\in\mathrm{J}
,
e(\ell(e_{u})-)\in\mathbb{H}
and for all
s\in(0,\ell(e_{u}))
, it holds that

e_{u}(s)\in\mathbb{H}
and
e_{u}(s-)\in\mathbb{H}
.

For
s\in(0,\ell(e_{u}))
, we have
e_{u}(s)\in\mathbb{H}
by definition of
\ell
and

e_{u}(s-)\in\mathbb{H}
by (
108
), which also implies that
e(\ell(e_{u})-)\in\mathbb{H}
.

(d) Almost surely, for all
t>s\geq 0
, if
I_{t-}=I_{s}
, then
u:=\xi_{t}=\xi_{s}\in\mathrm{J}
and

\gamma_{u-}\leq s<t\leq\gamma_{u}
.

Since
(\xi_{r})_{r\geq 0}
increases only when
(I_{r})_{r\geq 0}
decreases, we have
\xi_{t}=\xi_{s}
. By definition
of
(\gamma_{v})_{v\geq 0}
, this implies that for
u=\xi_{t}
, we have
\gamma_{u-}\leq s<t\leq\gamma_{u}
, whence

u\in\mathrm{J}
.

(e) Almost surely, for all
t>s\geq 0
, if
I_{s}=Z_{1,s-}\land Z_{1,s}
and
\xi_{t}=\xi_{s}
,
then
\xi_{s}\in\mathrm{J}
and
s=\gamma_{\xi_{s}-}
.

As in (d), we have
u:=\xi_{s}\in\mathrm{J}
and
\gamma_{u-}\leq s<t\leq\gamma_{u}
. If
s>\gamma_{u-}
,
then by (c) and (
7
),

Z_{1,s}\land Z_{1,s-}>Z_{1,\gamma_{u-}}
, implying that
I_{s}<Z_{1,s}\land Z_{1,s-}
.

(f) Almost surely, for all
u\in\mathrm{J}
, all
s\in[0,\gamma_{u-})
,
I_{s}>I_{\gamma_{u-}}
.

By Bertoin
[
9
, Lemma 1 p 218]
,
(H_{v}:=-I_{\gamma_{v}})_{v\geq 0}
is a stable subordinator
and is thus
strictly increasing. Now for
u\in\mathrm{J}
and
s<\gamma_{u-}
, we have
\xi_{s}<u
, implying that
H_{\xi_{s}}<H_{u-}
,
i.e.
I_{\gamma_{\xi_{s}}}>I_{\gamma_{u-}}
. Since
s\leq\gamma_{\xi_{s}}
, the conclusion follows.

(g) Almost surely, for all
t\geq 0
, if
I_{t-}=Z_{1,t-}
, then
\Delta Z_{t}=0
.

As in (a), it suffices to show that
\Delta Z_{1,t}=0
. If first
\Delta Z_{1,t}>0
, then

I_{t}=I_{t-}=Z_{1,t-}\land Z_{1,t}
and
Z_{1,t}>Z_{1,t-}
. By right continuity, there is
s>t
such that

I_{s}=I_{t}
, whence
\xi_{t}\in\mathrm{J}
and
t=\gamma_{\xi_{t}-}
by (e). Thus
\Delta Z_{t}=0
by (a).
If next
\Delta Z_{1,t}<0
, then we also have
\xi_{t}\in\mathrm{J}
(because else,
\Delta Z_{t}=0

exactly as in the proof of Theorem
7
, see the last paragraph before Step 2) and
t>\gamma_{\xi_{t}-}

(because else,
\Delta Z_{t}=0
by (a)). Thus
t\in(\gamma_{\xi_{t}-},\gamma_{\xi_{t}}]
, so that

Z_{1,t-}>Z_{1,\gamma_{\xi_{t}-}}
by (
108
), which contradicts the fact that

I_{t-}=Z_{1,t-}
.

Step 4.
Here we show that
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}g_{\varepsilon}=g
a.s. We set
U^{\varepsilon}_{1,t}=U^{\varepsilon}_{t}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{1}

and
I^{\varepsilon}_{t}=\inf_{s\in[0,t]}U^{\varepsilon}_{1,s}
.

Step 4.1.
We have
\Gamma=\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}g_{\varepsilon}\leq g
. Indeed, consider a (random) sequence
\varepsilon_{k}\to 0

such that
\lim_{k}g_{\varepsilon_{k}}=\Gamma
. Recall that
d>g+\delta
, introduce
m=(g+d)/2

and
n_{k}=\sup\{{n\geq 0}:\rho^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{n}\leq m\}
, as well as
\bar{g}_{k}=\rho^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{n_{k}}
and

\bar{d}_{k}=\tau^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{n_{k}+1}
. Up to extraction, we may assume that
\bar{g}_{k}\to\bar{g}\in[0,m]
and
that
\bar{d}_{k}\to\bar{d}\in[\bar{g},\infty]
.

(i) We have
\bar{g}\leq g
. Else,
\bar{g}_{k}>g
for all
k
large enough, whence

U^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{1,g}\geq I^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{\bar{g}_{k}}=U^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{1,\bar{g}_{k}-}
by (
121
). But

\lim_{k}U^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{1,g}=Z_{1,g}
and, up to extraction,
\lim_{k}U^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{1,\bar{g}_{k}-}=Z_{1,\bar{g}}
or
Z_{1,\bar{g}-}
by
Lemma
87
. Thus
Z_{1,g}\geq Z_{1,\bar{g}}
or
Z_{1,g}\geq Z_{1,\bar{g}-}
. By Step 3-(c), we have

Z_{1,t}>Z_{1,g}
and
Z_{1,t-}>Z_{1,g}
for all
t\in(g,d)
. Thus
\bar{g}\notin(g,d)
. Since
\bar{g}\leq m<d
,
we have
\bar{g}\leq g
.

(ii) We have
\bar{g}\geq g
. By definition of
n_{k}
,

\rho_{n_{k}+1}^{\varepsilon_{k}}>m
, which implies that
\tau_{n_{k}+1}^{\varepsilon_{k}}>(g+m)/2=:a

for all
k
large enough, see (
120
). We have
\bar{g}_{k}<a
for
k
large enough by (i).
Hence by (
121
),
I^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{a}=I^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{\bar{g}_{k}-}
.
We have
I^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{a}\to I_{a}
and, by Lemma
87
, up to extraction,
I^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{\bar{g}_{k}-}\to I_{\bar{g}}
or
I_{\bar{g}-}
.
Thus
I_{a}=I_{\bar{g}}
or
I_{a}=I_{\bar{g}-}
.
Since
(I_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is nonincreasing, we conclude that
I_{a}=I_{\bar{g}}
in any case.
But
a\in(g,d)
, whence
I_{a}=I_{g}
and we end with
I_{g}=I_{\bar{g}}
.
This is not possible if
\bar{g}<g
, see Step 3-(f).

(iii) We have
\bar{d}\geq d
. Else,
\bar{d}\in[g,d)
. We have already seen in (ii) that
\bar{d}_{k}=\tau_{n_{k}+1}^{\varepsilon_{k}}>a

for
k
large enough, so that
\bar{d}\in[a,d)\subset(g,d)
. By definition of
\bar{d}_{k}
, we have

U^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{1,\bar{d}_{k}}<U^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{1,\bar{g}_{k}-}
. Using Lemma
87
and that
g
is a continuity point of

(Z_{t})_{t\geq 0}
by Step 3-(a), we deduce that either
Z_{1,\bar{d}}\leq Z_{1,g}
or
Z_{1,\bar{d}-}\leq Z_{1,g}
.
This is not possible if
\bar{d}\in(g,d)
by Step 3-(c).

(iv) As a consequence,
\ell(V^{\varepsilon_{k},n_{k}})=\bar{d}_{k}-\bar{g}_{k}\to\bar{d}-\bar{g}\geq d-g>\delta
by definition of
g
and
d
.
Thus for all
k
large enough,
\sigma_{\varepsilon_{k}}\leq n_{k}
, whence
g_{\varepsilon_{k}}=\rho^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{\sigma_{\varepsilon_{k}}}\leq\rho^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{n_{k}}=\bar{g}_{k}\to g
. Hence
\Gamma=\lim_{k}g_{\varepsilon_{k}}\leq g
.

Step 4.2.
We have
\theta=\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}g_{\varepsilon}\geq g
. Observe that
\theta
is finite by Step 4.1
and consider a (random) sequence
\varepsilon_{k}\to 0
such that
\lim_{k}g_{\varepsilon_{k}}=\theta
.
By (
121
) and since

d_{\varepsilon_{k}}=\tau^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{\sigma_{\varepsilon_{k}}+1}>\rho^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{\sigma_{\varepsilon_{k}}}+\delta=g_{\varepsilon_{k}}+\delta,

we have
I^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{g_{\varepsilon_{k}}+\delta}=I^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{g_{\varepsilon_{k}}-}
.
By Lemma
87
, up to extraction, we have
I^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{g_{\varepsilon_{k}}+\delta}\to I_{\theta+\delta}
or
I_{(\theta+\delta)-}

and
I^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{g_{\varepsilon_{k}}-}\to I_{\theta}
or
I_{\theta-}
. By monotony, we conclude that

I_{(\theta+\delta)-}=I_{\theta}
in any case. Step 3-(d) then implies that for
u=\xi_{\theta}
, we have

u\in\mathrm{J}
, and
\gamma_{u-}\leq\theta
and
\ell(e_{u})=\gamma_{u}-\gamma_{u-}\geq\delta
,
whence
\ell(e_{u})>\delta
by Step 3-(b). Thus
\sigma\leq u
, which implies
that
g=\gamma_{\sigma-}\leq\gamma_{u-}\leq\theta
.

Step 5.
We next show that
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}g_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}=g^{\prime}
a.s.

Step 5.1.
We have
\Gamma^{\prime}=\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}g_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\leq g^{\prime}
. Indeed, consider a (random) sequence
\varepsilon_{k}\to 0

such that
\lim_{k}g_{\varepsilon_{k}}^{\prime}=\Gamma^{\prime}
. We recall that
d^{\prime}>g^{\prime}
and we set

m=(g^{\prime}+d^{\prime})/2
. We introduce
n_{k}=\sup\{n\geq 0:\rho_{n}^{\varepsilon_{k}}\leq m\}
and set
\bar{g}^{\prime}_{k}=\rho^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{n_{k}}

and
\bar{d}^{\prime}_{k}=\tau^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{n_{k}+1}
. Exactly as in Step 4.1, we may assume that
\lim_{k}\bar{g}^{\prime}_{k}=\bar{g}^{\prime}\in[0,m]

and that
\lim_{k}\bar{d}^{\prime}_{k}=\bar{d}^{\prime}\in[\bar{g}^{\prime},\infty]
and we can show that
\bar{g}^{\prime}=g^{\prime}
and that
\bar{d}^{\prime}\geq d^{\prime}
.
By definition of
g^{\prime},d^{\prime}
, there is
t\in(g^{\prime},d^{\prime}]
such that
|Z_{t}-Z_{g^{\prime}}|>y
. By Step 3-(a) and
since
\bar{g}^{\prime}_{k}\to g^{\prime}
, we know that
U^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{\bar{g}^{\prime}_{k}-}\to Z_{g}
.

(i) If first
t\in(g^{\prime},\bar{d}^{\prime})
, then
t\in(\bar{g}_{k}^{\prime},\bar{d}^{\prime}_{k})
for all
k
large enough. Since

U^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{t}\to Z_{t}
, we conclude that for all
k
large enough,
|U^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{t}-U^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{\bar{g}^{\prime}_{k}-}|>y
, implying that

M(V^{\varepsilon_{k},n_{k}})>y
and thus that
\sigma^{\prime}_{k}\leq n_{k}
, whence
g_{k}^{\prime}\leq\bar{g}_{k}^{\prime}\to g^{\prime}
.
Hence
\Gamma^{\prime}=\lim_{k}g_{\varepsilon_{k}}^{\prime}\leq g^{\prime}
.

(ii) If next
t=\bar{d}^{\prime}
, then
t=d^{\prime}
(because
t\leq d^{\prime}\leq\bar{d}^{\prime}
). We now prove that
\bar{d}^{\prime}_{k}=t

for
k
large enough. This will imply that
U^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{\bar{d}_{k}^{\prime}}=U^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{t}\to Z_{t}
, so that

M(V^{\varepsilon_{k},n_{k}})\geq|U^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{\bar{d}_{k}^{\prime}}-U^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{\bar{g}^{\prime}_{k}-}|>y
for
k
large enough and thus that
\Gamma^{\prime}\leq g^{\prime}
as in (i).
The following three points show that
\bar{d}^{\prime}_{k}=t
for
k
large enough.

\bullet
We have
\bar{d}^{\prime}_{k}=\rho^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{n_{k}+1}\geq(g^{\prime}+m)/2=:a
for
k
large enough
exactly as in Step 4.1-(ii).

\bullet
We have
\inf_{s\in[a,t-g^{\prime})}d(Z_{g^{\prime}+s}-Z_{g^{\prime}},\mathbb{H})>0
by (
108
) (the length of the
excursion starting at
g^{\prime}
is
d^{\prime}-g^{\prime}=t-g^{\prime}
), so that for
k
large enough, for all
s\in[a,t)
,

U^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{s}-U^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{\bar{g}_{k}^{\prime}-}\in\mathbb{H}
(recall that
(U^{\varepsilon}_{s})_{s\geq 0}
converges locally uniformly to
(Z_{s})_{s\geq 0}
).

\bullet
We have
Z_{t}-Z_{g^{\prime}}\notin\bar{\mathbb{H}}
by (
108
), so that for

k
large enough,
U^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{t}-U^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{\bar{g}_{k}^{\prime}-}\notin\mathbb{H}
.

Step 5.2.
We have
\theta^{\prime}=\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}g_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\geq g^{\prime}
. Observe that
\theta^{\prime}<\infty
by Step 5.1 and
consider a (random) sequence
\varepsilon_{k}\to 0

such that
\lim_{k}g_{\varepsilon_{k}}^{\prime}=\theta^{\prime}
. By definition of
g^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}
, there is
s_{k}>0
such that

I^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{(g^{\prime}_{\varepsilon_{k}}+s_{k})-}=I^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{g^{\prime}_{\varepsilon_{k}}-}=U^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{1,g^{\prime}_{\varepsilon_{k}}-}
, see (
121
), and

|U^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{g^{\prime}_{\varepsilon_{k}}+s_{k}}-U^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{g^{\prime}_{\varepsilon_{k}}-}|>y
. Up to extraction,
we may assume
that
\lim_{k}s_{k}=s_{0}\in[0,\infty]
.

(i) It holds that
s_{0}<\infty
, else, we would have
I_{\infty}=I_{\theta^{\prime}}
, which does a.s. not occur.

(ii) Since
|U^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{g^{\prime}_{\varepsilon_{k}}+s_{k}}-U^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{g^{\prime}_{\varepsilon_{k}}-}|>y
for all
k
, we infer from Lemma
87
that either

|Z_{\theta^{\prime}+s_{0}}-Z_{\theta^{\prime}}|\geq y
or
|Z_{(\theta^{\prime}+s_{0})-}-Z_{\theta^{\prime}}|\geq y

or
|Z_{\theta^{\prime}+s_{0}}-Z_{\theta^{\prime}-}|\geq y
or
|Z_{(\theta^{\prime}+s_{0})-}-Z_{\theta^{\prime}-}|\geq y
.

(iii) Exactly as in Step 4.2, we have
\xi_{\theta^{\prime}+s_{0}}=\xi_{\theta^{\prime}}
.

(iv) We have
s_{0}>0
, because
else, (ii) would necessarily give us
Z_{\theta^{\prime}}=\lim_{k}U^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{g^{\prime}_{\varepsilon_{k}}+s_{k}}
,
Z_{\theta^{\prime}-}=\lim_{k}U^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{g^{\prime}_{\varepsilon_{k}}-}

and
|Z_{\theta^{\prime}}-Z_{\theta^{\prime}-}|\geq y
. Moreover, since
I^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{g^{\prime}_{\varepsilon_{k}}-}=U^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{1,g^{\prime}_{\varepsilon_{k}}-}
, we find

I_{\theta^{\prime}-}=Z_{1,\theta^{\prime}-}
. By Step 3-(g), this implies that
\Delta Z_{\theta^{\prime}}=0
,
which contradicts the fact that
|Z_{\theta^{\prime}}-Z_{\theta^{\prime}-}|\geq y
.

(v) Since
I^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{g^{\prime}_{\varepsilon_{k}}-}=U^{\varepsilon_{k}}_{1,g^{\prime}_{\varepsilon_{k}}-}
, Lemma
87
implies that either
I_{\theta^{\prime}}=Z_{1,\theta^{\prime}}

or
I_{\theta^{\prime}}=Z_{1,\theta^{\prime}-}
or
I_{\theta^{\prime}-}=Z_{1,\theta^{\prime}}
or
I_{\theta^{\prime}-}=Z_{1,\theta^{\prime}-}
. In any case, we find

I_{\theta^{\prime}}=Z_{1,\theta^{\prime}-}\land Z_{1,\theta^{\prime}}
. This, together with (iii), (iv) and
Step 3-(e), tells us that
u=\xi_{\theta^{\prime}}\in\mathrm{J}
and that
\theta^{\prime}=\gamma_{u-}
.
By Step 3-(a),
\theta^{\prime}
is a continuity point of
(Z_{t})_{t\geq 0}
, so that (ii) rewrites as

|Z_{\gamma_{u-}+s_{0}}-Z_{\gamma_{u-}}|\geq y
or
|Z_{(\gamma_{u-}+s_{0})-}-Z_{\gamma_{u-}}|\geq y
. Since

s_{0}\in(\gamma_{u-},\gamma_{u}]
by (iii), this implies that
M(e_{u})\geq y
, whence
M(e_{u})>y
by Step 3-(b). Thus

\sigma^{\prime}\leq u
, so that
g^{\prime}=\gamma_{\sigma^{\prime}-}\leq\gamma_{u-}=\theta^{\prime}
.

Step 6.
Since
(U^{\varepsilon}_{t})_{t\geq 0}\to(Z_{t})_{t\geq 0}
for the
{\bm{\mathrm{J}}_{1}}
-topology and since

g=\gamma_{\sigma-}
and
g^{\prime}=\gamma_{\sigma^{\prime}-}
are continuity points of
(Z_{t})_{t\geq 0}
by Step 3-(a), we
deduce from Steps 4 and 5 that almost surely,

\bm{w}_{\varepsilon}=(U^{\varepsilon}_{g_{\varepsilon}+t}-U^{\varepsilon}_{g_{\varepsilon}-})_{t\geq 0}
goes to
\bm{w}=(Z_{g+t}-Z_{g})_{t\geq 0}
and

\bm{w}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}=(U^{\varepsilon}_{g^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}+t}-U^{\varepsilon}_{g^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}-})_{t\geq 0}
goes to
\bm{w}^{\prime}=(Z_{g^{\prime}+t}-Z_{g^{\prime}})_{t\geq 0}

for the
{\bm{\mathrm{J}}_{1}}
-topology.

Step 7.
Here we check that a.s.,

\bm{v}_{\varepsilon}=(U^{\varepsilon}_{(g_{\varepsilon}+t)\land d_{\varepsilon}}-U^{\varepsilon}_{g_{\varepsilon}-})_{t\geq 0}
goes to
\bm{v}=(Z_{(g+t)\land d}-Z_{g})_{t\geq 0}

for the
{\bm{\mathrm{J}}_{1}}
-topology and
M(\bm{v}_{\varepsilon})
goes to
M(\bm{v})
.
By Steps 1 and 2 (and since
\mathfrak{n}_{*}(M=y)=0
), this will prove that

\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\cdot|\ell>\delta)\to\mathfrak{n}_{*}(\cdot|\ell>\delta)
and that

\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(M>y|\ell>\delta)\to\mathfrak{n}_{*}(M>y|\ell>\delta)
.

Recalling the notation of Step 6, we have
d_{\varepsilon}-g_{\varepsilon}=\ell(\bm{w}_{\varepsilon})=\ell(\bm{v}_{\varepsilon})
and

d-g=\ell(\bm{w})=\ell(\bm{v})
. We consider some (random) continuous increasing
\lambda_{\varepsilon}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}

such that
\lambda_{\varepsilon}(0)=0
,

\sup_{t\geq 0}|\lambda_{\varepsilon}(t)-t|\to 0
and
\sup_{[0,T]}|\bm{x}_{\varepsilon}(t)-\bm{w}(t)|\to 0
for all
T>0
, where

\bm{x}_{\varepsilon}(t)=\bm{w}_{\varepsilon}(\lambda_{\varepsilon}(t))
. Observe that
\ell(\bm{w}_{\varepsilon})=\lambda_{\varepsilon}(\ell(\bm{x}_{\varepsilon}))

and
M(\bm{v}_{\varepsilon})=M(\bm{w}_{\varepsilon})=M(\bm{x}_{\varepsilon})
.

Almost surely, for all
\varepsilon
small enough,
\ell(\bm{x}_{\varepsilon})=\ell(\bm{w})
:
this follows from the facts that

\bullet
we have
\bm{w}_{\varepsilon}(t)\in\mathbb{H}
for all
t\in[0,\delta]
because

\ell(\bm{w}_{\varepsilon})>\delta
by definition of
d_{\varepsilon}
, so that
for
\varepsilon
small enough,
\bm{x}_{\varepsilon}(t)\in\mathbb{H}
for all
t\in[0,\delta/2]
;

\bullet
by (
108
),

\inf_{[\delta/2,\ell(\bm{w}))}d(\bm{w}(t),\mathbb{H}^{c})>0
, so that for
\varepsilon
small enough,
\bm{x}_{\varepsilon}(t)\in\mathbb{H}

for
t\in[\delta/2,\ell(\bm{w}))
;

\bullet
by (
108
),
\bm{w}(\ell(\bm{w}))\notin\bar{\mathbb{H}}
,
so that for
\varepsilon
small enough,
\bm{x}_{\varepsilon}(\ell(\bm{w}))\notin\bar{\mathbb{H}}
.

Consequently, since
M(\bm{v}_{\varepsilon})=M(\bm{x}_{\varepsilon})
, for all
\varepsilon
small enough such that

\ell(\bm{x}_{\varepsilon})=\ell(\bm{w})
,

|M(\bm{v}_{\varepsilon})-M(\bm{w})|=\Big|\sup_{t\in[0,\ell(\bm{w})]}|\bm{x}_{\varepsilon}(t)|-\sup_{t\in[0,\ell(\bm{w})]}|\bm{w}(t)|\Big|\leq\sup_{t\in[0,\ell(\bm{w})]}|\bm{x}_{\varepsilon}(t)-\bm{w}(t)|\to 0.

Moreover,
\bm{v}_{\varepsilon}=(\bm{w}_{\varepsilon}(t\land\ell(\bm{w}_{\varepsilon}))_{t\geq 0}
goes to
\bm{v}=(\bm{w}(t\land\ell(\bm{w})))_{t\geq 0}

for the
{\bm{\mathrm{J}}_{1}}
-topology, because
\sup_{t\geq 0}|\lambda_{\varepsilon}(t)-t|\to 0
and, still for all
\varepsilon
small enough
so that
\ell(\bm{x}_{\varepsilon})=\ell(\bm{w})
,

\sup_{t\in[0,\infty)}|\bm{v}_{\varepsilon}(\lambda_{\varepsilon}(t))-\bm{v}(t)|=\sup_{t\in[0,\infty)}|\bm{x}_{\varepsilon}(t\land\ell(\bm{x}_{\varepsilon}))-\bm{w}(t\land\ell(\bm{w}))|=\sup_{t\in[0,\ell(\bm{w})]}|\bm{x}_{\varepsilon}(t)-\bm{w}(t)|\to 0.

Step 8.
Finally, we verify that for
\bm{v}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}=(U^{\varepsilon}_{(g_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}+t)\land d_{\varepsilon}}-U^{\varepsilon}_{g_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}-})_{t\geq 0}

and
\bm{v}^{\prime}=(Z_{(g^{\prime}+t)\land d}-Z_{g^{\prime}})_{t\geq 0}
, we have
\ell(\bm{v}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime})\to\ell(\bm{v})
a.s.
By Steps 1 and 2 (and since
\mathfrak{n}_{*}(\ell=\delta)=0
), this will prove that

\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\ell>\delta|M>y)\to\mathfrak{n}_{*}(\ell>\delta|M>y)
.

Recalling the notation of Step 6, we have
\ell(\bm{v}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime})=\ell(\bm{w}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime})
and
\ell(\bm{v}^{\prime})=\ell(\bm{w}^{\prime})
.
Let
\lambda_{\varepsilon}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
be continuous, increasing, with

\lambda_{\varepsilon}(0)=0
,

\sup_{t\geq 0}|\lambda_{\varepsilon}(t)-t|\to 0
and
\sup_{[0,T]}|\bm{x}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(t)-\bm{w}^{\prime}(t)|\to 0
for all
T>0
, where

\bm{x}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(t)=\bm{w}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(\lambda_{\varepsilon}(t))
. Observe that
\ell(\bm{w}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime})=\lambda_{\varepsilon}(\ell(\bm{x}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}))
.
To complete the proof, it suffices to check that a.s., for all
\varepsilon
small enough,
\ell(\bm{x}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime})=\ell(\bm{w}^{\prime})
.
This can be checked as Step 7,
provided
\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\ell(\bm{x}^{\prime}_{\varepsilon})>0
a.s. But on
\{\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\ell(\bm{x}^{\prime}_{\varepsilon})=0\}
, there is

\varepsilon_{n}\to 0

such that
\ell(\bm{x}^{\prime}_{\varepsilon_{n}})\to 0
, whence
M(\bm{x}^{\prime}_{\varepsilon_{n}})\to 0
(since
\bm{x}^{\prime}_{\varepsilon_{n}}
converges locally
uniformly to
\bm{w}
which is càd and since
\bm{w}(0)=0
).
This is not possible, because
M(\bm{x}^{\prime}_{\varepsilon})=M(\bm{w}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime})>y
a.s. by definition.
∎

We continue to study the convergence of
\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}
to
\mathfrak{n}_{*}
and prove some uniform
estimates on
\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}
.

Lemma 74
.

Grant Assumptions
18
and
22
-(a).

(i) There is a constant
C>0
such that
\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(M>y)\leq Cy^{-\alpha/2}
for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
, all
y>0
.

(ii) For all
\delta>0
, all
y>0
, as
\varepsilon\to 0
,

\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(M>y,\ell\leq\delta)\to\mathfrak{n}_{*}(M>y,\ell\leq\delta).

Proof.

For (i), it suffices that
C:=\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1]}\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(M>1)<\infty
. Indeed, by (
103
)
and since
M(\Phi_{\varepsilon}(e))=\varepsilon^{1/\alpha}M(e)
,
this will imply that
\mathfrak{n}^{1}(M>\varepsilon^{-1/\alpha})=\varepsilon^{1/2}\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(M>1)\leq C\varepsilon^{1/2}
for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
,
and thus that

\mathfrak{n}^{1}(M>z)\leq Cz^{-\alpha/2}
for all
z>1
. This also holds true when
z\in(0,1)
, since
\mathfrak{n}^{1}

is a finite measure. By (
103
) again, we will find that for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
, all
y>0
,

\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(M>y)=\varepsilon^{-1/2}\mathfrak{n}^{1}(M>y\varepsilon^{-1/\alpha})\leq Cy^{-\alpha/2}.

To prove that
\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1]}\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(M>1)<\infty
, we use Lemmas
71
and
73
, writing

\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(M>1)=\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\ell>1)\frac{\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(M>1|\ell>1)}{\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\ell>1|M>1)}\longrightarrow\mathfrak{n}_{*}(\ell>1)\frac{\mathfrak{n}_{*}(M>1|\ell>1)}{\mathfrak{n}_{*}(\ell>1|M>1)}\quad\text{as $\varepsilon\to 0$.}

Hence,
\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(M>1)
goes to some finite constant as
\varepsilon\to 0
.
Since finally
\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(M>1)\leq\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\mathcal{E})=\chi_{\mathrm{G}}\varepsilon^{-1/2}
for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
, we conclude that

\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1]}\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(M>1)<\infty
.

We next prove (ii). Writing

\displaystyle\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(M>y,\ell\leq\delta)=

\displaystyle\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(M>y)-\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(M>y,\ell>\delta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\ell>\delta)\frac{\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(M>y|\ell>\delta)}{\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\ell>\delta)|M>y)}-\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\ell>\delta)\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(M>y|\ell>\delta),

it suffices to use Lemmas
71
and
73
.
∎

We now check (
112
), which is a consequence of the previous lemma.

Proof of (
112
).

By Lemma
74
-(i) with
\varepsilon=1
, we know that for
(U_{t})_{t\geq 0}
a continuous-time random walk
with incremental law
\mathrm{F}_{1}
, jumping rate
1
and initial condition
U_{0}\sim\mathrm{G}_{1}
with a moment
of order
\alpha/2
,
there is a constant
C>0
such that for all
y>0
,
\mathbb{P}(M(U)>y)\leq Cy^{-\alpha/2}
.
Consequently, for
(\mathbf{S}_{n})_{n\geq 0}
a random walk with incremental law
\mathrm{F}_{1}
issued from
0
,
independent of some
U_{0}\sim\mathrm{G}_{1}
,
\mathbb{P}(\check{M}(U_{0}+\mathbf{S})>y)\leq Cy^{-\alpha/2}
.
But we clearly have
\check{\ell}(\mathbf{S})\leq\check{\ell}(U_{0}+\mathbf{S})
(since
U_{0}\in\mathbb{H}
),
so that
\check{M}(\mathbf{S})\leq\check{M}(U_{0}+\mathbf{S})+|U_{0}|
. Thus

\mathbb{P}(\check{M}(\mathbf{S}))>y)\leq\mathbb{P}(\check{M}(U_{0}+\mathbf{S})>y/2)+\mathbb{P}(|U_{0}|>y/2)\leq Cy^{-\alpha/2}.

∎

We can finally give the

Proof of Proposition
59
under Assumption
22
-(a).

First, (
84
) directly follows from the fact that
\mathfrak{n}_{*}(\ell>\delta)+\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1]}\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\ell>\delta)<\infty

by Lemmas
27
and
71
.

The estimate (
85
) (with
\theta\in(0,1/2)
) follows from the facts that

\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\ell>\delta)\leq C\delta^{-1/2}
and

\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(M>y)<Cy^{-\alpha/2}
, see Lemmas
71
and
74
-(i).

Concerning (
86
), we first write, for
\delta\in(0,1)
,

\int_{\mathcal{E}}(\ell(e)\wedge 1)(\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}-\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon,\delta})(\mathrm{d}e)=\int_{\mathcal{E}}(\ell(e)\wedge 1)\bm{1}_{\{\ell(e)\leq\delta\}}\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{d}e)\leq\int_{0}^{\delta}\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\ell>u)\mathrm{d}u.

By Lemma
71
, this is smaller than
C\int_{0}^{\delta}u^{-1/2}\mathrm{d}u\leq C\delta^{1/2}
, from which we conclude
that
\lim_{\delta\to 0}\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1]}\int_{\mathcal{E}}(\ell(e)\wedge 1)(\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}-\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon,\delta})(\mathrm{d}e)=0
.
Next,

\int_{\mathcal{E}}(M(e)\wedge 1)(\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}-\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon,\delta})(\mathrm{d}e)=\int_{\mathcal{E}}(M(e)\wedge 1)\bm{1}_{\{\ell(e)\leq\delta\}}\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{d}e)=\int_{0}^{1}\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(M>y,\ell\leq\delta)\mathrm{d}y.

We have
\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(M(e)>y,\ell(e)\leq\delta)\to\mathfrak{n}_{*}(M(e)>y,\ell(e)\leq\delta)
for each
y>0

by Lemma
74
-(ii), and
\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1)}\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(M(e)>y,\ell(e)\leq\delta)\leq C_{*}y^{-\alpha/2}

by Lemma
74
-(i). By dominated convergence,

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\mathcal{E}}(M(e)\wedge 1)(\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}-\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon,\delta})(\mathrm{d}e)=\int_{0}^{1}\mathfrak{n}_{*}(M(e)>y,\ell(e)\leq\delta)\mathrm{d}y=\int_{\mathcal{E}}(M(e)\wedge 1)\bm{1}_{\{\ell(e)\leq\delta\}}\mathfrak{n}_{*}(\mathrm{d}e).

This last quantity tends to
0
as
\delta\to 0
by (
9
) and since
\mathfrak{n}_{*}(\ell=0)=0
.

We now check (
87
). We fix
\delta>0
and
\phi:\mathcal{E}\to\mathbb{R}
bounded and continuous for the
{\bm{\mathrm{J}}_{1}}
-topology.
By Lemma
73
,
\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\phi|\ell>\delta)\to\mathfrak{n}_{*}(\phi|\ell>\delta)
. Since

\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon,\delta}(\mathrm{d}e)={\bm{1}}_{\{\ell(e)>\delta\}}\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{d}e)
and

\mathfrak{n}_{*}^{\delta}(\mathrm{d}e)={\bm{1}}_{\{\ell(e)>\delta\}}\mathfrak{n}_{*}(\mathrm{d}e)
, this rewrites as

\frac{\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon,\delta}(\phi)}{\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\ell>\delta)}\to\frac{\mathfrak{n}_{*}^{\delta}(\phi)}{\mathfrak{n}_{*}(\ell>\delta)}.

Recalling that
\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\ell>\delta)\to\mathfrak{n}_{*}(\ell>\delta)

by Lemma
71
, we find
\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon,\delta}(\phi)\to\mathfrak{n}_{*}^{\delta}(\phi)
as desired.

We next verify (
88
), which resembles Lemma
32
. We fix
\delta>0
and
\eta\in(0,\delta/2)

and write

\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon,\delta}(\ell_{r}<\eta)=\chi_{\mathrm{G}}\varepsilon^{-1/2}\mathbb{P}(\ell_{r}(Y^{\varepsilon})<\eta,\ell(Y^{\varepsilon})>\delta),

where we recall that
Y^{\varepsilon}_{t}=O_{\varepsilon}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}u)
, see Subsection
10.1
.
Applying the strong Markov property at time
\ell_{r}(Y^{\varepsilon})
, we get

\displaystyle\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon,\delta}(\ell_{r}<\eta)=

\displaystyle\chi_{\mathrm{G}}\varepsilon^{-1/2}\mathbb{E}\Big[{\bm{1}}_{\{\ell_{r}(Y^{\varepsilon})<\eta\land\ell(Y^{\varepsilon})\}}g_{\varepsilon}(Y^{\varepsilon}_{\ell_{r}(Y^{\varepsilon})},\delta-\ell_{r}(Y^{\varepsilon}))\Big]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\chi_{\mathrm{G}}\varepsilon^{-1/2}\mathbb{E}\Big[{\bm{1}}_{\{\ell_{r}(Y^{\varepsilon})<\eta\land\ell(Y^{\varepsilon})\}}g_{\varepsilon}(Y^{\varepsilon}_{\ell_{r}(Y^{\varepsilon})},\delta/2)\Big],

where
g_{\varepsilon}(x,t)=\mathbb{P}(\ell(Y^{\varepsilon,x})>t)
, with
(Y^{\varepsilon,x}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
defined in Subsection
10.1
.
By (
113
), we find

g_{\varepsilon}(x,\delta/2)\leq\Big(C\delta^{-1/2}(\varepsilon^{1/2}+x_{1}^{\alpha/2})\Big)\land 1\leq C_{\delta}(\varepsilon^{1/2}+x_{1}^{\alpha/2}\land 1),

for some constant
C_{\delta}>0
depending on
\delta
.
Consequently,
\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon,\delta}(\ell_{r}<\eta)\leq C_{\delta}(\chi_{\mathrm{G}}I_{\eta,\varepsilon}+J_{\eta,\varepsilon})
, where

I_{\eta,\varepsilon}=\mathbb{P}(\ell_{r}(Y^{\varepsilon})<\eta\land\ell(Y^{\varepsilon}))\leq\mathbb{P}(\ell_{r}(Y^{\varepsilon})<\ell(Y^{\varepsilon}))=:I_{\varepsilon}

and

\displaystyle J_{\eta,\varepsilon}=

\displaystyle\chi_{\mathrm{G}}\varepsilon^{-1/2}\mathbb{E}\Big[{\bm{1}}_{\{\ell_{r}(Y^{\varepsilon})<\eta\land\ell(Y^{\varepsilon})\}}\Big((Y^{\varepsilon}_{\ell_{r}(Y^{\varepsilon})}\cdot\mathbf{e}_{1})^{\alpha/2}\land 1\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{E}}{\bm{1}}_{\{\ell_{r}(e)<\eta\land\ell(e)\}}[(e(\ell_{r}(e))\cdot\mathbf{e}_{1})^{\alpha/2}\land 1]\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{d}e).

It remains to show that
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}I_{\varepsilon}=0

and
\lim_{\eta\to 0}\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}J_{\eta,\varepsilon}=0
.

Recall that
(Y^{\varepsilon}_{t})_{t\geq 0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{=}}(\varepsilon^{1/\alpha}Y^{1}_{t/\varepsilon})
, see Subsection
10.1
, so that

(\ell_{r}(Y^{\varepsilon}),\ell(Y^{\varepsilon}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{=}}(\varepsilon\ell_{\varepsilon^{-1/\alpha}r}(Y^{1}),\varepsilon\ell(Y^{1})).

Thus
I_{\varepsilon}=\mathbb{P}(\ell_{\varepsilon^{-1/\alpha}r}(Y^{1})<\ell(Y^{1}))
, which tends to
0
as
\varepsilon\to 0

by the monotone convergence theorem,
since
\ell_{\varepsilon^{-1/\alpha}r}(Y^{1})
a.s. increases as
\varepsilon
decreases and since a.s.,
\ell_{\varepsilon^{-1/\alpha}r}(Y^{1})=\ell(Y^{1})
for all
\varepsilon
small enough. Indeed,
since
(Y^{1}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is a (continuous-time) random walk of which the incremental law
\mathrm{F}_{1}
has a density,
we a.s. have
\min_{t\in[0,\ell(Y^{1}))}d(Y_{t}^{1},\mathbb{H}^{c})>0
, so that
Y^{1}_{t}\in B_{d}(\varepsilon^{-1/\alpha}r\mathbf{e}_{1},\varepsilon^{-1/\alpha}r)

for all
t\in[0,\ell(Y^{1}))
if
\varepsilon
is small enough.

Next, recalling that
B_{d}(r\mathbf{e}_{1},r)=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:|x|^{2}<2rx_{1}\}
, when
\ell_{r}(e)<\ell(e)
, we have

0\leq e(\ell_{r}(e))\cdot\mathbf{e}_{1}\leq|e(\ell_{r}(e))|^{2}/(2r)
, so that, for some constant
C_{r}>0

depending on
r
,

J_{\eta,\varepsilon}\leq C_{r}\int_{\mathcal{E}}\Big(\sup_{t\in[0,\eta\land\ell(e)]}|e(t)|^{\alpha}\land 1\Big)\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{d}e)=C_{r}\int_{0}^{1}\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}\Big(\sup_{t\in[0,\eta\land\ell(e)]}|e(t)|>y^{1/\alpha}\Big)\mathrm{d}y.

Assume for a moment that for each
\eta>0
, for a.e.
y\in(0,1)
,

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}\Big(\sup_{t\in[0,\eta\land\ell(e)]}|e(t)|>y^{1/\alpha}\Big)=\mathfrak{n}_{*}\Big(\sup_{t\in[0,\eta\land\ell(e)]}|e(t)|>y^{1/\alpha}\Big).

(122)

Then by dominated convergence, since
\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\sup_{t\in[0,\eta\land\ell(e)]}|e(t)|>y^{1/\alpha})\leq\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(M>y^{1/\alpha})\leq Cy^{-1/2}
by Lemma
74
-(i), we find that

\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}J_{\eta,\varepsilon}\leq C_{r}\int_{0}^{1}\mathfrak{n}_{*}\Big(\sup_{t\in[0,\eta\land\ell(e)]}|e(t)|>y^{1/\alpha}\Big)\mathrm{d}y=C_{r}\int_{\mathcal{E}}\Big(\sup_{t\in[0,\eta\land\ell(e)]}|e(t)|^{\alpha}\land 1\Big)\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{d}e)

This last quantity tends to
0
as
\eta\to 0
, as shown at the end of the proof of Lemma
32
.

To complete the proof of (
88
), it remains to show (
122
). We write

\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}\Big(\sup_{s\in[0,\eta\wedge\ell(e)]}|e(s)|>y^{1/\alpha}\Big)=\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon,\eta}\Big(\sup_{s\in[0,\eta]}|e(s)|>y^{1/\alpha}\Big)+\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(M(e)>y^{1/\alpha},\ell(e)\leq\eta).

Thanks to Lemma
74
-(ii), the second term converges to
\mathfrak{n}_{*}(M(e)>y^{1/\alpha},\ell(e)\leq\eta)

as
\varepsilon\to 0
. Regarding the first term, it follows from (
87
) that

\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon,\eta}
converges weakly to
\mathfrak{n}_{*}^{\eta}
. Since
e\mapsto\sup_{[0,\eta]}|e(s)|
is continuous
at each
e
such that
\Delta e(\eta)=0
, which holds true for
\mathfrak{n}_{*}
-a.e.
e\in\mathcal{E}
,
we get that the law of
\sup_{s\in[0,\eta]}|e(s)|
under
\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon,\eta}
converges to the law of

\sup_{s\in[0,\eta]}|e(s)|
under
\mathfrak{n}_{*}^{\eta}
. Hence, for a.e.
y\in(0,1)
,

\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon,\eta}(\sup_{s\in[0,\eta]}|e(s)|>y^{1/\alpha})\to\mathfrak{n}_{*}^{\eta}(\sup_{s\in[0,\eta]}|e(s)|>y^{1/\alpha})
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

Finally, we prove (
89
). Fix
r>0
and write, for
\delta>\eta>0
(recall that
\ell>\ell_{r}
),

\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\ell_{r}>\eta)\geq\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\ell_{r}>\eta,\ell>\delta)=\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\ell>\delta)-\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon,\delta}(\ell_{r}\leq\eta).

Using Lemma
71
and (
88
), we find

\liminf_{\eta\to 0}\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\ell_{r}>\eta)\geq\delta^{-1/2}\mathfrak{n}_{*}(\ell>1).

Letting
\delta\to 0
, we conclude that
\liminf_{\eta\to 0}\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}(\ell_{r}>\eta)=\infty
as desired.
∎

10.5
A technical lemma for the scattering process

It remains to show Lemma
56
. We first show an intermediate result.

Lemma 75
.

Grant Assumption
18
and Assumption
22
and fix
r>0
.
Let
U
be a
\mathrm{G}_{+}
-distributed random variable and let
\mathrm{P}_{\varepsilon}
be a
Poisson measure on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
with intensity
\varepsilon^{-1}\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{F}(v)\mathrm{d}v
.
We introduce
V_{t}^{\varepsilon}=U+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(v-V_{s-}^{\varepsilon})\mathrm{P}_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}v)
and

X_{t}^{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon^{1/\alpha-1}\int_{0}^{t}V_{s}\mathrm{d}s
, as well as the stopping time

\ell_{r}(X^{\varepsilon})=\inf\{t>0:X_{t}^{\varepsilon}\notin B_{d}(r\mathbf{e}_{1},r)\}
.
There exists
\zeta\in(0,1/2]
and
a_{0}>0
such that

\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{-\zeta}\mathbb{P}(\ell_{r}(X^{\varepsilon})>a_{0})>0.

Proof.

We write
\mathrm{P}_{\varepsilon}=\sum_{n\geq 1}\delta_{(T_{n}^{\varepsilon},W_{n})}
, where

(W_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is i.i.d. and
\mathrm{F}
-distributed,
(E_{n}^{\varepsilon})_{n\geq 1}
is i.i.d.
and
\mathrm{Exp}(\varepsilon^{-1})
-distributed and
T_{n}^{\varepsilon}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}E_{k}^{\varepsilon}
. We have

X_{T_{1}^{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon^{1/\alpha-1}E_{1}^{\varepsilon}U\quad\text{and for }n\geq 2,\quad X_{T_{n}^{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon^{1/\alpha-1}E_{1}^{\varepsilon}U+\varepsilon^{1/\alpha-1}\sum_{k=2}^{n}E_{k}^{\varepsilon}W_{k-1}.

We introduce the Poisson process
M^{\varepsilon}_{t}=\sum_{n\geq 1}{\bm{1}}_{\{T^{\varepsilon}_{n}\leq t\}}
with parameter
\varepsilon^{-1}
.
Since
(X^{\varepsilon}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is linear on
[T_{n}^{\varepsilon},T_{n+1}^{\varepsilon})
,
n\geq 0
(with
T_{0}^{\varepsilon}=0
),
and since
B_{d}(r\mathbf{e}_{1},r)
is convex, we have, for all
a>0
,

\{\ell_{r}(X^{\varepsilon})>a\}\supset\{X^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n}^{\varepsilon}}\in B_{d}(r\mathbf{e}_{1},r)\text{ for all }n=1,\dots,M^{\varepsilon}_{a}+1\}.

(123)

We next consider another (independent) sequence
(F_{n}^{\varepsilon})_{n\geq 1}
of
\mathrm{Exp}(\varepsilon^{-1})
-distributed i.i.d.
random variables and we let
S_{n}^{\varepsilon}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}F_{k}^{\varepsilon}
. Then
\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}=\sum_{n\geq 1}\delta_{(S_{n}^{\varepsilon},\varepsilon^{1/\alpha-1}E_{n+1}^{\varepsilon}W_{n})}
is a Poisson measure on
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}

with intensity
\varepsilon^{-1}\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{F}_{\varepsilon}(z)\mathrm{d}z
, with
\mathrm{F}_{\varepsilon}
defined in Notation
52
. Thus for

Y_{t}^{\varepsilon}=\varepsilon^{1/\alpha}E_{1}^{\varepsilon}U+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}z\mathrm{K}_{\varepsilon}(\mathrm{d}s,\mathrm{d}z)\quad\text{and}\quad\ell(Y^{\varepsilon})=\inf\{t>0:Y^{\varepsilon}_{t}\notin\mathbb{H}\},

the process
(Y^{\varepsilon}_{t\land\ell(Y^{\varepsilon})})_{t\geq 0}
is
\chi_{\mathrm{G}}^{-1}\varepsilon^{\zeta}\mathfrak{n}^{\varepsilon}
-distributed, with

\zeta=1/2
under Assumption
22
-(a) and
\zeta=\beta/\alpha\in(0,1/2)

under Assumption
22
-(b), see (
102
).
We thus deduce from (
89
) that
\lim_{a\to 0}\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{-\zeta}\mathbb{P}(\ell_{r}(Y^{\varepsilon})>a)=\infty
, so that
for some
a_{0}>0
,

p:=\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{-\zeta}\mathbb{P}(\ell_{r}(Y^{\varepsilon})>3a_{0})>0.

We now introduce the Poisson process
O^{\varepsilon}_{t}=\sum_{n\geq 1}{\bm{1}}_{\{S^{\varepsilon}_{n}\leq t\}}
with parameter
\varepsilon^{-1}
and
observe that
Y^{\varepsilon}_{S_{n}^{\varepsilon}}=X^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n+1}^{\varepsilon}}
for all
n\geq 0
, whence

\displaystyle\{\ell_{r}(Y^{\varepsilon})>3a_{0}\}=

\displaystyle\{Y^{\varepsilon}_{S_{n}^{\varepsilon}}\in B_{d}(r\mathbf{e}_{1},r)\text{ for all }n=0,\dots,O^{\varepsilon}_{3a_{0}}\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{X^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n}^{\varepsilon}}\in B_{d}(r\mathbf{e}_{1},r)\text{ for all }n=1,\dots,O^{\varepsilon}_{3a_{0}}+1\}.

Recalling (
123
), we see that

\mathbb{P}(\ell_{r}(X^{\varepsilon})>a_{0})\geq\mathbb{P}(\ell_{r}(Y^{\varepsilon})>3a_{0})-\mathbb{P}(O^{\varepsilon}_{3a_{0}}<M^{\varepsilon}_{a_{0}}).

Hence
\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{-\zeta}\mathbb{P}(\ell_{r}(X^{\varepsilon})>a_{0})\geq p-\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\varepsilon^{-\zeta}\mathbb{P}(O^{\varepsilon}_{3a_{0}}<M^{\varepsilon}_{a_{0}})=p
, because

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(O^{\varepsilon}_{3a_{0}}<M^{\varepsilon}_{a_{0}})=\mathbb{P}(e^{M^{\varepsilon}_{a_{0}}-O^{\varepsilon}_{3a_{0}}}>1)\leq\mathbb{E}[e^{M^{\varepsilon}_{a_{0}}-O^{\varepsilon}_{3a_{0}}}]=\exp\Big(\frac{a_{0}}{\varepsilon}[e-1+3(e^{-1}-1)]\Big)

and because
e-1+3(e^{-1}-1)<0
. We used that
(M^{\varepsilon}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
and
(O^{\varepsilon}_{t})_{t\geq 0}

are independent Poisson processes with parameter
\varepsilon^{-1}
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
56
.

We let to the reader the care to recall Definition
19
. We only recall here that
\lambda(x,v,s)

is defined by (
22
) and that
T_{1}^{\varepsilon}=\lambda(\bm{X}_{0}^{\varepsilon},\varepsilon^{(1-\alpha)/\alpha}\bm{V}_{0}^{\varepsilon},E^{\varepsilon}_{1})
and that
for any
n\geq 1
,
T_{n+1}^{\varepsilon}=T_{n}^{\varepsilon}+\lambda(\bm{X}^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n}^{\varepsilon}},\varepsilon^{(1-\alpha)/\alpha}\bm{V}^{\varepsilon}_{T_{n}^{\varepsilon}},E^{\varepsilon}_{n+1})
.
Finally,
N_{t}^{\varepsilon}=\sum_{n\geq 1}{\bm{1}}_{\{T_{n}^{\varepsilon}\leq t\}}
.

Step 1.
It suffices that for all
t>0
,
\max_{n=1,\dots,\lfloor t/\varepsilon\rfloor}|T_{n}^{\varepsilon}-n\varepsilon|\to 0

in probability as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

Indeed, this implies that for all
t>0
,
\sup_{[0,t]}|\varepsilon N^{\varepsilon}_{s}-s|\to 0
in probability, because

\text{for all $\eta\in(0,t)$, all $\varepsilon\in(0,\eta/2)$,}\quad\Big\{\sup_{[0,t]}|\varepsilon N^{\varepsilon}_{s}-s|>\eta\Big\}\subset\Big\{\max_{n=1,\dots,\lfloor 2t/\varepsilon\rfloor}|T_{n}^{\varepsilon}-n\varepsilon|>\eta/2\Big\}.

Indeed, if there is
s\in[0,t]
such that
\varepsilon N^{\varepsilon}_{s}-s>\eta
, then with
n=\lfloor\frac{s+\eta}{\varepsilon}\rfloor\in\{0,\dots,\lfloor\frac{2t}{\varepsilon}\rfloor\}
, we have
T_{n}^{\varepsilon}\leq s\leq(n+1)\varepsilon-\eta<n\varepsilon-\eta/2
,
while if there is
s\in[0,t]
such that
\varepsilon N^{\varepsilon}_{s}-s<-\eta
(which implies that
s>\eta
),
then with
n=\lfloor\frac{s-\eta}{\varepsilon}\rfloor\in\{0,\dots,\lfloor\frac{t}{\varepsilon}\rfloor\}
,
we have
T_{n}^{\varepsilon}\geq s\geq\varepsilon n+\eta>n\varepsilon+\eta/2
.

This also implies that
\max_{n=1,\dots,N^{\varepsilon}_{t}}|T_{n}^{\varepsilon}-n\varepsilon|\to 0
in probability, because for all
\eta>0
,

\mathbb{P}\Big(\max_{n=1,\dots,N^{\varepsilon}_{t}}|T_{n}^{\varepsilon}-n\varepsilon|>\eta\Big)\leq\mathbb{P}\Big(N^{\varepsilon}_{t}>\frac{2t}{\varepsilon}\Big)+\mathbb{P}\Big(\max_{n=1,\dots,\lfloor 2t/\varepsilon\rfloor}|T_{n}^{\varepsilon}-n\varepsilon|>\eta\Big).

By the above discussion, the first term tends to
0
, while the second one tends to
0
by assumption.

Step 2.
Since
\lambda(x,v,s)=s
when
x+vs\in\mathcal{D}
, it holds that
T^{\varepsilon}_{n+1}-T^{\varepsilon}_{n}=E^{\varepsilon}_{n+1}

when
\bm{X}^{\varepsilon}_{T^{\varepsilon}_{n+1}}\in\mathcal{D}
. Since moreover we always have
\lambda(x,v,s)\leq s
,

S^{\varepsilon}_{n}-R^{\varepsilon}_{n}\leq T^{\varepsilon}_{n}\leq S^{\varepsilon}_{n},\quad\text{where}\quad S^{\varepsilon}_{n}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}E^{\varepsilon}_{k}\quad\text{and}\quad R^{\varepsilon}_{n}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}E^{\varepsilon}_{k}{\bm{1}}_{\{\bm{X}^{\varepsilon}_{T^{\varepsilon}_{k}}\in\partial\mathcal{D}\}},

whence

\max_{n=1,\dots,\lfloor t/\varepsilon\rfloor}|T_{n}^{\varepsilon}-n\varepsilon|\leq\max_{n=1,\dots,\lfloor t/\varepsilon\rfloor}|S_{n}^{\varepsilon}-n\varepsilon|+R^{\varepsilon}_{\lfloor t/\varepsilon\rfloor}.

Since
S^{\varepsilon}_{n}-n\varepsilon
is a martingale, we deduce from Doob’s inequality that

\mathbb{E}\Big[\max_{n=1,\dots,\lfloor t/\varepsilon\rfloor}|S^{\varepsilon}_{n}-n\varepsilon|^{2}\Big]\leq 4\text{Var }S^{\varepsilon}_{\lfloor t/\varepsilon\rfloor}=4\lfloor t/\varepsilon\rfloor\text{Var }E^{\varepsilon}_{1}=4\lfloor t/\varepsilon\rfloor\varepsilon^{2}\to 0.

Step 3.

It only remains to show that
R^{\varepsilon}_{\lfloor t/\varepsilon\rfloor}\to 0
in probability as
\varepsilon\to 0
. We fix
t>0
and
\eta>0

and we consider

\zeta\in(0,1/2]
as Lemma
75
. We write

\mathbb{P}(R^{\varepsilon}_{\lfloor t/\varepsilon\rfloor}>\eta)\leq I_{\varepsilon}+J_{\varepsilon}
, where

I_{\varepsilon}=\mathbb{P}\Big(\max_{n=1,\dots,\lfloor t/\varepsilon\rfloor}E^{\varepsilon}_{n}\geq\varepsilon^{(\zeta+1)/2}\Big),\quad J_{\varepsilon}=\mathbb{P}(m^{\varepsilon}_{t}>\eta\varepsilon^{-(\zeta+1)/2})\quad\hbox{and}\quad m^{\varepsilon}_{t}=\sum_{k=1}^{\lfloor t/\varepsilon\rfloor}{\bm{1}}_{\{\bm{X}^{\varepsilon}_{T^{\varepsilon}_{k}}\in\partial\mathcal{D}\}}.

First,
I_{\varepsilon}=1-\mathbb{P}(E^{\varepsilon}_{1}<\varepsilon^{(\zeta+1)/2})^{\lfloor t/\varepsilon\rfloor}=1-(1-e^{-\varepsilon^{-(1-\zeta)/2}})^{\lfloor t/\varepsilon\rfloor}

tends to
0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.

Next, we let
\tau^{\varepsilon}_{1}=\inf\{T^{\varepsilon}_{k}:k\geq 1,\bm{X}^{\varepsilon}_{T^{\varepsilon}_{k}}\in\partial\mathcal{D}\}

and
\tau^{\varepsilon}_{n+1}=\inf\{T^{\varepsilon}_{k}:k\geq 1,T^{\varepsilon}_{k}>\tau^{\varepsilon}_{n},\bm{X}^{\varepsilon}_{T^{\varepsilon}_{k}}\in\partial\mathcal{D}\}
for
n\geq 1
.
Thanks to Remark
3
, there exists
r>0
such that
B_{d}(x+r\mathbf{n}_{x},r)\subset\mathcal{D}
for all
x\in\partial\mathcal{D}

so that, if we let

\gamma_{n+1}^{\varepsilon}=\inf\{t>0:\bm{X}_{\tau_{n}^{\varepsilon}+t}^{\varepsilon}\notin B_{d}(\bm{X}_{\tau_{n}^{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}+r\mathbf{n}_{\bm{X}_{\tau_{n}^{\varepsilon}}^{\varepsilon}},r)\}
,
then
\gamma_{n+1}^{\varepsilon}\leq\tau_{n+1}^{\varepsilon}-\tau_{n}^{\varepsilon}
for all
n\geq 1
. Thus

\tau^{\varepsilon}_{n}\geq\sum_{k=2}^{n}\gamma^{\varepsilon}_{k}
for all
n\geq 2
.

By the strong Markov property of

(\bm{X}^{\varepsilon}_{t},\bm{V}_{t}^{\varepsilon})_{t\geq 0}
and by the rotational invariance of
\mathrm{F}

and
\mathrm{G}
, the sequence
(\gamma_{n}^{\varepsilon})_{n\geq 2}
is i.i.d. and
\gamma_{2}^{\varepsilon}
has the same law
as
\ell_{r}(X^{\varepsilon})
in Lemma
75
.

All in all, setting
n_{\varepsilon}=\lfloor\eta\varepsilon^{-(\zeta+1)/2}\rfloor
,

J_{\varepsilon}=\mathbb{P}(\tau^{\varepsilon}_{n_{\varepsilon}}<t)\leq\mathbb{P}\Big(\sum_{k=2}^{n_{\varepsilon}}\gamma_{k}^{\varepsilon}<t\Big)\leq e^{t}\mathbb{E}\Big[\exp\Big(-\sum_{k=2}^{n_{\varepsilon}}\gamma_{k}^{\varepsilon}\Big)\Big]=e^{t}\big(\mathbb{E}[e^{-\ell_{r}(X^{\varepsilon})}]\big)^{n_{\varepsilon}-1}.

By Lemma
75
, there are
q>0
and
a_{0}>0

so that
\mathbb{P}(\ell_{r}(X^{\varepsilon})>a_{0})\geq q\varepsilon^{\zeta}
for all
\varepsilon\in(0,1]
small enough, implying that

\mathbb{E}[e^{-\ell_{r}(X^{\varepsilon})}]\leq\mathbb{E}[e^{-a_{0}{\bm{1}}_{\{\ell_{r}(X^{\varepsilon})>a_{0}\}}}]=1-(1-e^{-a_{0}})\mathbb{P}(\ell_{r}(X^{\varepsilon})>a_{0})\leq 1-c\varepsilon^{\zeta},

where
c=(1-e^{-a_{0}})q>0
. We thus find

J_{\varepsilon}\leq e^{t}(1-c\varepsilon^{\zeta})^{n_{\varepsilon}-1}
,
which tends to
0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.
∎

Appendix A
Geometric lemmas and inequalities

In this section, we check that the cutoff function
\Lambda
is continuous,
we build some regular families of isometries,
we establish some parameterization lemmas of constant use and we prove the geometric
inequalities stated in Proposition
39
.

A.1
Continuity of the cutoff function

Here we check the following result.

Lemma 76
.

Assume that
\mathcal{D}
is open, bounded and strictly convex. The function
\Lambda:{}\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0mu\mathcal{D}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to{}\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0mu\mathcal{D}}

defined in (
10
) is continuous.

Proof.

Let
V:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
be defined by
V(x)=d(x,{}\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0mu\mathcal{D}})
. The function
V
is continuous and convex.
For
(y,z)\in{}\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0mu\mathcal{D}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we define
v_{y,z}:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
by
v_{y,z}(t)=V(y+t(z-y))
. Observing that

v_{y,z}
is continuous and convex and that
v_{y,z}(0)=0
(because
y\in{}\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0mu\mathcal{D}}
), we may define

t_{y,z}=\max\{t\in[0,1]:v_{y,z}(t)=0\}
. It then holds that
\Lambda(y,z)=y+t_{y,z}(z-y)
.

We consider a sequence
(y_{k},z_{k})\in{}\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0mu\mathcal{D}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
converging to some
(y,z)\in{}\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0mu\mathcal{D}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
and we have to show that
\lim_{k}\Lambda(y_{k},z_{k})=\Lambda(y,z)
. Since the sequence
(\Lambda(y_{k},z_{k}))_{k\geq 1}

is valued in
{}\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0mu\mathcal{D}}
which is compact, it suffices to show that
\Lambda(y,z)
is its
only accumulation point. We thus may assume that
\lim_{k\to\infty}\Lambda(y_{k},z_{k})
exists.
Extracting a subsequence, we may moreover assume that
t_{y_{k},z_{k}}
converges to some
t\in[0,1]
.
Finally, using again subsequences, we may assume either (a) for all
k\geq 1
,
z_{k}\in{}\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0mu\mathcal{D}}
or
(b) for all
k\geq 1
,
z_{k}\notin{}\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0mu\mathcal{D}}
.

Case (a).
In this case, we have
z=\lim_{k}z_{k}\in{}\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0mu\mathcal{D}}
, whence
\Lambda(y_{k},z_{k})=z_{k}\to z=\Lambda(y,z)
.

Case (b).
We always have
t\leq t_{y,z}
. Indeed, this follows from the fact that
v_{y,z}(t)=0
,
because
v_{y,z}(t)=V(y+t(z-y))=\lim_{k}V(y_{k}+t_{y_{k},z_{k}}(z_{k}-y_{k}))=0
.

If first
t=t_{y,z}
, which means that
\lim_{k}t_{y_{k},z_{k}}=t_{y,z}
, then

\Lambda(y_{k},z_{k})=y_{k}+t_{y_{k},z_{k}}(z_{k}-y_{k})
tends to
y+t_{y,z}(z-y)=\Lambda(y,z)
as
k\to\infty
.

If next
t<t_{y,z}
, then
v_{y,z}(s)=0
(implying
y+s(z-y)\in{}\mkern 3.0mu\overline{\mkern-3.0mu\mathcal{D}}
)
for all
s\in(t,t_{y,z})
. Moreover,
y+s(z-y)=\lim_{k}(y_{k}+s(z_{k}-y_{k}))\in\mathcal{D}^{c}
for
all
s\in(t,t_{y,z})
, because
\mathcal{D}^{c}
is closed and because

y_{k}+s(z_{k}-y_{k})\in\mathcal{D}^{c}
for all
k
large enough so that
s>t_{y_{k},z_{k}}
.
Thus
\partial\mathcal{D}
contains the line segment
\{y+s(z-y):s\in(t,t_{y,z})\}
,
which is not possible by strict convexity of
\mathcal{D}
, except if
y=z
. In such a case,

\Lambda(y_{k},z_{k})=y_{k}+t_{y_{k},z_{k}}(z_{k}-y_{k})
tends to
y+t(z-y)=y=\Lambda(y,z)
.
∎

A.2
Some regular families of isometries

We recall that for
